# Tikamātikā


<a id="org7d91243"></a>

## Introduction


<a id="org28b6cfd"></a>

### mātikā

1.  mātikā &#x2013; meanings

    [
    
    -   "table of contents"
    -   "index"
    -   "like a mother that produces children; produces meanings"
    -   "like a canal that guides water; flows into meanings"
    
    ]  

2.  3 types (acc. to subject)

    [
    
    -   **vinaya** mātikā: 2 pātimokkhas
    -   **suttanta** mātikās: dyads: 84: taught to abhidhammika monks; how to understand suttas based on abhidhamma
        -   indriyasamvara indriya asamvara: indriyesu guttadvārattā&#x2026;etc.
        -   kalyāṇamittatā: 8 mahākusalacittas and 8 mahākiriyacittas when associating a good friend
        -   kalyāṇamitta: does good deeds 3 doors and restrained evil deeds; who has knowledge and practice and attainment in dhamma
    -   **abhidhamma** mātikā: duka and tika mātikās
    
    ]  

3.  2 types and #

    [
    
    -   **tika** mātikā (triads): 22 mātikās x 3 dhammas: 66
    -   **duka** mātikā (dyad): 100 mātikās x 2 dhammas: 200
    
    ]

4.  3 stages of study of mātikās

    [
    
    -   terminology
    -   anumeration of ultimate realities
    -   categorization into 4 groups: khandha, āyatana, dhātu, and ariyasacca
    
    ]  

5.  3 abhidheyyatthas: aspects to be studied for each term

    [
    
    -   **abhidhānattha** / **vacanattha**: how to define the term. characteristics of a term that can be shown with word formation are here.
        -   Padasādhana (etymology)
    -   **sabhāvattha**: the nature
    -   **dabbattha / sarūpattha**: ultimate representation of the term
    -   classification into sabbasaṅgaha groups
    -   notes:
        -   **abhidheyyattha** can have all 3 meanings
        -   some qualities appear in the etymology but not all meanings. Some are found in the sāsana.
        -   in some cases (vedanā), abhidhānattha and sabhāvattha are the same.
        -   dhamma: 4 definitions (3 abhidhānattha, 1 sabhāvattha [but all are actually sabhāvattha])
            -   the first 3 through word formation, and nissattanijjīvatā
        -   kusala: nature not expressed etymologically
            -   ārogyaṭṭha, anavajaṭṭha
                -   attha (from artha) > aṭṭha (influence from sanskrit r)
    
    ]    

6.  text related

    1.  2 methods for naming tikas
    
        -   **ādiladdha**: based on the first element &#x2013; like kusalattika
        -   **sabbaladdha**: based on an element common to all 3 &#x2013; like vedanāttika
    
    2.  buddhajayanti vs chaṭṭhasaṅgāyana
    
        -   upādiṇṇa and upādinna
        -   paṭhavi and pathavī


<a id="orgd601cd0"></a>

### dhamma

1.  dhamma &#x2013; meaning

    1.  3 morphological definitions (exam)
    
        -   **attano sabhāvaṁ dhārentīti dhammā**: natures that bear their own instrinsic properties are called dhamma
            -   only paramatthas have sabhāva
            -   the reality and its intrinsic properties are the same thing
        -   **paccayehi dhārīyantīti dhammā**: natures that are known with the help of their causes are called dhamma
            -   paccayas help a reality's arising, continuance through generations, and growth
            -   only a saṅkhataparamattha can get the support of paccayas
            -   here only applies to saṅkhata
            -   asaṅkhata is not known through paccayas
            -   saṅkhatadhammas are inseparable from their paccayas in the causal sense not spatial
            -   if the cause exists, the result exists
        -   **yathāsabhāvato dhārīyantīti dhammā**: natures that are known according to their intrinsic natures are called dhamma
        -   notes:
            -   √dhar: bear, know
                -   √dhar ma > dhamma
                    -   √dhar ramma > dhamma
    
    2.  Samānādhikaraṇa vs Bhinnādhikaraṇa
    
        -   **samānādhikaraṇa**: apposition; same case and refer to the same thing
            -   adhikaraṇa: place where they are located
                -   kusalā dhammā: both refer to the same thing
            -   purpose of adding "dhamma" at the end of the tikas: to show that these realities
                -   are not a self
                -   are connected to a self, not part of a self
                -   are not connected to a self
                    -   example: cetanā: 3 doctrines of self related to cetanā as an example
                        -   it itself is the self
                        -   different and inseparable (avinibbhoga): if one vanishes, the other vanishes
                        -   different and separable: if one vanishes, the other may still exist
        -   **bhinnādhikaraṇa**: non-apposition
    
    3.  Meanings of dhamma
    
        -   **pariyatti**: scriptures
            -   suttaṁ geyyaṁ veyyākaraṇaṁ…
        -   **hetu**: cause
            -   hetumhi ñāṇaṁ dhammapaṭisambhidhā (the knowledge about the cause is called dhammapaṭisambhidā)
        -   **guṇa**: wholesome quality
            -   na hi dhammo adhammo ca,
                ubho sama vipākino,
                adhammo nirayaṁ neti
                dhammo neti suggatiṁ (thra)
        -   **nissattanijjīvatā**: non-self, soulless
            -   jīva: exists for a long period without passing away (soul)
            -   dhammesu dhammānupassi viharati (DN)
        -   **paññatti**: can refer to concepts
            -   sabbe dhammā anattā, but comm.: only khandhas because it was for vipassanā
        -   **dhammatā**: natural laws
        -   in mātikā: non-self realities (except paññattis)
    
    4.  Extra notes
    
        -   **kiriyamayajavana**: exludes the phala: because ku aku and kiriya of arahant excluding the phalacitta
            -   when avijjā is present, mundane javanacittas surely are kiriyamayajavana
        -   **abyāpāra naya**:
            -   abyāpāra: adj. inactive; ineffective [na > a + vi + ā + √par + \*a] ~
            -   avijjā > saṅkhāra: avijjā doesn't have the intention of producing saṅkhāras: no effort from the part of avijjā
            -   whenever avijjā is present, the arising of saṅkhāras cannot be prevented, even if one does not wish for their arising, they arise
        -   dhamma is maybe the most explained word in our literature
            -   5 / 6 in dhammasaṅgaṇī but there are more depending on the context
            -   in mātikā: non-self realities, neither self nor connected with a self (nissattanijjīvatā)
            -   **nissatta**: not satta; related to kārakadiṭṭhi
                -   a person who exerts effort, does things
                -   etymologically: **sañjatīti satto** (one who is attached to existence is a being)
            -   **nijjīva**: that which exists throught saṁsāra or one life without being destroyed, like a permanent thing (upanishads); jīvadiṭṭhi
                -   jīv: to live
                -   existing without stopping
                -   this view is suppressed by udayabbayañāṇa when one sees dhammas perishing
        -   dhātu: shows non-selfness, here as well, same meaning here
        -   attha: meaning, reality, that which is known by the mind
            -   western philosophy: cetasikas called propositions (tbc)
            -   mental content: ārammaṇa (attha)
                -   sometimes differentiated between the actual object, so similar to cittanimitta
        -   if there was no consciousness, there would be no meaning (attha)
        -   sabbe dhammā anattāti yadā paññāya passati…
            -   dhamma includes Nibbāna but comm. here: 5 aggregates because it's instructions for vipassanā
            -   parivārapāḷi: Nibbāna is anattā
                -   wrong idea: nicca > dukkha > anattā, so if Nibbāna is not anicca, it is not anattā
                    -   anattā does not depend on anicca nature
                    -   sabhāva cannot change itself; so sabhāva is opposed to a self (unchangeable)
        -   bahiddhā tika does not include paññatti; bahiddhārammaṇa includes dhammas that take paññatti
            -   paññatti is bahiddhā but is not included in mātikā
            -   paññatti of nothingness (ākāsānañcāyatana) is neither bahiddhā nor ajjhatta: special case
    
    5.  analysis of dhamma (maybe repeated)
    
        Thereafter, the meanings of the term “dhamma” should be learned. Its verbal and intrinsic 
        meanings are as follows: 
        (1). Attano sabhāvaṃ dhārentīti dhammā – “Natures that bear their own intrinsic 
        properties are called dhamma”. 
        (2). Paccayehi dhārīyantī’ti dhammā – “Natures that are known with the help their causes 
        are called dhamma”. 
        (3). Yathāsabhāvato dhārīyantī’ti dhammā – “Natures that are known according to their 
        intrinsic natures are called dhamma”. 
        Finally, one should learn the dabbattha or ultimate realities referred to by the expression 
        ‘kusalā dhammā’. These are “21 kusala cittas and their associated 38 cetasikas.” 
        The brief classification of kusala dhammas under the aggregates and related categories is as 
        follows: 
        Kusala dhammas are categorized; 
        (1). In 4 khandhas 
        (2). In 2 āyatanas 
        (3). In 2 dhātus 
        (4). Some in 2 saccas and some are free from saccas. 

2.  a dhamma doesn't associate itself &#x2013; simile

    -   6 people travelling: each is accompanied by 5, not 6
    -   index finger cannot touch itself
    -   appanākosalla: how to make the mnd happier; recalling pīti and its associated dhammas
        -   in some occasions, when "connected with sth" is given, the reality itself is included, but not in abhidhamma

<a id="orgc0dfba6"></a>

### useful information for mātikā

1.  important points to remember

    -   **in general**
        -   mention "except appamañña" in many places
        -   exclude lobha from dukkhasacca
        -   add "when"-statement to anekanta ārammaṇa classifications
        -   saṅkiliṭṭattika = hīnattika
        -   remember to specify as lokiya
            -   17 LOK ku ct
            -   32 LOK vip ct
        -   remember rūpa and arūpa rāga
            -   4 di vip ct with RU and ARU RA
        -   mention missing cetasikas, especially virati and appamañña; as issā and macchariya in ajjhatārammaṇattika
    -   **vipākadhamma**:
        -   vipāka: all vipākacittas, no rūpa
        -   vipākadhamma: includes maggas
    -   **vedanāttika**: exclude vedanā
    -   **upādiṇṇattika**
        -   20 kmj rūpa in upādiṇṇupādāniya
        -   anupādiṇṇupādāniya:
            -   17 ctjr
            -   15 utjr
            -   14 āhjr
    -   **vitakkattika**
        -   vt and vc
            -   vt of the 1st pada goes to the 2nd pada
            -   vc
                -   of the 1st pada goes to muttaka
                -   of the 2nd pada goes to the 3rd pada
        -   rūpa and Nibbāna are avitakka avicāra dhammā
    -   **pītittika**
        -   pīti from 1st pada goes to 2nd pada
        -   63 sukhas from 2nd pada:
            -   51 are in the 1st pada
            -   12 are muttaka
                -   su in Kv and 11 4th jh ct
    -   **dassanattika**
        -   apāyagāmī / non-apāyagāmī "combo"
        -   gross / subtle kā rā and pṭgh
        -   rūpa and arūpa rāga
    -   **dassanenappahātabbahetukattika**
        -   except amoha in mmc (nevadas.)
        -   3rd pada is basically everything else, regardless of hetu
    -   **ācayattika**
        -   ācayagāmino: udhc mmc included
        -   apacayagāmino: 4 magga cittacetasika
    -   **sekkhattika**
        -   sekkhā: 1st 7 loku ctcsk
        -   asekkhā: arahattaphala
    -   **micchattattika**
        -   ānatariyakamma is separated from the niyatāmicchādiṭṭhis
        -   cetasikas are also divided
        -   remember "7th J"
        -   aniyatādhammā: 85 ct (-2 other pada)
            -   cittas follow citta chart order: 12 aku, 10 viñ, etc.
    -   **maggārammaṇattika**
        -   maggārammaṇā dhammā (take magga as obj)
            -   separate by ariyapuggala
            -   obj: same or lower magga
        -   maggahetukā dhammā (ass. hetus in magga)
            -   sampāpaka (8 mgg as one): 28 csk
            -   sampāpaka + mūla (amoha): 35 csk
            -   mūla (alobha, adosa and amoha): 36 csk
        -   maggādhipatino dhammā
            -   ārammaṇādhipati (respects magga as obj): only one's own highest magga
            -   sahajātādhipati (ass. / is mgg and adhipati)
                -   ass. mgg and adhipati
                -   is mgg and adhipati
    -   **uppannattika**
        -   anuppanna: no vpk cittacetasika or kmjr
            -   17 ctjr
            -   15 utjr
            -   14 āhjr
        -   uppādino: vpk cittacetasika and kmjr set to arise
        -   tikamuttaka: Nibbāna
        -   no āhrj / kmj sadda
        -   āyatana, dhātu:
            -   10 ctj/utj/āhrj sukhumarūpa
            -   9 kmj sukhumarūpa
    -   **atītārammaṇattika**
        -   mātikā: kkc takes 3 kāla and paññatti
            -   comm. only takes atīta objects
        -   vrt takes anāgata, paccuppanna, and Nibbāna
            -   no atīta or paññatti
    -   **ajjhattārammaṇattika**
        -   tikamuttaka: object is natthibhāvapaññatti (absence of internal past ākā)
        -   issā: only bahiddhārammaṇa
        -   macchariya: here, only ajjhattār.
        -   virati: no paññattārammaṇa
        -   appamañña: only bahiddhārammaṇa

4.  cetasikas &#x2013; principles

    -   **issā**: 
        -   cittas
            -   2 dmc (sometimes, separately from macchariya and kukkucca)
        -   object
            -   ekantabahiddhārammaṇa
            -   cannot arise with natthibhāvapaññatti as object
                -   (*:q: can it arise with other paññattis as object?*)
    -   **macchariya**
        -   cittas
            -   2 dmc (sometimes, separately from issā and kukkucca)
        -   object
            -   ekanta ajjhattārammaṇa
                -   ajjhatta here in the sense of being seen as mine, not of being part of one's own khandhas
            -   cannot arise with natthibhāvapaññatti as object
                -   (*:q: can it arise with other paññattis as object?*)
    -   **kukkucca**
        -   cittas
            -   2 dmc (sometimes, separately from issā/macchariya)
        -   object
            -   ekanta atītārammaṇa
    -   **virati**
        -   cittas
            -   in 8 mku (sometimes, separately)
            -   in 8 lokuttara (always, together)
        -   object
            -   ekanta paramatthārammaṇa
                -   kāmāvacarārammaṇa: ok
                    -   ctcsk, rūpas: ok (tbc)
                -   mahaggatārammaṇa: no
                -   lokuttara: 
                    -   cittacetasika: no
                    -   nibbāna: yes, when in lokuttaracittas, not mahakusalas
            -   can be ajjhattārammaṇa or bahiddhārammaṇa
                -   bahiddhārammaṇa: not paññatti
    -   **appamañña**
        -   cittas
            -   8 mku (sometimes, separately)
            -   15 rpv (sometimes, separately)
        -   ekanta sattapaññattārammaṇa (ekanta bahiddhārammaṇa)
    
    1.  Self-evident cetasika groups
    
        [
        
        -   52 (all)
        -   38 (except 14 akusala)
        -   27 (except sobhana)
        -   13 (aññasamāna)
        -   7 (sabbacittasādhāraṇa)
        -   note: other numbers require mentioning what is missing
        
        ]

<a id="org2114676"></a>

### Insights about the tikas as a whole

-   kusalatika: what should be done, what should be abandoned, what should be attained; assāda, adīnava, nissaraṇa
-   vedanāttika: most appealing reality (sukhavedanā); taṇhādutiyo puriso, vedanā is food for taṇhā; the bait
-   savitakka and pīti tikas: jhānaṅgas; jhānas in order (includes lok and loku)
    -   upakkilesa sutta (SN maybe): Buddha developed these jhānas to eradicate defilements; he mentions savitakka, etc (5 fold method); bodhisatta also developt eh 5 fold method, not only for those with less wisdom;
        -   comm: 2 jhānas (lok and loku); lok 5 fold; loku 4 fold; stpt 1st jh, skdg 2nd, ang 3rd, arahat mg 4th jh (4fold method)
            -   arupa jhaānas not explciitly mentioned under bodhi tree
    -   ratanasutta: only concentartion in magga is elaborated  samādhina tena samo na vijjati
    -   jhānanāgāmi
    -   classifications of samādhi: savit, avit vic, avit avic, sappī, nippī, sukha, no sukha, upekkhā
-   dassanattika: jhānaṅgas lead to the eradication of defilements
-   dassa hetuka: shows strength of defilements to be eradicated
-   vipākadhamma tika: vipākavaṭṭa, kammavaṭṭa
-   upādiṇṇattiika: in ssr, upādiṇṇupādāniya is the base (loiya vipaka ct and kmjrp) constitute the base of living beings; upapattibhava; like the ground for plants to grow
-   saṅkiliṭṭha: other dhammas that depend on the base dhammas; plants that grow on the base
    -   they grow bc of kamma (saṅkilitthasamkilesika etc)
        -   akusala: direct aradication; 2 steps; anusayas first then thenparityutthana is prevented; aku ctj rūpa as well is removed
            -   taken as 1 thing as eradication of defilements
        -   followed by eradiation of upadinnakhandhas that would arise because of akusalas
            -   er. def. also er. kusalas but not completely which becomes kiriya
                -   ku er. not like aku. er.: sobhanadhammas remain; same citta but becomes kiriya due to lack of anusaya's influence
            -   by er. def. -> er. future upadinna khandhas (base) -> er. all other khandhas that depend on them
            -   ptsbdmg: aku & upadinnakhandhas: ajjhatta
                -   abhi: anything that belong to oneself
        -   kusala eradication: 2 steps;
            -   ariyas enter samāpattis to suppress pain, sickness, physical bliss emphasized with similes connecated with samāpattis
-   ācayatika: gami (sampāpaka hetu: lok and loku)
    -   lok sppk: takes to existences; ācayagami
        loku sppk: takes to Nib; apacayagami
    -   maggaṅgas are the sppk hetus
    -   but here in dhsgn, the whole ctpd is taken
    -   related to maggapaccaya (lok loku); paṭṭhāna: only the maggaṅgas, but here all ctpd
    -   mulatika: why not only maggangas? all ctpd because they function as a unit; matika in terms of ctpd normally
-   sekkhā: 3 trainings to remove defilements; sekkhasutta: includes kalyanaputhujjana as sekkha; only sekkha beyond defilements can er. defilements; to er. defilements, the sekha must go beyond the range of def.
    -   the "real" sekha is found in magga cittas
    -   phalacittas: vimutti
    -   vipassanā of a stpn is deeper than that of a puthujjana and leads to a higher magga; same for higher ones
        -   lower phala supports the vipassanā done to attain the next magga; exc. arahattaphala (beyond training; asekkha)

<a id="orgd7d7de5"></a>

### extra notes

-   mūlaṭīka opinion (not the norm): none akusalaetanas of a noble being gives any result. because in paṭṭhāna: some akusala must be eradicated by higher maggas is in one group (sakadagami to arahat); this cetanā is not under kammapaccaya; so this
    -   norm: they do not produce patisandhi
    -   ptsbdmg: uddhaccacetana gives result and falls under bhāvanāya pahātabba
        -   can be the uddhaccacetana of a puthujjana
-   gotrabhūñāṇa does not take saṅkhāras, so it is not included as vipassanāñāṇa
-   in some places: lokuttaravipassanā: maggañāṇa &#x2013; in the sense that it eradicates defilements
-   in Laos, it is recited in funerals
-   ālaṅkāra course (Myamar):
    -   AN, Dhammapada, Vinayapiṭaka
        -   in terms of abhidhamma and of grammar (kcyn)
-   Dhamma: doctrine
    dhamma: reality
-   funeral ceremonies:
    -   SL: sīla, aniccāvatasaṅkhāra&#x2026; …dāna of paṁsukūla robe, sharing merits with the deceaded
    -   Thai / Lao: recite mātikās in front of the body
        -   cat tied to a pole: tradition came from a sādhu who had a cat; the cat would eat the food of the pūja; he used to tie the cat to a pole before the pūja; later generations kept doing it without understanding
        -   case of the elder who had a hole in his patta and used to put it agains the sun to see the hole and cover it; students would follow in the same way
-   kusala:
    -   ku sal
    -   kusa la
    -   ku sa la

1.  intrinsic vs extrinsic

    -   intrinsic: inherent, natural to the phenomena
        -   hardness is the intrinsic nature of paṭhavi
    -   extrinsic: originating from the outside, external, extraneous

1.  kusala

    -   **kosallasambhūtā anavajja-sukhavipaka-lakkhaṇā kusalā**
        -   kosallasambhūtā: produced by wisdom (*:q: clarify*)
        -   anavajja: faultless
        -   sukhavipāka: capable of yielding good results in the future
    -   praised by the wise (for being faultless)
    -   healthy (mentally) for emerging from the sickness of defilements
    -   beneficial to one who produced them
    -   refined
    -   soft
    -   subtle
    -   destructive of unwholesome that have arisen and not yet arisen
    -   only mentalities

                    -   faultless -> praise by the wise

                    -   bodhipakkhiyadhammas associated with vipassanā, magga, and phala are also termed kusala even though not all of them necessarily produce good results.
                        -   This information suggests that to coin a certain reality as “kusala” it is not compulsory for it to produce pleasant results in the future.10
                        -   Aparaṃ pana, bhante, etadānuttariyaṃ yathā bhagavā dhammaṃ deseti kusalesu dhammesū

    6.  analysis of kusala (maybe repeated)
    
        “Kusala” is a Pāli word that can be interpreted in several ways. In Abhidhamma perspective it 
        stands for mentalities that are have arisen due to wisdom, faultless and therefore praised by the 
        wise and bring pleasant results in future – “Kosallasambhūtā anavajja-sukhavipaka-lakkhaṇā 
        kusalā.” 
        There are few intrinsic characteristics of the term kusala.  
        (1). Ārogyaṭṭhena … kusalaṃ – These mental states are called kusala, due to their 
        healthiness. 
        (2). Anavajjaṭṭhena … kusalaṃ – These mental states are called kusala, due to being 
        faultless. 
        (3). Sukhavipākaṭṭhena kusalaṃ – These mentalities are called kusala, due to their nature 
        of bringing pleasant results after their existence (in future).5 
        (4). Sundaraṭṭhena (kusalaṃ) – These mentalities are called kusala, they are beneficial to 
        the person who produced them. 
        (5). Chekaṭṭhena (kusalaṃ) – These mentalities are called kusala, since they are the 
        skillfulness of the mind. Their skilfullness is due to the softness and subtlness of 
        wholesome mentalities. 
        In addition, the term kusala also has the characteristics of “sevitabbaṭṭha” (to be associated 
        with) and “uparibhāgaṅgagamanīyaṭṭha” (the nature of leading up/leading to blissful realms 
        and Nibbāna/giving pleasant results whenever resulting). 
        According to the explanation found in the commentary to the Dhammasaṅgaṇī, there are 
        several abhidhānatthas (etymological explanations) of the term ‘kusala.’ They are as follows: 
        (1). The natures that cause to shake, cause to move, cause to tremble and destroy the 
        disgusting evil states are called kusala – “Kucchite pāpake dhamme salayanti 
        calayanti kampenti viddhaṃsentī’ti kusalā.”6 
        (2). The natures which cut off the unwholesome states that are called kusa are called kusala. 
        The unwholesome states are called kusa, since they lie latently, as they are not 
        eradicated, in a disgusting manner. Unwholesome states are disgusting due to being 
        5Pv-a. 8. 
        6Dhs-a. 81. 
        vi 
        connected with impure states such as rāga, dosa and moha.7 – “Kucchitena (vā) 
        ākārena sayantī’ti kusā. Te akusalasaṅkhāte kuse lunantī’ti kusalā.”8 
        (3). The natures that should be taken (produced) by means of the wisdom that is called kusa 
        are called kusala (the wisdom is called kusa since it lessens (planes), reduces and ends 
        disgusting unwholesome natures) – “Kucchitānaṃ (vā) sānato tanukaranato 
        osānakaraṇato ñāṇaṃ kusaṃ nāma. Tena ñāṇena lātabbā’ti kusalā. “9 
        (4). The natures that cut of both the arisen and non-arisen unwholesome natures, like the 
        kusa grass, are called kusala (kusa grass, when held by the hand, cut the hand from its 
        both edges) – “Yathā (vā) kusaṃ ubhayabhāgagataṃ hatthappadesaṃ lunanti, 
        evamime’pi (kusala-dhammā’pi) uppannānuppannabhāvena ubhayabhāgagataṃ 
        kilesapakkhaṃ lunanti. Tasmā kusā viya lunantī’ti’pi kusalā.” 10  
    
    7.  meanings of kusala acc. to context
    
        -   sabhiya sutta: kusala: **Arahant**, who has cut off the sheaths of kamma, existence (bhava) and sense bases (āyatana)
            -   puggalavācī dabbattha: person-designating substantial meaning
            -   dhammavāci dabbattha: dhamma-designating sustantial meaning: either to the eight path factors beginning with right view (sammā diṭṭhi) found in the four supramundane path cittas, or to the four path cittuppādas themselves, which cut off the sheaths of kamma, existence and sense bases
        -   Netti method: kusala: **akusala cittas which cognize kusala dhammas** may be interpreted as ‘kusala’ – “Tattha kusalāti kusaladhammārammaṇā.”
        -   other usages of kusala
            -   kusala taṇhā
            -   kusala māna

2.  akusala

    -   faulty
    -   blamed by the wise (for being faulty)
    -   capable of yielding bad results in the future
    -   produced by ignorance
    -   unhealthy (mentally) (roga) for being afflicted by the sickness of defilements and for hurting the mind
        -   Rāgādayohi dhammā cittasantānassa rujjanaṭṭhena rogānāma&#x2026; Imānipana tehi vuṭṭhitattā kusalānināma arogāni nāmāti attho. (Prd. 52.).
    -   detrimental to one who produced them
    -   unrefined
    -   coarse
    -   gross
    -   only mentalities
    -   On the other hand, the mental states that are opposite to kusala natures are called akusala.33 These are the natures that arise due to ignorance, faulty and therefore dispraised by the wise and which can produce unpleasant future results – “Akosallasambhūā sāvajjadukkhavipākalakkhaṇā akusalā.”
    
    1.  sabhāva saṁvaṇṇanā
    
        -   **rogaṭṭhena akusalaṃ**: since they are sickness, mental illness; sickness causes pain; akusala > mental pain and suffering
        -   **sāvajjaṭṭhena akusalaṃ**: mixed with faults (kilesas); so, dispraised by the wise
        -   **dukkhavipākaṭṭhena akusalaṃ** – these ultimate realities are called akusala since they bring ill results in future (after their existence).  if akusala realities arise in one's mind stream, they will produce unpleasant results in his/her mind stream.
        -   **asundaraṭṭhena akusalaṃ** – these ultimate realities are called akusala, since they do not bring benefits to the person who produces them, rather bring harm.
        -   **achekaṭṭhena akusalaṃ** – these ultimate realities are called akusala, since they are the unskilled. the mind becomes blunt and gross, when it is occupied with akusala.
        -   Another sabhāvattha of “akusala” can be fetched from the definitions given to kusala in the commentaries.
        -   **kucchitaṭṭhena akusalaṃ** 34: since they are disgusting. 35
            -   also the base for various types of calamities.36
        -   extra:
            -   **asevitabbaṭṭha** (not to be associated with)
            -   **adhobhāgaṅgagamanīyaṭṭha** (the nature of leading down/leading to awful realms/giving unpleasant results when ever resulting).37
            -   **pahātabbattha** (to be abandoned)
    
    2.  Abhidhānattha / Abhidheyyattha Saṃvaṇṇanā
    
        -   **na kusalā akusalā**:
            -   opposite of kusala
            -   “na”: not just absence of kusala, but the opposing nature to kusala, preventing the arising of kusalas and weakening them
        -   **akosallasambhūtaṭṭhena akusalāni**:
            -   kosalla: the nature of a wise person, wisdom
            -   akosalla: ignorance
            -   natures which arise due to akosalla and maintained by akosalla are called akusala 38
            -   this is a definition done based on the ancient commentary
                -   ahirika and anottappa welcome akusala, needed for the continuation of akusala long term (as well as akusalavīriya)
                -   hiri and ottappa shrink from akusala
    
    3.  Dabbaṭṭha saṁvaṇṇanā &#x2013; Pāḷi
    
        [
        Katame dhammā akusalā? Dvādasa akusalacittuppādā – ime dhammā akusalā.
        
        -   why not catūsu bhūisu? or tīsu (since there is lobha in arupa realm)?
            -   akusala is only kamavacara: mostly arise in kāmāvacara, not object of arūpa taṇhā
        
        ]    

3.  abyākata

    -   indeterminate states (na a vi ā √kar ta)
    -   neither declared as kusala nor akusala
    -   do not produce results in the future
        -   **avipākalakkhaṇā abyākatā** 39 (sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇanā)
            -   not giving good or bad results (vipāka) like wholesome or unwholesome realities by means of nānakkhaṇika-kammapaccaya
            -   abyākata does become causes (paccaya) for various results (paccayuppanna) by means of all the other conditional forces other than the nānakkhaṇika-kamma-paccaya
    
    1.  abhidhānattha / vacanattha saṁvaṇṇanā
    
        -   **abyākata**: natures that are neither mentioned as kusala nor akusala – “na byākatā’ti abyākatā, kusalākusalabhāvena akathitā’ti attho” 40
        -   **2 explanations**:
            -   neither **expounded** as kusala nor as akusala are called abyākatā – “Na byākatāti abyākatā, kusalākusalabhāvena akathitāti attho.”
            -   kusala and akusala are collectively called byākata (vi + ā + √kar + ta)
                -   “vi”: opposition (virodha)
                -   “ā”: the nature of facing
            -   **byākata**: realities that are made to be opposite to each other by their own causes. that means the kusala and akusala realities.
            -   they are opposite to each other in terms of their intrinsic nature of giving pleasant and unpleasant results (lakkhaṇato) and being the abandoner (pahāyaka) and the reality that must be abandoned (pahātabba).
            -   on the other hand, the realities that are other than byākata are abyākata.
            -   they are the realities that do not yield results (avipāka).
            -   **avipāka** realities:
                -   are not opposed to any reality as kusalas, which give good results, are opposed to akusalas, which give bad results, and vice versa.
                -   moreover, avipāka realities do no abandon any other reality and they are not to be abandoned by any other reality. (not pahāyaka, abandoner, and appahātabba)
            -   this definition is given by the author of the mūlaṭīkā as one of his personal opinions.41
            -   **why “not expouned/preached/said”?** they are in other triads under terms such as sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā, cakkhuviññāṇa, phassa, and so on.
                -   though they are mentioned under other terms, those terms are neither included in nor relevant to this particular triad. what is relevant here are only kusala and akusala. therefore, abyākata signifies that these realities are not mentioned as either kusala or akusala
            -   kusala -> pleasant results
                akusala -> unpleasant results
                abyākata -> no results 42
    
    2.  dabbattha saṁvaṇṇanā
    
        **Katame dhammā abyākatā? Catūsu bhūmīsu vipāko, tīsu bhūmīsu 
        kiriyābyākataṃ, rūpañca, nibbānañca – ime dhammā abyākatā**

4.  other

    Remarks 
    Since this is an inclusive triad (as all the ultimate realities are included in it), 
    it is called a “nippadesattika” – a triad which covers the whole range of 
    ultimate realities.  
    Moreover, since the triad is coined based on the first word out the three, this 
    is called an “ādiladdhanāmattika” – a triad which got its name based on the 
    first word (pada).   

5.  oppositions

    -   arguments against the idea of kusala triad (kusalattika) based on the three terms (pair-terms) kusalā dhammā, akusalā dhammā and abyākatā dhammā
        -   **argument 1 (apposition, same meaning)**: kusala, akusala, and abyākata mean the same as dhamma, being in apposition with it, causing the tika to collapse into a group of one (ekaka), equalizing the meaning of the 3 terms
            -   each word in each pair refers to the same thing
            -   indo sakko: then each word in each pair would
            -   all tikas would become ekaka
            -   then the entire mātikās would become just one: dhamma
        -   **argument 2 (apposition, different meaning of dhamma)**: each term dhamma refers to different things
            -   kusala would become non-existent
            -   dhamma means vijjamānabhāva (existing nature)
            -   if the dhamma 1 refer to vijjamānabhāva, the other terms dhamma would mean something else
            -   if each term dhamma refers to something different, dhamma 1 would mean vijjamānabhāva and the other terms dhamma would refer to avijjamānabhāva
                -   the same could be said from the perspective of the term dhamma that follows akusala or abyākata
        -   **argument 3 (non-apposition, different meaning of dhamma)**: each term refers to different things, which leads to a hexad (at most) instead of a triad
            -   ku, aku, by, dh1, dh2, dh3, all with different meanings
            -   indo kuvero: each refer to a different thing
            -   if so, all tikas would become chakas and all dukas catukkas (at most, other combinations are possible)
            -   **variation: (non-apposition, same meaning of dhamma)**: which leads to a tetrad (ku, aku, by, dh)
                -   mūla-ṭ: dhamma here must have 3 different meanings otherwise there would be no reason for its usage in three places
                    -   like māsa: month, a type of grain, a golden coin

6.  notes

    -   **samānādhikaraṇa**:
        -   nīla: refer to the flower emphasizing its blue-nature (guṇa) (the blue thing)
            -   nīla + a: one who has nīla is called nīla
            -   pañño: paññā + a: one who has paññā is called pañño
        -   uppala: refers to the flower emphasizing the flower-nature (jāti)
        -   2 types
    -   **bhinnādhikaraṇa**:
        -
    -   **adhikaraṇa**: the object referred to by a word

<a id="org63837d2"></a>

### extra notes

-   **books with methods (naya) that should be learned under a teacher** (Myanmar tradition)
    -   mātikā of dhammasaṅganī
    -   dhātukathā: 14 methos
    -   yamaka
    -   paṭṭhāna
-   **non-negating 'a'**
    -   vajjāvajjaṁ, phalāphalaṁ, bhavābhavaṁ: "small and big" fault, fruit, or existence; no negation here
        -   the 'a' here is difficult to explain
        -   does not apply to kusalākusalaṁ
-   **anavajja**: must produce vipāka to be kusala in mātikā
    -   but kusala not necessarily refer to what produces vipāka; st, it refers to phalacitta or kiriyacittas


<a id="org1b60fc1"></a>

## 2. Vedanāttika


<a id="org459e8ac"></a>

### Pāḷi

[

-   **sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā**
-   **dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā**
-   **adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā**

]  


<a id="orgf736a52"></a>

### meaning

[

-   **sampayuttā**: connected with sukha does not include sukha, etc.
-   **sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā** – non-self-realities that are conjoined with pleasant feeling through characteristics such as ekuppāda, as though they have become one with that pleasant feeling (as though they have entered into a single mode of existence with that pleasant feeling).
-   **dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā** – non-self-realities that are conjoined with painful/unpleasant feeling through characteristics such as ekuppāda, as though they have become one with that painful/ unpleasant feeling (as though they have entered into a single mode of existence with that unpleasant feeling).
-   **adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā** – non-self realities that are conjoined with neither pleasant nor unpleasant feeling through characteristics such as ekuppāda, as though they have become one with that neither pleasant nor unpleasant feeling (as though they have entered into a single mode of existence with that neither pleasant nor unpleasant feeling).
    ]

1.  Vedanā

    -   mental factor which tastes the ārammaṇa rasa
    -   renderings: “feeling” or “sensation.”
    -   here, both abhidhānattha and sabhāvattha are the same
    
    1.  3 definitions
    
        -   **vedayatīti vedanā** – “the thing which tastes the ārammaṇa rasa is called vedanā.” “
        -   **vindanti ca etāya sattā sātaṃ vā asātaṃ vā labhantīti vedanā** – “the thing with the help of which beings obtain pleasant or unpleasant rasa is called vedanā.”
        -   **vedayitamattameva (vā) etanti vedanā** – “the mere act of tasting the ārammaṇa rasa is called vedanā.”
    
    2.  anubhavana bheda
    
        -   3 based on the manner it tastes the ārammaṇa rasa
            -   sukha vedanā
            -   dukkha vedanā
            -   adukkhamasukha vedanā
    
    3.  The sukha, dukkha and adukkhamasukha nature of vedanā can be explained in 3 ways:
    
        -   First method: vedanā tastes the object
            -   **vedayatīti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā vedanāti vuccati. kiñca vedayati? sukhampi vedayati, dukkhampi vedayati, adukkhamasukhampi vedayati. vedayatīti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā vedanāti vuccati.** 1 (khajjanīyasuttaṁ)
                -   reality which feels/tastes
                    -   comm.: what does the vedanā taste? sukha, dukkha and adukkhamasukha
                        -   sukha refers to a desirable object (iṭṭhārammaṇa)
                            dukkha refers to an undesirable object (aniṭṭhārammaṇa)
                            adukkhamasukha refers to a neutral object (majjhattārammaṇa).2
                        -   the reality which tastes sukha objects (sukhārammaṇa), dukkha objects (dukkhārammaṇa) and adukkhamasukha objects (adukkhamasukhārammaṇa)
                        -   basis for an object to be classified as sukha, dukkha, or adukkhamasukha
                            -   the nature of being a condition for the threefold feelings:
                                sukhārammaṇa: an object which becomes a condition for the arising of sukha vedanā
                                dukkhārammaṇa: an object which becomes a condition for the arising of dukkha vedanā
                                adukkhamasukhārammaṇa: an object which becomes a condition for the arising of adukkhamasukhārammaṇa
                -   it is not a living being who tastes the object, but a non-self-reality called vedanā, which arises due to conditions such as base (vatthu) and object (ārammaṇa).3 2
                    -   comm.: an object is classified as sukha, dukkha or adukkhamasukha based on its nature of being a supportive condition for the arising of sukha, dukkha and adukkhamasukha feelings respectively.
                    -   classification of objects is based on the nature of the feelings
                -   threefold feelings can be defined as follows:
                    -   the feeling which tastes sukha/iṭṭha objects are called sukha vedanā.
                    -   the feeling which tastes dukkha/aniṭṭha objects are called dukkha vedanā.
                    -   the feeling which tastes adukkhamasukha/majjhatta objects are called adukkhamasukha vedanā
                        -   Do these definitions contradict with the abhidhānattha and sabhavattha of vedanā?
                            -   In those definitions, vedanā was defined as a reality which tastes the ārammaṇa rasa. However, here, vedanā is defined as a reality which tastes the ārammaṇa (object) itself.   These definitions do not contradict with each other owing to the way the term “ārammaṇa rasa” is being defined.
                                -   ārammaṇa = ārammaṇarasa
                                -   **maṇimañjūsā Ṭīkā** (tika of the abhidhammatthavibhavini, not in sinhala; box of jewels): ārammaṇa rasa is defined as follows: **Ārammaṇañca taṃ raso cāti ārammaṇaraso”4** – “It is the ārammaṇa (object) and also the rasa (something to be tasted), hence called ārammaṇarasa.” According to this definition, the ārammaṇa (object) is also called rasa, and therefore, the object itself is the ārammaṇa rasa. An object is called rasa since it is something tasted – **rasīyatīti raso**.
                                -   vsm: vedanākkhandha (aggregate of feeling) is the reality which tastes the flavor of paṭhavi dhatu – “Kese pathavīdhātu kakkhaḷalakkhaṇā …pe…assāsapassāse pathavīdhātu kakkhaḷalakkhaṇāti tadārammaṇarasānubhavanakavedanā nature vedanākkhandho.”5
                                    -   vsm-ṭ: the flavor of paṭhavi as its intrinsic of hardness (thaddhabhāva) – “Tattha tassa rasānubhavanakavedanāti thaddhabhāvasaṅkhātassa phoṭṭhabbasabhāvassa anubhavanavasena pavattavedanā.”6 We shall investigate this point deeper.  The phrase “iṭṭhānubhavanlakkhaṇā sukhā” found in the Dhammasaṅgaṇī commentary has been explained in the Mūlaṭīkā in two ways.  Sabhāvato saṅkappato ca yaṃ iṭṭhaṃ, tadanubhavanaṃ iṭṭhākārānubhavanaṃ vā iṭṭhānubhavanaṃ.
    
    4.  a
    
        Accordingly, the characteristic of “iṭṭhānubhavana lakkhaṇa” of pleasant 
        feeling refers to the tasting a desirable object, either by its own nature or by 
        way of conceptual construction. In another way, “iṭṭhānubhavana lakkhaṇa” 
        means tasting the desirable facet (ākāra) of an object.7 
        
        The exposition given in the Atthasālinī Bhāsāṭīkā is helpful in understanding 
        this notion.  
    
    5.  Exposition of the Atthasālinī Bhāsāṭīkā
    
        Following is how the two interpretations of the sub-commentary has been 
        explained in the Bhāsāṭīkā.  
    
    6.  first interpretation of the Ṭīkā
    
        – **Sabhāvato saṅkappato ca yaṃ iṭṭhaṃ, tadanubhavanaṃ iṭṭhānubhavanaṃ** (The object that fits to be desired is called “iṭṭha” – **icchitabbanti iṭṭhaṃ** )
        
        Normally “iṭṭha” object is rendered into English as “desirable object.” There 
        are two types of iṭṭha objects.  
        
        -   **Sabhāva iṭṭha** – Naturally desirable object; an object that is desirable
        
        intrinsically  
        
        -   **Parikappa iṭṭha** – Conceptually desirable object; the object that is
        
        desired by the one who cognizes it by way of conceptual construction 
        
        Among the objects, the objects that are liked by the majority of men of the 
        middle class are considered “sabhāva iṭṭha.” Such desirable objects are tasted 
        by the pleasant feeling connected with the resultant consciousnesses (vipāka 
        cittas) and also non-resultant consciousnesses which arise as **javana** cittas. 
        
        -   **note: vipāka always experiences the object as it is (no parikappa, only sabhāva)**
        
        -   **Parikappa iṭṭha**: objects which are either undesirable or neutral by nature, but are tasted as desirable by the one who cognizes them. This is by way of conceptual construction (saṅkappato).
        
        For example, even though excrement is undesirable by nature, it is considered as a desirable object by a dog (in the mind of a dog). Therefore, the dog becomes delighted when seeing excrement. A very ugly child, who is not a desirable object (to the eye), is seen as a desirable object by the mother due to her love and loving-kindness towards the child. These “parikappa iṭṭha” objects are not tasted by the feelings in resultant consciousnesses. Instead, only pleasant feelings connected with javana cittas taste them.8
        
        [According to this explanation, when pleasant feeling arises in an object that is 
        desirable by nature, it is stated as “The pleasant feeling tastes the (naturally) 
        desirable object.” If pleasant feeling arises in a non-desirable object (undesirable 
        or neutral object), it is stated as “The pleasant feeling tastes the conceptually 
        desirable object.”] 
        4 
        
        -   (*:q: no neutral object*)
    
    7.  second interpretation of the Ṭīkā – **Iṭṭhākārānubhavanaṃ iṭṭhānubhavanaṃ**
    
        -   not to divide the object into two types (anubhāva and parikappa):
            Since a desirable object has to be classified into two as sabhāva iṭṭha and
        
        parikappa iṭṭha in the above explanation, Mūlaṭīkā has given another explanation for the aforesaid commentarial definition in order not to divide the desirable object into to those two categories. In each object, we may get three types of “mental contents” (ākāra) – iṭṭhākāra (desirable mental content), aniṭṭhākāra (undesirable mental content) and majjhattākāra (neutral mental content).
        
        Excrement is undesirable by nature. But there is a (possibility of a) desirable mental content in it which is liked by dogs. Therefore, dogs cognize the desirable mental content of undesirable excrement and produce the pleasant feeling. A very ugly child is an undesirable object. But there is a (possibility of a) desirable mental content in it.
        
        Therefore, the undesirable child manifest in the mother’s mind associated with loving-kindness as a desirable mental content. As a result, the mother gets pleasant feeling towards the very ugly child. We can get an undesirable mental content and a neutral mental content9 in the Buddha who is a highly desirable object (atiiṭṭhārammaṇa).
        
        Therefore, in some ascetics of other religions, domanassa javanas arise cognizing the undesirable mental content cognizing the Buddha. In people who have less faith, while thinking about the Buddha, equanimous feeling arises cognizing the neutral mental content (iṭṭha majjhattākāra).
        
        In people with strong faith, somanassa javanas arises, cognizing the highly desirable mental content (ati-iṭṭhākāra). Thus, the pleasant feeling cognizes the desirable mental content of the object in which various mental contents are possible – **iṭṭhākārānubhavanaṃ iṭṭhānubhavanaṃ** 10
        
        The above is the explanation given in the Bhāsāṭīkā.

        tasting an object as a desirable object: considering it as a desirable object
        
        For an object to be tasted as desirable, it is not necessary for 
        the object to be desirable by nature. Even undesirable and neutral objects can 
        be taken as desirable. This idea is expressed in the Mūlaṭīkā phrase “sabhāvato 
        ca saṅkappato ca.” The manner an object is considered by the mind is called 
        “ākāra” or “citta nimitta” (“mental content”) in the lesson of consciousness.  
        
        Desirability, undesirability and neutrality of Mental content (ārammaṇassa iṭṭhādi-ākāra) “Ākāra” of an object is how an object is known, tasted or recognized by 
        mentalities. This is called “citta nimitta”, “gayhākāra” and “gahetabbākāra.” 
        In Western Philosophy, we call it the “mental content.”  
        
        -   every object can be taken as itta, anittha, or majjhatta: what about nibbana? (*:q: what about nibbāna?*)
        
        5 
        When a consciousness cognizes an object, it has a mental content about it. 
        There is a desirability, undesirability or neutrality in a mental content. These 
        three properties of desirability, undesirability and neutrality are found in the 
        mental content. They are called iṭṭhākāra, aniṭṭhākāra and majjhattākāra 
        respectively.
        
        Since we normally do not distinguish the object of reference (ārammaṇa) and 
        the mental content (ākāra) as two different things, these properties of the 
        mental content are considered as the properties of the object itself. Therefore, 
        these properties are said to be the desirability (iṭṭhākāra), undesirability 
        (aniṭṭhākāra) and neutrality (majjhattākāra) of the object.  
        
        Hereon, the desirability of a mental content is considered as the desirability of 
        the object (ārammaṇassa iṭṭhākāra), the undesirability of a mental content is 
        considered as the undesirability of the object (ārammaṇassa aniṭṭhākāra) and 
        the neutrality of a mental content is considered as the neutrality of the object 
        (ārammaṇassa majjhattākāra).  
        
        Therefore, arising of pleasant feeling means tasting the iṭṭhākāra (desirability) 
        of any type of an object. For that the object does not require to be desirable by 
        nature.  
        
        The same should be understood with regard to the arising of unpleasant and 
        equanimous feelings.  
        
        In the explanations related to defilements (kiles kathā) found in Tipiṭika books, 
        the words such as subha nimitta, paṭigha nimitta and upekkhā nimitta refer to 
        objects, which are desirable and so on, and the properties of desirability and 
        so on of an object.  
        
        (1). Subha nimitta – naturally or conceptually desirable objects or 
        desirability of objects that are supportive to lust  
        (2). Paṭigha nimitta – naturally or conceptually undesirable objects or 
        undesirability of objects that are supportive to anger  
        (3). Upekkhā nimitta – naturally or conceptually neutral object11 or 
        neutrality of objects that are supportive to equanimity associated with 
        ignorance (aññanupekkhā)
    
    8.  three types of properties in every object bhasatika
    
        – desirability, undesirability and neutrality
        
        These three properties of an object are called the flavor (ārammaṇa rasa).  
        
        -   object = flavor
        
        Here, the object and the flavor are not considered as two different things. The 
        object itself is the flavor (ārammaṇa rasa). When it is thus considered, when 
        6 mentalities such as phassa take the object, vedanā also tastes that same object. 
        So, the mentalities in one mental cluster have the same object.  
        
        -   
        
        If the object and its flavor are identical, when mentalities such as phassa and 
        so on take the object according to their nature, they too know the flavor of the 
        object. If so, shouldn’t they also be considered to be tasting? The answer is, it 
        must be said that mentalities such as phassa do taste. But they do not taste as well 
        as vedanā does. They are as if tasting the object partially by means of touching 
        the object and so on. (Here, when mentalities such as phassa and saññā touches and recognizes the objects respectively, they are considered as partially tasting the object.).12

        vedanā tastes the flavor of the object
        **Ārammaṇassa raso ārammaṇaraso** – “The flavor of an object is called āramamṇa rasa.”13 (based on paramatthadpn "**ārammaṇassa iṭṭhāniṭṭharasaṁ**")
        
        -   flavor is a quality/property of the object, not identical with the object. The desirability, undesirability and neutrality of an object are its flavors.
        
        2 definitions:
        
        -   ārammaṇañca (bring from previous section)
        -   ārammaṇassa raso ārammaṇaraso
        
        In phrases such as **Manāpiketi manavaḍḍhake, piyarūpe iṭṭheti attho. Iṭṭhāniṭṭhabhāvo ca puggalavasena ca dvāravasena ca gahetabbo**,14 iṭṭhabhāva, aniṭṭhabhāva and majjhattabhāva are the flavor of an object. based on the puggala and dvāra.
        
        -   cdvm follows this method:
            -   all is desirable, undesirable, or neutral
            -   object is the substance, taste its quality
        
        -   mind knows the object and simultaneously its flavor
        -   Just as the flavor is different from the object, the experience of the flavor is also different from the consciousness that knows the object.  (/:q: /)
        
        -   The flavor exists because of the object, and the experiencing of that flavor exists
        
        because of the consciousness that knows the object. Therefore, this experiencing 
        of flavor is something that arises together with consciousness and depends on 
        it. For this reason, it is a mental factor (cetasika). 
        
        -   notes:
            -   rasa is a conditional force that supports the respective vedanā
        
        -   Knowing or experiencing the flavor/taste is called “tasting” or “feeling.” Therefore, it is given the name vedanā. Just as there can be no object without a flavor, there can never be consciousness without the mental factor of feeling that experiences the flavor of the object. Consciousness always arises together with 7 feeling. Hence, feeling is a mental factor common to all states of consciousness (sabba citta sādhāraṇa cetasika).
        
        -   3 vedanās acc. to 3 rasas
        -   Feeling is of three kinds, according to the three kinds of flavors of the object: When the mind experiences the good (desirable) nature of an object, it is called pleasant feeling or joyful feeling. When the mind experiences the bad (undesirable) nature of an object, it is called painful feeling or unpleasant mental feeling. When the mind experiences the neutral nature of an object, it is called equanimous feeling. Just as the nature of water changes because of dye, the mind changes its character because of feeling. In this regard, iṭṭhārammaṇa rasa means the desirability (iṭṭhabhāva) of an object. Aniṭṭhārammaṇa rasa means the undesirability (aniṭṭhabhāva) of an object. Majjhattārammaṇa rasa means the neutrality (majjhattabhāva) of an object.
        
        -   
        
        There must be an inherent difference between two objects for one to become 
        desirable while the other to become undesirable. This difference is owing to 
        its “unique nature” (visesa bhava). It is mentioned in the commentary of the 
        Khajjanīya Sutta that desirable object becomes a condition for 
        pleasant/agreeable (sāta) feeling and an undesirable object becomes a 
        condition for unpleasant/disagreeable (asāta) feeling. The conditional force in 
        a desirable object which supports the arising of a pleasant feeling is its 
        desirability. The conditional force in an undesirable object which supports the 
        arising of an unpleasant feeling is its undesirability. 
        
        Contact (phassa) means striking against the object. That is, it means coming 
        into contact with the pleasant or unpleasant flavor of the object, arriving at the 
        object’s flavor and taking the object. It is the striking against the object; the 
        touching of the object. 
        
        Because of this, feeling (vedanā) arises experiencing the flavor of the object. 
        Therefore, the Tathāgata taught: “phassapaccayā vedanā” – “feeling arises 
        due to contact.” This is the explanation given in the Paramatthadīpanī.15 
        Contact strikes the object in this way in order to make the desirable, 
        undesirable, and neutral flavors of the object manifest to the arising mental 
        cluster (cituppāda).16 
        According to this method of definition, when it is said that feeling (vedanā) 
        tastes the flavor of the object, it means that, when the object is taken, feeling 
        becomes sensitive to the object’s pleasant, unpleasant, or neutral flavor and 
        thereby tastes the object. 
        8 
        In a single metal cluster, all associated mentalities have the same object 
        (ekārammaṇa). Therefore, the object of feeling is the same object as the object 
        of consciousness. Feeling tastes exactly the same object, that consciousness 
        takes. 
        However, an object is something that can be tasted because it has a flavor 
        (ārammaṇa rasa). If there were no object-flavor, the object would not be 
        something that could be tasted. Therefore, when feeling tastes an object, it 
        necessarily experiences the object together with its flavor. 
        This can be clarified by distinguishing between a condition (paccaya) and the 
        force (power) of a condition (paccaya satti). A condition gives rise to its 
        resultant, but in reality, what assists the arising of the resultant is not merely 
        the condition itself, but the force of the condition (paccaya satti) – that is, the 
        condition’s capacity to produce the resultant. A thing (phenomenon) is truly a 
        condition because it possesses conditional power. If conditional force is 
        absent, a phenomenon cannot be a condition. Since conditional power exists 
        in the condition, instead of saying that conditional power produces the 
        resultant, it is said that a condition endowed with conditional power produces 
        the resultant.  
        In the same way, an object is called “rasa” (“ārammaṇañca taṃ raso cāti 
        ārammaṇaraso”) because it is something that can be tasted (“rasīyatīti raso”). 
        An object has the capacity to be tasted. This capacity allows the object to be 
        tasted by mentalities. This capacity is its quality of being desirable, 
        undesirable, or neutral. If this quality were absent, the object would not be 
        something that could be tasted; that is, it would not be a “rasa.” 
        When feeling tastes an object, what it truly tastes is that desirable, undesirable, 
        or neutral quality of the object. However, instead of saying that feeling tastes 
        the rasa of the object, it is said that feeling tastes the object endowed with 
        rasa. Likewise, because the object is capable of being tasted, the object itself 
        is regarded as rasa, and it is said that feeling experiences the object which is 
        the rasa. 
    
    10. assāda and iṭṭhārammaṇarasa
    
        -   assāda
            -   **sukha and somanassa** vedanā that arise because of a particular dhamma 17
            -   **assādīyatīti assādo** 18: the very **arising** of sukha and somanassa (especially somanassa) due to a dhamma
            -   the **nature of being supportive** for the arising of sukha and somanassa vedanā 19
                -   through conditional forces such as ārammaṇapaccayasatti and so on 20
            -   **assāditabbo assādo**: the quality that makes a dhamma fit to be enjoyed by somanassa 21 22
                -   fit to be enjoyed: possesses the conditional power to give rise to joyful feeling 23 (*:q: can't any dhamma do this?*)
                    -   when a phenomenon is enjoyed through joyful feeling, the quality in that phenomenon that allows it to be enjoyed is meant here. That quality is the desirability of the object. :(*:q: it seems all dhammas would have the conditional power to give rise to all 3 vedanās*)
            -   **desirable objects**: visible forms and other agreeable objects &#x2013; **assādīyatīti assādo** 24
                -   Netti Aṭṭhakathā: sukha and somanassa vedanā themselves are also called enjoyment because they are enjoyed through greed 25
                    -   (*:q: not only through greed*)
            -   **assādeti etāyāti assādo**: that (taṇhā) through which beings enjoy pleasure (sukha) and pleasant objects (sukhārammaṇa) 26
            -   (7). Just as beings enjoy dhammas because of craving, they also enjoy desirable and undesirable phenomena because of distortions (vipallāsa). Therefore, diṭṭhi vipallāsa, citta vipallāsa and saññā vipallāsa are also regarded as assāda.27 Here too, assāda is understood according to the definition “assādeti etāyāti assādo.”
        
        Under the third and fourth explanation, the conditional power that assists pleasant feeling (sukha vedanā) and joyful feeling (somanassa vedanā) it terms of ārammaṇa paccaya and so on is nothing other than the desirability in objects. That is, it is the desirable ārammaṇa rasa. Therefore, in one sense, the desirable flavor (iṭṭhārammaṇa rasa) of an object itself can be called its assāda.
    
    11. Third method
    
        The third method of explaining the threefold division of feelings is found in 
        one of the ways the Paramatthadīpanī explains the piece of teaching on 
        vedanā in the Khajjanīya Sutta. The passage of the Khajjanīya Sutta is as 
        follows: 
        
        Vedayatīti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā vedanāti vuccati. Kiñca vedayati? Sukhampi 
        vedayati, dukkhampi vedayati, adukkhamasukhampi vedayati. Vedayatīti kho, 
        bhikkhave, tasmā vedanāti vuccati.28 
        
        One way in which the Paramatthadīpanī explains the phrase is as follows: 
        “Kiñcavedayatī”ti kathañcavedayati. “Sukhampi vedayatī”ti sukhaṃ hutvāpi 
        vedayati. Sukhabhāvena vedayatīti vuttaṃ hoti.It is said that it feels in the mode 
        of pleasantness. 
        
        “Kathaṃ vedayati” – Here, the word “kathaṃ” refers to the manner or mode. 
        It feels being pleasure (sukhaṃ hutvā vedayati), that is, it feels in the mode of 
        pleasure (with the nature of pleasure) (sukhabhāvena vedayati). In this 
        explanation, the pleasant quality of feeling itself is emphasized. 
        Here, without focusing on the object, the explanation highlights that whenever 
        feeling experiences any object, it does so with a pleasant mode. That 
        pleasantness – the sweetness of feeling itself – is what is emphasized. 
        Now, let us understand separately the terms “sukha,” “dukha,” and 
        “adukkhamasukha” of the threefold feelings. 
    
    12. Sukha
    
        -   **sukha vedanā**: the phenomenon that tastes a desirable object, or that tastes an object as desirable
            -   sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇanā: **iṭṭhānubhavanalakkhaṇā sukhā** (vedanā) 29
                -   that is, experiencing a desirable object, or experiencing an object as desirable 30
                -   it can also be said that sukha has the characteristic of experiencing the desirable flavor of an object, that is, the desirable quality present in the object
            -   3 abhidhānattha saṃvaṇṇaṇā: 
                -   **sukhayatīti sukhā** 31: it is called sukha because it makes the associated mental factors and the physical body it depends on become agreeable (laddhassāda) and supported (anuggahita).32 in this definition, the word “sukha” is used in the feminine form because it refers to pleasant feeling (sukhā vedanā) which is in feminine gender. therefore, in this triad, “sukha” does not merely mean pleasure in general, but specifically highlights the comforting nature of feeling.33
                -   **suṭṭhu khādati khanati vā kāyikaṃ mānasikaṃ ābādhanti sukhaṃ** 34: it is called sukha because it thoroughly dispels (eats, digs out) bodily and mental afflictions.”
                -   **sukaraṃ okāsadānaṃ etassāti sukhaṃ**: it is called sukha because it is easy to allow space for. this explanation is mentioned in the visuddhimagga ṭīkā as the opinion of some teachers.35
    
    13. Dukkha
    
        -   **dukkha vedanā**: the phenomenon that tastes an undesirable object, or that tastes an object as undesirable
        -   sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇanā: **aniṭṭhānubhavanalakkhaṇā dukkhā** (vedanā) 36
        -   That is, experiencing an undesirable object, or experiencing an object as undesirable. It can also be said that dukkha has the characteristic of experiencing the undesirable flavor of an object, that is, the undesirable quality present in the object.
        -   3 abhidhānattha saṃvaṇṇaṇā
            -   (1). “Dukkhayatīti dukkhā”37 – “It is called dukkha because it afflicts and oppresses the associated mental factors and the physical body it depends on 38
            -   (2). “Duṭṭhu khādati, khanati vā kāyikaṃ mānasikaṃ assādaṃti dukkhaṃ”39 – it is called dukkha because it badly destroys bodily and mental enjoyment/comfort 12
            -   (3). “Dukkharaṃ okāsadānaṃ etassāti dukkhaṃ” – “It is called dukkha because it is difficult to allow space for. This explanation is also mentioned in the Visuddhimagga Ṭīkā as the opinion of some teachers.”40
    
    14. Adukkhamasukha
    
        -   adukkhamasukha: upekkhā vedanā: the phenomenon that experiences a neutral object, which is neither pleasant nor unpleasant, or that experiences an object as neutral.
        -   this is its sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇanā: “ubhayaviparītānubhavanalakkhaṇā adukkhamasukhā (vedanā)”41
        -   That is, experiencing an object that is different from both desirable and undesirable, or experiencing an object as neutral. The literal meaning of “adukkhamasukha” is given by the expression: “Na dukkhā na sukhā adukkhamasukhā.” This means neither pleasant nor painful.
        -   The formation of the word is as follows: [na + dukkhā > adukkhā; na + sukhā > asukhā; adukkhā + m + asukhā > adukkhamasukhā]

                                -   e.g.: when referring to disciples, V. Srpt is mentioned

                                    -   puriso ca puriso ca -> purisapurisā -> purisā (ekasesa naya)
                -   **ekanirodhatā**: having the same cessation when a group of realities has one single cessation, that group is called ekanirōdha
                -   **ekavatthukatā**: having the same base (depending on the same base) when a group of phenomena depends on the same base, that group is called ekavatthuka
                -   **ekārammaṇatā**: having the same object when a group of phenomena has the same object, that group is called ekārammaṇa

                    -   ekārammaṇa: eliminates the possibility of rūpas that have the same nissaya(vatthu)
                        -   misinterpretation: multiple cittas taking the same object -> removed by ekavatthuka

                        -   matters for the explanation
