                    -   phenomena that possess these four characteristics are said to be sahagata,
                    -   ampayutta, and saṃsaṭṭha with one another
    
    16. why all four characteristics are given?
    
        the mūlaṭīkā explains that these four characteristics are stated to remove two 
        possible misunderstandings
        
        (1). removing the idea that material and mental phenomena that arise 
        together can be sampayutta 
        if only the characteritic of ekuppādatā were stated, one might think 
        that material phenomena (rūpa) and immaterial phenomena (nāma) 
        that arise together are sampayutta. 
        for example: 
        
        (a). mind-produced rūpa arises together with consciousness 
        (b). paṭisandhi rūpa and paṭisandhi citta arise together to prevent this misunderstanding, the characteristic of ekanirōdhatā is 
        stated. Nāma last for one mind-moment, while rūpa lasts for seventeen 
        mind-moments. Therefore, they do not cease together. 
        However, if only the characteristics of ekuppādatā and ekanirōdhatā 
        were stated, one might think that inseparable matter (avinibbhoga 
        rūpa) are sampayutta, since they arise and cease together. To prevent 
        this, characteristic of ekavatthukatā is stated. 
        
        Still, someone might think rūpa within a material group, which arise 
        and cease together, has ekavatthukatā in two ways: 
        (a). The term “vatthu” can mean something that serves as a support 
        (nissaya) for something else. When understood in this sense of 
        support, it can be considered that great elements in a rūpa 
        kalāpa share one common base. 
        Each of the four great elements serves as a support-condition 
        (nissaya paccaya) for the other three great elements. Because 
        three great elements depend on one single nissaya which is 
        called vatthu, they can be described as having one base in 
        common: “Ekaṃ vatthu nissayo etesaṃti ekavatthukāni.” 
        (b). The term “vatthu” can also mean vatthu rūpa, which serves as 
        the ground for the arising of consciousness. 
        15 
        From this perspective, in a material group that contains a vatthu 
        rūpa, the great elements in that group may be considered as 
        having one common base. 
        For example, in a kamma-produced material group such as the 
        cakkhu dasaka kalāpa, there is only one vatthu rūpa. That 
        vatthu rūpa exists by depending on the great elements within 
        the same material group. 
        Therefore, the great elements in such a group can be described 
        as having one common base: “Ekaṃ vatthu etesu sannissitanti 
        ekavatthukāni.”48 
        Even so, since rūpa never takes an object, it never has the characteristic 
        of ekārammaṇatā. Therefore, rūpa and nāma are never sampayutta.49 
        (2). Removing the idea that mentalities from different mental clusters 
        are sampayutta 
        If only the characteristic of ekārammaṇatā were stated, one might 
        think that multiple cittuppādas within the same cognitive process such 
        as pañcadvārāvajjana50 and sampaṭicchana,  , or even in different 
        beings, are sampayutta because they take the same object. 
        To remove this idea, the characteristic of ekavatthukatā is stated. 
        If only ekārammaṇatā and ekavatthukatā were stated, one might think 
        that different cittuppādas such as pañcadvārāvajjana and 
        sampaṭicchana, santīraṇa near death51 are sampayutta because they 
        share the same object and heart base (hadaya vatthu). 
        To remove this, ekanirōdhatā is stated. 
        Then a question may arise whether the three characteristics mentioned 
        above are found also in realities that have different arisings, or whether 
        they exist only in realities that have the same arising. In response to 
        this question, “ekuppādatā” is stated.52 
        By this, it is made clear that those three characteristics exist only in 
        realities that have the same arising, and not in realities that have 
        different arising. 
        Accordingly, it becomes clear that mentalities in different cittuppādas 
        are never sampayutta with one another. 
        Now, let us examine each term of the triad individually. 
        16 
    
    17. Sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā
    
        Sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā are the non-self-natures that are 
        conjoined with pleasant feeling through characteristics such as ekuppāda, as 
        though they have become one with that pleasant feeling (as though they have 
        entered into a single mode of existence with that pleasant feeling). 
        We have already studied its sabhāvattha and abhidhānattha. Now we shall 
        look into its dabbattha. 
    
    18. Dabbattha Saṃvaṇṇanā (Ultimate Representation)
    
        The ultimate representatives of the word sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā 
        dhammā are as follows.  
        (1). 63 sukha sahagata cittas  
        (2). Their associated 46 cetasikas (except vedanā, dosa, issā, macchariya, 
        kukkucca and vicikicchā) 
    
    19. Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā
    
        dhammā according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        
        Katame dhammā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā? Kāmāvacarakusalato (nom.) cattāro somanassasahagatacittuppādā, akusalato cattāro kāmāvacarakusalassa vipākato ca kiriyato ca pañca, rūpāvacaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca vipākato ca kiriyato ca, lokuttaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca vipākato ca, etthuppannaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ ṭhapetvā – ime dhammā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā.  
        
        -   note:
            -   tika catukka jhāna: 3 sukha jhānas in the 4-fold method, and 4 in the 5-fold
            -   it seems that it should be 6
    
    20. Classification into the sabba saṅgaha
    
        Following is how the sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā realities are categorized 
        in the sabba saṅgaha. 
        Kusala dhammas are categorized; 
        (1). In 3 khandhas 
        (2). In 2 āyatanas 
        (3). In 3 dhātus 
        (4). Some in 3 saccas and some are free from saccas. 
    
    21. Dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā
    
        Dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā are the non-self natures that are 
        conjoined with unpleasant feeling through characteristics such as ekuppāda, 
        as though they have become one with that unpleasant feeling (as though they 
        have entered into a single mode of existence with that unpleasant feeling). 
        17 
        
        We have already studied its sabhāvattha and abhidhānattha. Now we shall 
        look into its dabbattha. 
    
    22. Dabbattha Saṃvaṇṇanā (Ultimate Representation)
    
        The ultimate representatives of the word sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā 
        dhammā are as follows.  
        (1). 3 dukkha sahagata cittas  
        (2). Their associated 21 cetasikas (except vedanā, pīti, lobha, diṭṭhi, māna 
        and vicikicchā) 
    
    23. Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā
    
        dhammā according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        Katame dhammā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā? Kāmāvacarakusalato cattāro 
        somanassasahagatacittuppādā, akusalato cattāro kāmāvacarakusalassa 
        vipākato ca kiriyato ca pañca, rūpāvacaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca 
        vipākato ca kiriyato ca, lokuttaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca vipākato ca, 
        etthuppannaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ ṭhapetvā – ime dhammā sukhāya vedanāya 
        sampayuttā.  
    
    24. Classification into the sabba saṅgaha
    
        Following is how the dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā realities are categorized 
        in the sabba saṅgaha. 
        Kusala dhammas are categorized; 
        (1). In 3 khandhas 
        (2). In 2 āyatanas 
        (3). In 3 dhātus 
        (4). In 1 sacca 
    
    25. Adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā
    
        Adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā are the non-self natures 
        that are conjoined with neither pleasant nor unpleasant feeling through 
        characteristics such as ekuppāda, as though they have become one with that 
        neither pleasant nor unpleasant feeling (as though they have entered into a 
        single mode of existence with that neither pleasant nor unpleasant feeling). 
        We have already studied its sabhāvattha and abhidhānattha. Now we shall 
        look into its dabbattha. 
    
    26. Dabbattha Saṃvaṇṇanā (Ultimate Representation)
    
        The ultimate representatives of the word sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā 
        dhammā are as follows.  
        18 
        (1). 55 adukkhama-sukha sahagata cittas  
        (2). Their associated 46 cetasikas (except vedanā, pīti, dosa, issā, 
        macchariya and kukkucca) 
    
    27. Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya
    
        sampayuttā dhammā according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the 
        Dhammasaṅganī 
        
        Katame dhammā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā? Kāmāvacarakusalato cattāro 
        somanassasahagatacittuppādā, akusalato cattāro kāmāvacarakusalassa 
        vipākato ca kiriyato ca pañca, rūpāvacaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca 
        vipākato ca kiriyato ca, lokuttaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca vipākato ca, 
        etthuppannaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ ṭhapetvā – ime dhammā sukhāya vedanāya 
        sampayuttā.
        
        1.  katame dhammā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā? kāmāvacarakusalato cattāro somanassasahagatacittuppādā, akusalato cattāro kāmāvacarakusalassa vipākato ca kiriyato ca pañca, rūpāvacaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca vipākato ca kiriyato ca, lokuttaratikacatukkajjhānā kusalato ca vipākato ca, etthuppannaṃ sukhaṃ vedanaṃ ṭhapetvā – ime dhammā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā.
            1.  katame dhammā dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā? dve domanassasahagatacittuppādā, dukkhasahagataṃ kāyaviññāṇaṃ, etthuppannaṃ dukkhaṃ vedanaṃ ṭhapetvā – ime dhammā dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā.
            2.  katame dhammā adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā? kāmāvacarakusalato cattāro upekkhāsahagatacittuppādā, akusalato cha, kāmāvacarakusalassa vipākato dasa, akusalassa vipākato cha, kiriyato cha, rūpāvacaraṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ kusalato ca vipākato ca kiriyato ca, cattāro arūpāvacarā kusalato ca vipākato ca kiriyato ca, lokuttaraṃ catutthaṃ jhānaṃ kusalato ca vipākato ca, etthuppannaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ vedanaṃ ṭhapetvā – ime dhammā adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā. tisso ca vedanā, rūpañca, nibbānañca – ime dhammā na vattabbā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttātipi, dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttātipi, adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttātipi.
    
    28. Classification into the sabba saṅgaha
    
        Following is how the dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttā realities are categorized 
        in the sabba saṅgaha. 
        Kusala dhammas are categorized; 
        (1). In 3 khandhas 
        (2). In 2 āyatanas 
        (3). In 7 dhātus 
        (4). Some in 3 saccas and some are free from saccas. 
    
    29. Tikamuttakā dhammā
    
        There are some realities that does not include in any of the three terms of this 
        tika. They are called tikamuttakā dhamma (realities which are freed from the 
        triad).  
        Their dabbattha is as follows.  
        (1). 63 sukha vedanā in 63 sukha sahagata cittas 
        (2). 3 dukkha vedanā in 3 dukkha sahagata cittas 
        (3). 55 adukkhamasukha vedanā in 55 adukkhamasukha sahagata cittas 
        (4). 28 rūpa 
        (5). Nibbāna 
        In this triad, vedanā cetasika is freed from the triad since only one feeling arise 
        in each mental cluster and there is no other vedanā to be conjoined with it.  
        No reality conjoins with rūpa and Nibbāna.  
        19 
    
    30. Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the tikamuttaka dhammas of the vedanāttika
    
        according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        Tisso ca vedanā, rūpañca, nibbānañca – ime dhammā na vattabbā sukhāya 
        vedanāya sampayuttātipi, dukkhāya vedanāya sampayuttātipi, ya vedanāya 
        sampayuttātipi. 
        Remarks 
        
        Since this is a non-inclusive triad (as all the ultimate realities are not included 
        in it), it is called a “sappadesa tika” – a triad which has a range of ultimate 
        realities that is not covered by the triad.  
        
        Moreover, since the triad is coined based on a word found in all the three 
        terms, this is called an “sabbaladdhanāma tika” – a triad which got its name 
        based on all the words (pada).   
        
        Starting from here, all tikas are ādiladdhanāma tikas.  
    
    31. Sequence of the discourse (desanākkama)
    
        We need to understand why the vedanāttika was taught after the kusalattika.  
        First, the Tathāgata taught the Kusala triad, dividing all phenomena into three 
        groups: wholesome, unwholesome, and indetermined. Then, within each of 
        these groups, he showed that phenomena are further divided in many ways 
        according to which type of feeling they are associated with. To make this clear, 
        he taught the vedanāttika, beginning with the expression “sukhāya vedanāya 
        sampayuttā dhammā”. 
        
        Some wholesome phenomena are associated with pleasant feeling, while some 
        wholesome phenomena are associated with neither-painful-nor-pleasant 
        feeling. 
        
        Some unwholesome phenomena are associated with pleasant feeling, some are 
        associated with painful feeling, and some are associated with neither-painful
        nor-pleasant feeling. 
        
        Among indetermined phenomena, those that are capable of being associated 
        with feeling are also divided: some are associated with pleasant feeling, and 
        some with neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling. Phenomena that are not 
        capable of association with feeling are not associated with any feeling at all. 
        
        After teaching the vedanāttika following the kusalattika, the Buddha then 
        taught the vipākattika in order to explain, separately, resultant nature and so 
        on of realities associated with pleasant, painful, and neutral feeling. 
        Among phenomena associated with pleasant feeling, some are resultants 
        (vipāka), some are result-producing (vipākadhamma), and some are neither 
        20 
        resultant nor result-producing (neva vipāka na vipākadhamma). The same type 
        of analysis is given for phenomena associated with painful feeling and for 
        those associated with neither-painful-nor-pleasant feeling. 
        In this way, the purpose of the sequence of the triads and dyads must be shown 
        accordingly.53 
        21

        vedeti anubhavati. Parato padadvayepi eseva nayo. Kathaṃ panetaṃ ārammaṇaṃ sukhaṃ 
        dukkhaṃ adukkhamasukhaṃ nāma jātanti? Sukhādīnaṃ paccayato. Svāyamattho "yasmā ca 
        kho, mahāli, rūpaṃ sukhaṃ sukhānupatitaṃ sukhāvakkanta"nti imasmiṃ mahālisutte

        somanassaṃ, ayaṃ rūpassa assādo. (S. II. 23.). 
        22 
        "Yaṃ kho, bhikkhave, ime pañca kāmaguṇe paṭicca uppajjati sukhaṃ somanassaṃ, ayaṃ 
        vuccati kāmānaṃ assādo"tiādivacanato sukhavedanāva assādoti āha "assādabhāvato"ti. 
        (Anu-t. I. 93.). 
        18 Aparo nayo – yanti sukhaṃ, somanassañca. Ayanti ca napuṃsakaliṅgena niddiṭṭhaṃ 
        sukhasomanassameva assādapadamapekkhitvā pulliṅgena niddisīyati, imasmiṃ pana 
        vikappe sukhasomanassānaṃ uppādoyeva tehi uppādavantehi niddiṭṭho, sattiyā, sattimato ca 
        abhinnattā. Na hi sukhasomanassamantarena tesaṃ uppādo labbhati. Iti purimavedanaṃ 
        paṭicca sukhasomanassuppādopi purimavedanāya assādo nāma assādīyateti katvā. (D-abh-ṭ. 
        I. 408-9.). 
        19 “Yaṃ vedanaṃ paṭicca uppajjati sukhaṃ somanassaṃ, ayaṃ vedanāya assādo"ti evaṃ 
        assādañca yathābhūtaṃ viditvā&#x2026; (D-a. I. 101.). 
        Yanti yasmā, yaṃ vā sukhaṃ somanassaṃ. Paṭiccāti ārammaṇapaccayādibhūtaṃ vedanaṃ 
        labhitvā. Ayanti sukhasomanassānaṃ paccayabhāvo, sukhasomanassameva vā, "assādo"ti 
        padaṃ pana apekkhitvā pulliṅganiddeso. (D-ṭ. I. 150-1.). 
        Because this explanation is given in relation to feeling, the Ṭīkā mentions only the causal 
        power (paccaya satti) present in somanassa vedanā as a condition for sukha and somanassa 
        feelings. 
        However, since the above discourse was preached by the Tathāgata also with reference to 
        material phenomena (rūpa) and others, it should be understood that assāda is the factor that 
        functions as the causal force for the arising of sukha and somanassa feelings in any given 
        dhamma. 
        yasmā 
        Therefore, the Abhinava Ṭīkā on the Dīgha Nikāya states this as follows. 
        Yanti 
        paccayabhāvasaṅkhātahetuto. Vedanaṃ paṭiccāti purimuppannaṃ 
        ārammaṇādipaccayabhūtaṃ vedanaṃ labhitvā. Sukhaṃ somanassanti sukhañceva 
        somanassañca. 
        Ayanti 
        purimavedanāya 
        yathārahaṃ 
        pacchimuppannānaṃ 
        sukhasomanassānaṃ paccayabhāvo. Assādo nāma assāditabboti katvā. (D-abh-ṭ. I. 408.). 
        20 Yanti yasmā, yaṃ vā sukhaṃ somanassaṃ. Paṭiccāti ārammaṇapaccayādibhūtaṃ vedanaṃ 
        labhitvā. (D-ṭ. I. 150-1.). 
        Even the pakatūpanissaya paccaya force helps for the arising of the sukha and somanassa 
        vedanā. 
        Kathaṃ pana vedanaṃ ārabbha sukhaṃ uppajjati, nanu phoṭṭhabbārammaṇanti? 
        Cetasikasukhasseva ārabbha pavattiyamadhippetattā nāyaṃ doso. Ārabbha pavattiyañhi 
        visesanameva somanassaggahaṇaṃ somanassaṃ sukhanti yathā "rukkho sīsapā"ti 
        aññapaccayavasena uppattiyaṃ pana kāyikasukhampi assādoyeva, yathālābhakathā vā esāti 
        daṭṭhabbaṃ. (D-abh-ṭ. I. 409.). 
        21 Ayanti sukhasomanassānaṃ paccayabhāvo, sukhasomanassameva vā, "assādo"ti padaṃ 
        pana apekkhitvā pulliṅganiddeso. Ayañhettha saṅkhepattho – purimuppannaṃ vedanaṃ 
        ārabbha somanassuppattiyaṃ yo purimavedanāya assādetabbākāro somanassassādanākāro, 
        ayaṃ assādoti. (D-ṭ. I. 151.). 
        Since all these passages are based primarily on somanassa vedanā, it should be understood 
        that assāda is explained here by focusing on somanassa, in accordance with the manner in 
        which gratification is taken in the object – namely, in the pleasant feeling that is made the 
        object. 
        23 
        In the case of serving as a condition for bodily pleasant feeling (kāyika sukha vedanā), assāda 
        should not be understood merely as the way in which dhammas are gratified, but also as 
        including other modes of conditionality (paccaya bhāva). 
        Therefore, the Abhinava Ṭīkā on the Dīgha Nikāya states it as follows. 
        Katvā. 
        Ayañhettha 
        saṅkhepattho – 
        purimamuppannaṃ vedanaṃ ārabbha 
        somanassuppattiyaṃ yo purimavedanāya paccayabhāvasaṅkhāto assādetabbākāro, 
        somanassassa vā uppādasaṅkhāto tadassādanākāro, ayaṃ purimavedanāya assādoti. 
        Kathaṃ pana vedanaṃ ārabbha sukhaṃ uppajjati, nanu phoṭṭhabbārammaṇanti? 
        Cetasikasukhasseva ārabbha pavattiyamadhippetattā nāyaṃ doso. Ārabbha pavattiyañhi 
        visesanameva somanassaggahaṇaṃ somanassaṃ sukhanti yathā "rukkho sīsapā"ti 
        aññapaccayavasena uppattiyaṃ pana kāyikasukhampi assādoyeva, yathālābhakathā vā esāti 
        daṭṭhabbaṃ. (D-abh-ṭ. I. 409.). 
        22 Yanti yasmā, yaṃ vā sukhaṃ somanassaṃ. Paṭiccāti ārammaṇapaccayādibhūtaṃ vedanaṃ 
        labhitvā. Ayanti sukhasomanassānaṃ paccayabhāvo, sukhasomanassameva vā, "assādo"ti 
        padaṃ pana apekkhitvā pulliṅganiddeso. (D-abh-ṭ. I. 150-1.). 
        Yanti 
        yasmā 
        paccayabhāvasaṅkhātahetuto. Vedanaṃ paṭiccāti purimuppannaṃ 
        ārammaṇādipaccayabhūtaṃ vedanaṃ labhitvā. Sukhaṃ somanassanti sukhañceva 
        somanassañca. 
        Ayanti 
        purimavedanāya 
        yathārahaṃ 
        pacchimuppannānaṃ 
        sukhasomanassānaṃ paccayabhāvo. Assādo nāma assāditabboti katvā. (D-abh-ṭ. I. 408.). 
        23 Yanti yasmā, yaṃ vā sukhaṃ somanassaṃ. Paṭiccāti ārammaṇapaccayādibhūtaṃ vedanaṃ 
        labhitvā. Ayanti sukhasomanassānaṃ paccayabhāvo, sukhasomanassameva vā, "assādo"ti 
        padaṃ pana apekkhitvā pulliṅganiddeso. (D-t. I. 150-1.). 
        24 Yathā cetaṃ sukhaṃ somanassaṃ, evaṃ iṭṭhārammaṇampi. Vuttampi cetaṃ – "so 
        tadassādeti taṃ nikāmetī"ti "rūpaṃ assādeti abhinandati, taṃ ārabbha rāgo uppajjatī"ti, 
        "saṃyojanīyesu, bhikkhave, dhammesu assādānupassino"ti ca. (Nett-ṭ. 20.). 
        25 Tattha assādīyatīti assādo, sukhaṃ somanassañca. Vuttañhetaṃ – “Yaṃ, bhikkhave, 
        pañcupādānakkhandhe paṭicca uppajjati sukhaṃ somanassaṃ, ayaṃ pañcasu 
        upādānakkhandhesu assādo”ti. (Nett-a. 19-20.). 
        Yaṃ, bhikkhaveti ettha yanti paccattavacanaṃ, tañca sukhaṃ, somanassanti dvayena 
        samānādhikaraṇanti katvā "assādīyatīti assādo, sukhaṃ,-somanassañcā"ti vuttaṃ. 
        Sukhādivedanā viya manāpiyarūpādipi avītarāgassa assādetabbanti āha "evaṃ 
        iṭṭhārammaṇampī"ti. (Nett-ṭ. 25-6.). 
        26 Assādeti etāyāti vā assādo, taṇhā. Taṇhāya hi kāraṇabhūtāya puggalo sukhampi 
        sukhārammaṇampi assādeti. (Nett-a. 20.). 
        27 Yathā ca taṇhā, evaṃ vipallāsāpi. Vipallāsavasena hi sattā aniṭṭhampi ārammaṇaṃ 
        iṭṭhākārena assādenti. (Nett-a. 20.). 
        Iti katvā ayamattho diṭṭhābhinandanādibhāvato vipallāsesupi sambhavatīti āha "evaṃ 
        vipallāsāpī"ti. Aniṭṭhampīti pi-saddena iṭṭhampīti yojetabbaṃ. (Nett-ṭ. 26.).  
        28 S. II. 71. 
        29 Dhs-a. 84. 
        30 Sabhāvato saṅkappato ca yaṃ iṭṭhaṃ, tadanubhavanaṃ iṭṭhākārānubhavanaṃ vā 
        iṭṭhānubhavanaṃ. (Ml-ṭ. I. 37.). 
        24 
        31 Dhs-a. 84. 
        32 In many places, the body is considered as the vatthu (base) of the vedanā. 
        33 Sampayutte vatthuñca karajakāyaṃ sukhayati laddhassāde anuggahite karotīti sukhā. 
        Sukhāti vedanāsaddamapekkhitvā sukhabhāvamattassa appakāsanena napuṃsakaliṅgatā na 
        katā. (Ml-ṭ. I. 37.). 
        34 Suṭṭhu vā khādati, khaṇati vā kāyikaṃ ābādhanti sukhaṃ. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 136.).   
        This definition is given for the bodiliy pleasure. When the word sukha is defined based on 
        anubhavana bheda, the phrase “mānasikaṃ ābādhaṃ” must be insterted.  
        35 Sukaraṃ okāsadānaṃ etassāti sukhanti apare. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 136.). 
        36 Dhs-a. 84. 
        37 Dhs-a. 84. 
        38 Dukkhayatīti dukkhaṃ, kāyaṃ, sampayuttadhamme ca vibādhatīti attho. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 136.). 
        39 Vsm-ṭ. II. 136. 
        This definition is given for the bodiliy pain. When the word dukkha is defined based on 
        anubhavana bheda, the phrase “mānasikaṃ assādaṃ” must be insterted.  
        40 Dukkaraṃ okāsadānaṃ etassāti dukkhanti apare. (Vsm-ṭ.. II. 136.). 
        41 Dhs-a. 84. 
        42 Dhs-a. 84. 
        43 Ml-ṭ. I. 37. 
        44 Ml-ṭ. I. 37. 
        45 Asamānapaccayehi ekajjhaṃ uppattito samānapaccayehi ekajjhaṃ uppatti sātisayāti 
        ukkaṃsagativijānanavasena "samānapaccayehi sahuppattikāti attho"ti vuttaṃ. (Anu-ṭ. I. 
        40.). 
        46 Srd-ṭ. I. 323. 
        47 Atha vā uppajjanaṃ uppādo, uppajjati etasmāti uppādoti duvidhopi uppādo ekuppādāti 
        ettha ekasesanayena saṅgahitoti iminā adhippāyena "samā…pe… attho"ti vuttaṃ siyā. (Anu
        ṭ. I. 40.). 
        48 
        Evamapi avinibbhogarūpesu ekaṃ mahābhūtaṃ sesamahābhūtopādārūpānaṃ 
        nissayapaccayo hotīti tena tāni ekavatthukānīti, cakkhādinissayabhūtāni vā bhūtāni ekaṃ 
        vatthu etesu sannissitanti ekavatthukānīti kappentassa tesaṃ sampayuttatāpatti siyāti 
        tannivāraṇatthaṃ "ekārammaṇā"ti vuttaṃ, ye ekuppādā…pe… ekārammaṇā ca honti, te 
        sampayuttāti. (Ml-ṭ. I. 37.). 
        Tena tāni ekavatthukānīti etassa ca "kappentassā"tiādinā sambandho. Tattha purimavikappe 
        ekaṃ vatthu nissayo etesanti yojanā, na ekaṃyeva vatthūti. Ekekabhūtassa 
        bhūtattayanissitattā catubhūtanissitattā ca upādārūpānaṃ. Dutiyavikappe pana ekaṃyeva 
        vatthu etesu nissitanti yojanā. Nissayanissitatāsaṅkhātaupakāropakattabbabhāvadīpanaṃ 
        ekavatthukavacananti dutiyavikappe mahābhūtavasena yojanā katā. Itarathā ekaṃ vatthu 
        etesūti samāsatthabhāvena upādārūpānampi pariggaho vattabbo siyā. (Anu-ṭ. I. 40.). 
        25 
        49 Sahuppattikānaṃ rūpārūpānañca aññamaññasampayuttatā āpajjeyyāti "ekanirodhā"ti 
        vuttaṃ, ye samānuppādā samānanirodhā ca, te sampayuttāti rūpārūpānaṃ 
        aññamaññasampayogo 
        nivārito 
        hoti. 
        Evamapi 
        avinibbhogarūpānaṃ 
        aññamaññasampayuttatā āpajjeyyāti "ekavatthukā"ti vuttaṃ, ye ekuppādā ekanirodhā 
        ekavatthukā ca, te sampayuttāti. Evamapi avinibbhogarūpesu ekaṃ mahābhūtaṃ 
        sesamahābhūtopādārūpānaṃ nissayapaccayo hotīti tena tāni ekavatthukānīti, 
        cakkhādinissayabhūtāni vā bhūtāni ekaṃ vatthu etesu sannissitanti ekavatthukānīti 
        kappentassa tesaṃ sampayuttatāpatti siyāti tannivāraṇatthaṃ "ekārammaṇā"ti vuttaṃ, ye 
        ekuppādā…pe… ekārammaṇā ca honti, te sampayuttāti. (Ml-ṭ. I. 37.). 
        50 
        Pañcaviññāṇasampaṭicchanānanti 
        Kiriyamanodhātucakkhuviññāṇādayopi 
        pākaṭoyamatthoti. (Anu-ṭ. I. 40.). 
        idaṃ 
        hi 
        nidassananti 
        daṭṭhabbaṃ. 
        ekārammaṇābhinnavatthukā 
        cāti 
        51 Santīraṇādīnanti ādi-saddena voṭṭhabbanajavanatadārammaṇāni saṅgayhanti, etāni ca 
        sampaṭicchanādīni cutiāsannāni idhādhippetānīti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Tāni hi taduddhaṃ 
        kammajarūpassa anuppattito ekasmiṃyeva hadayavatthusmiṃ vattanti, itarāni pana 
        purimapurimacittakkhaṇuppanne hadayavatthusmiṃ uttaruttarāni pavattantīti. (Anu-ṭ. I. 
        40.). 
        52 Paṭilomato vā ekārammaṇāti vutte ekavīthiyañca pañcaviññāṇasampaṭicchanānaṃ 
        nānāvīthiyaṃ parasantāne ca ekasmiṃ ārammaṇe uppajjamānānaṃ bhinnavatthukānaṃ 
        sampayuttatā āpajjeyyāti "ekavatthukā"ti vuttaṃ, ye ekavatthukā hutvā ekārammaṇā, te 
        sampayuttāti. 
        Evamapi sampaṭicchanasantīraṇādīnaṃ 
        sampayuttatā 
        āpajjeyyāti 
        "ekanirodhā"ti vuttaṃ, ye ekanirodhā hutvā ekavatthukā ekārammaṇā, te sampayuttāti. Kiṃ 
        pana nānuppādāpi evaṃ tividhalakkhaṇā honti, atha ekuppādā evāti vicāraṇāya ekuppādā 
        eva evaṃ tividhalakkhaṇā hontīti dassanatthaṃ "ekuppādā"ti vuttaṃ. (Ml-ṭ. I. 37-8.). 
        53 Etthāha – "kasmā panettha kusalattikānantaraṃ vedanāttikova vutto"ti? Kismiṃ pana 
        vuccamāne ayamanuyogo na siyā, apica avayavānaṃ anekabhedatādassanatthā 
        tikantaradesanā. Sammāsambuddhena hi kusalattikena sabbadhammānaṃ tidhā vibhāgaṃ 
        dassetvā puna tadavayavānaṃ kusalādīnampi anekabhedabhinnataṃ dassentena tesaṃ 
        vedanāsampayogavibhāgavibhāvanatthaṃ "sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā"ti vuttaṃ. Kusalā 
        hi dhammā siyā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā, siyā adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya 
        sampayuttā. Akusalā dhammā siyā sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā, siyā dukkhāya vedanāya 
        sampayuttā, 
        siyā 
        sampayogārahāti. 
        adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā, tathā abyākatā 
        Evaṃ 
        kusalādidhammānaṃ 
        paccekaṃ 
        vedanābhedena 
        vibhāgadassanatthaṃ kusalattikānantaraṃ vedanāttikaṃ vatvā idāni sukhasampayuttādīnaṃ 
        paccekaṃ vipākādibhedabhinnataṃ dassetuṃ vedanāttikānantaraṃ vipākattiko vutto. 
        Sukhasampayuttā hi dhammā siyā vipākā, siyā vipākadhammadhammā, siyā 
        nevavipākanavipākadhammadhammā. 
        Tathā 
        adukkhamasukhasampayuttā, 
        dukkhasampayuttā pana siyā vipākā, siyā vipākadhammadhammāti iminā nayena 
        avasesattikānaṃ dukānañca tassa tassa anantaravacane payojanaṃ vibhāvetabbaṃ. (Anu-ṭ. 
        I. 40-1.). 
        26 
    
    33. extra notes
    
        -   avipākasukhavedanā is always javana
        -   nyāya bindu (book) and in MN-a: a lady taken bya lion, a sensual person, and a monk
            -   for the lion, food, for the sensual person, an object of pleasure, for the yogi, asubha
                -   the asubha perception applies at any time, but not the others, so should be considered the true characteristic
    
    34. notes
    
        -   pleasant object
            -   seen / considered as pleasant
                -   felt as pleasant
                -   felt as unpleasant
                -   felt as neutral
            -   seen as unpleasant
                -   felt as pleasant
                -   felt as unpleasant
                -   felt as neutral
            -   seen as neutral
                -   felt as pleasant
                -   felt as unpleasant
                -   felt as neutral
        -   kamma is decided based on the object's nature, not the mental content
        -   vipākacittas experience the object according to its nature
            -   this shows that the object has an objective desirable / undesirable nature
        -   body in general iṭṭha, but in parts aniṭṭha
            -   bhante: iṭṭha quality is of the compound, arrangement, configuration, not individually; like a compound has qualities that an element by itself doesnt have
            -   buddha's relic are iṭṭha or aniṭṭha?
            -   beautiful person, but with body parts in different location, not beautiful anymore
            -   there must be some ultimate attribute in the way they are configures, because it decides whether the vipāka is kusala or akusala vipāka

2.  notes

    -   wisdom that takes an ittha object as anittha or majjhatta is still wisdom; doesn't mean wisdom can only see the ittha as ittha (seeing objects according to their real nature)
    -   deeper understanding of moha is not bearing the object
    -   taste of the mental content is necessary to explain the araht taking ittha as anittha without domanassa

<a id="orgcf6cb85"></a>

## 3. Vipākattika


<a id="orgaad403a"></a>

### Pāḷi

-   vipākadhammā
-   vipākadhammadhammā
-   nevanipākanavipākadhammadhammā


<a id="org035d973"></a>

### meaning

[

-   **vipākadhammā**: resultant dhammas
    -   note: **kammajarūpas are not included in vipāka** (*:q: why?*)
-   **vipākadhammadhammā**: the dhamma that possesses the nature / capacity of producing vipāka &#x2013; lokiya kusala and akusala cittuppādas
    -   paṭṭhāna (kammapaccaya): only cetanā has the capacity to produce vipāka, but here the whole cittuppāda is taken
    -   paṭiccasamuppāda (kammabhava): cetanā or cittuppāda does not include lokuttara magga; here it is included
-   **nevanipākanavipākadhammadhammā**: neither

]

1.  vipāka (my class notes)

    1.  2 types
    
        -   phala
        -   pakkaphala (ripened)
            -   pāka:
                -   pāka
                -   visiṭṭha pāka (vipāka)
    
    2.  hetu vs phala
    
        -   normally, different realities
        -   in pakkaphala: hetu ripens as phala; as if there were 2 stages (kammaṁ vipacati)
            -   kammaṁ vipākaṁ hoti (becomes): manner of saying, in truth, they are different realities
    
    3.  lakkhaṇādi
    
        -   lakkhaṇa:
            -   nirussāhasantabhāvalakkhaṇaṁ (and kammassakatattā)
                -   nirussāha (na ussāha): effortless: not produced by effort and are produced by past kamma
                    -   ussāha / byāpāra / vyāpāra (effort): not vīriya or cetanā; here, pavatti ākāra (the way the mind exists); differs in intensity between javanas (stronger) and others (weaker)
                        -   vip > Ma > J
                        -   vip > Pa > vip > Vo > J > vip
                        -   cetanā has an important connection here
                    -   saussāha / sabyāpāra: effortful; kamma- and kiriya- cittas
                    -   payoga:
                        -   cittābhisaṅkhāra payoga
                        -   sammā / micchā payoga
                        -   note:
                            -   cittas other than vipākas can be made to arise by effort, but not vipākas
                            -   (*:q: vipākas being nirussāha refer to them not having effort or not arising due to effort? or both?*)
                            -   payogasampatti / vipatti: give the chance to ku / aku vipākas to arise
                            -   in this case, this effort is necessary for the arising of the vipāka: vipāka just gives the opportunity for it to arise, not produce it;
                                -   kammassakatattā: vipāka arises due to the kamma, not the payoga
                -   santa: "peaceful" for not having ussāha
                -   phalacitta: special case, produced by magga, but also has effort, so it arises due to effort
        -   rasa:
        -   paccupaṭṭhāna:
        -   padaṭṭhāna

2.  pdf notes

    1.  The vedanāttika is constituted with three padas
    
        -   (1). Vipākā dhammā – non-self-realities which are the ripen stage of kamma, which have effortless and peaceful nature.
        -   (2). Vipākadhammadhammā (vipkākadhammā dhammā)1 – non-self-realities that have a ripening nature and are endowed with effort to produce vipāka.
        -   (3). Adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā – non-self-realities that neither have a peaceful and effortless vipāka nature nor have effort to produce vipāka.
    
    2.  vipāka and vipākadhamma &#x2013; meaning
    
        1.  vipāka &#x2013; sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇaṇā
        
            vipāka: vipāka citta cetasikas: those that arise as a result of past kamma
            
            vipākadhamma: kusalākusala cittacetasika: those that produce vipāka cittacetasikas
            
            pṭn-a:
            
            -   vipāka: nirussāhasantabhāvā vipākā: nature without effort and peaceful
            
            ussāha / byāpāra: effort or active striving; the “manner” (pavatti ākāra) found in action producing minds (kiriyamaya cittuppāda), that is, in wholesome, unwholesome and javana3 consciousness; (involvement/engagement/activity); not the same as the mental factor of viriya (energy)
            
            nirussāha / santabhāva: without such effort
            
            -   both together are called vipākabhāva
            
            Therefore, the intrinsic nature of vipāka (vipākaṭṭha) is being without effort (nirussāhaṭṭha) and being peaceful (santaṭṭha).
        
        2.  Explanation from the Mūlaṭīkā
        
            The Mūlaṭīkā explains the effortlessness and peacefulness of vipāka as follows: 
            
            1Atthasālinī Atthayojanā. 76. 
            
            2Nirussāhasantabhāvena nirussāhasantabhāvāya upakārako vipākadhammo vipākapaccayo. (Pp-a. 349.). 
            
            3Kiriyāmaya minds are used in many places to refer to javana minds. However, in expressions such as “kiriyamayacittasamuṭṭhānavāyodhātu,” it appears that manodvārāvajjana is also included. 
            1 
            
            So hi vipākānaṃ payogena asādhetabbatāya payogena aññathā vā sesapaccayesu siddhesu 
            kammassa katattāyeva siddhito nirussāho santabhāvo hoti, na kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo, 
            tathāsantasabhāvato yeva bhavaṅgādayo duviññeyyā. 
            
            Let us analyze this passage.  
            
            According to this passage, vipāka is without effort and peaceful for two reasons: it should not be produced by effort, and it arises even without present effort. 
            There are two types of payoga in this passage.  
            
            (1). Cittābhisaṅkhāra payoga – This means mental construction or mental preparation, 
            such as thinking: “I should give alms,” “I should kill my enemies,” “I should eat,” “I 
            should do this or that,” or even, “What cannot be done by a person with desire? 
            Therefore, I should fulfill the perfections through my desire.” In this way, the mind is 
            intentionally focused and shaped toward action. This mental construction is called 
            abhisaṅkhāra, and that itself is called payoga. 
            (2). Sammā or micchā payoga – Some past wholesome kamma ripens and gives results 
            when success of means (payoga sampatti) is present – this is called sammā payoga or 
            correct application of effort. 
            Some past unwholesome kamma ripens and gives results when failure of means is 
            present (payoga vipatti) – this is called micchā payoga or wrong application of effort. 
            Here, sammā or micchā payoga means the kind of supporting condition that becomes a 
            root cause for the arising of the result of kamma (kamma-phaluppatti-mūlahetubhūto 
            payogo). 
            
            Therefore, the nature of vipāka (vipākabhāva) – its effortlessness and peacefulness — should be understood as follows: 
            (1). “Payogena asādhetabbatā” – Vipāka is not to be produced or created by mental effort 
            or construction. Action-producing minds (kiriyāmaya cittas) arise through mental construction, such as intentional striving and planning. But vipāka minds are not things that should be intentionally created or brought into being. 
            (2). “Payogena aññathā sesapaccayesu siddhesu kammassa katattāyeva siddhibhāvo” – 
            Even without correct or wrong application of effort, when the other necessary 
            supporting conditions are present, vipāka arises simply because the kamma was done 
            in the past. This, too, is called its effortlessness and peacefulness. 
            Because the Buddha taught that some past wholesome and unwholesome kamma ripens 
            when success or means or failure of means is present, someone might think that vipāka 
            arises because of that sammā or micchā payoga. However, even without such payoga, 
            when other supporting factors such as gati sampatti (success of destination), gati vipatti 
            (failure of destination), upadhi sampatti (success of appearance), upadhi vipatti (failure 
            of appearance) and other necessary conditions such as nāma rūpa are present, vipāka 
            arises simply because the past kamma was performed.  
            Therefore, vipāka does not arise from a present effort. Its effortlessness and peacefulness mean: 
            It is not to be created by mental construction, and it arises because of past kamma, even without 
            the application of correct or wrong effort. 
            Vipāka states should not be produced by mental construction (cittābhisaṅkhāra). 
            2 
            Yet, vipāka can appear when the application of correct or wrong effort is present, and it can 
            also appear when they are not present. Therefore, whether sammā payoga or micchā payoga 
            exists or not, vipāka arises because the kamma was done in the past. 
            This is explained in the Anuṭīkā as follows: 
            Kammassa katattāyeva payoge sati asatipi ti vuttamatthaṃ avadhāraṇena dassento vipākānaṃ 
            nirussāhattaṃ pākaṭaṃ karoti. 
            The minds of those who have attained jhāna4 and of Arahants are also peaceful. This 
            peacefulness is due to the defilements being suppressed or destroyed in their mental continuum. 
            But the peacefulness of vipāka described here is not due to the suppression or destruction of 
            defilements. It is peaceful because it should not be produced by mental construction, and it 
            arises simply because past kamma was done. That is why the Mūlaṭīkā says: “na 
            kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo.”5 
            Furthermore, because vipāka is so peaceful in nature, even states such as bhavaṅga are difficult 
            to be known clearly: “tathāsantasabhāvato yeva bhavaṅgādayo duviññeyyā.” 
            
            cdvml (paṭṭhāna prakaraṇa sannaya) on vipākacittas:
            
            -   ku, aku, ki cittacetasikas possess ussāha in carrying out their own functions, such as perceiving and knowing
            -   ussāha here is not vīriya but a high active force of the functional speed that belongs to each of these states
            -   cittacetasikas with low functional speed are not capable of performing special actions such as giving and other deliberate deeds
            -   Vipāka mind and mental factors, on the other hand, are states that arise without effort, due to past wholesome and unwholesome kamma. Therefore, their functional speed in activities such as perceiving and knowing is very weak. Because they do not arise as acts of performing special actions such as giving, they do not require the strong functional speed that wholesome, unwholesome and functional mind and mental factors possess.
            -   since these vipāka mind and mental factors are without effort, they are also peaceful. The peacefulness of mind and mental factors is of two kinds:
            -   (1). Peacefulness due to the removal of defilements. The minds of those who have attained jhāna and of the Noble Ones are peaceful because defilements have been removed. Especially, the mind of an Arahant is peaceful for this reason.
            -   (2). Peacefulness due to slowness of functional speed. Mind and mental factors can also be peaceful because they function slowly. The peacefulness of vipāka minds is not due to the removal of defilements, but due to their slowness of functional speed.
            
            4 Na kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo yathā taṃ santānesu jhānasamāpattīsūti adhippāyo. (Anu-ṭ. III. 233.). 
            
            5 Nirussāhasantabhāvenāti etena saussāhehi vipākadhammadhammehi kusalākusalehi sārammaṇādibhāvena sadisavipākabhāvaṃ dasseti. So hi vipākānaṃ payogena asādhetabbatāya payogena aññathā vā sesapaccayesu siddhesu kammassa kaṭattāyeva siddhito nirussāho santabhāvo hoti, na kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo&#x2026; (Ml-ṭ. III. 172.). 
            
            From all the above, what becomes clear is this: 
            Wholesome and unwholesome kamma must be produced through cittābhisaṅkhāra payoga 
            (effort of mental construction), whereas vipāka should not be produced through 
            cittābhisaṅkhāra payoga. 
            However, a question now arises: What causes vipāka minds to arise? What is the force that 
            produces them? 
            The Dhamma states that they arise because kamma was done in the past. Therefore, now we 
            must explain the phrase: “kammassa katattā upacitatta” — “because kamma was done and 
            accumulated.” 
            For this purpose, first the term “vipākadhammadhammā” must be explained. 
            Sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇaṇā of the term “vipākadhamma”  
            “Vipākadhamma” refers to realities (states) whose nature is to produce vipāka in the future – 
            “vipākajanakaṭṭhena vipākasabhāvā, vipākapakatikā.”6 They are wholesome and 
            unwholesome states. In another way of speaking, they are states whose nature is to ripen – 
            “vipākapakatikāti vipaccanapakatikā.”7 
            Among them, wholesome states produce desirable vipāka in the future, and unwholesome 
            states produce undesirable vipāka in the future. 
            The natural meaning of vipākadhamma states is “having effort and involvement, and having 
            the nature of producing vipāka” – “saussāha-sabyāpāra-savipākaṭṭho.” 
            As explained above, “ussāha” refers to the manner found in action-producing mind-moments. 
            
            This itself is also called “byāpāra” (involvement/activity). 
            Being endowed with effort is meant by “saussāhaṭṭha,” and being endowed with activity is 
            meant by “sabyāpāraṭṭha”
            
            There is a group of states that possess this effort and activity. These states also have the nature of producing vipāka (“savipākaṭṭha”). Therefore, mind and mental factors that, with effort and activity, produce vipāka are meant by the term vipākadhamma. 
            
            Sahussaṭṭha, sabyāpāraṭṭha and savipākaṭṭha 
            Now we shall study these three properties of vipākadhamma in detail.  
            
            -   **sabyāpāraṭṭhena vipākadhammā**
                -   vsm-ṭ: being endowed with effort, being suitable (yogya) for producing vipāka
                    -   because a state exists in a certain way, vipāka can arise from it
                        -   sabyāpāratoti saīhato. tena yathā pavattamānāyassā vipākena bhavitabbaṃ, tathā pavattiṃ vadanto vipākuppādanayogyatamāha
                    -   this refers to the “manner” found in vipākadhammas
                -   Having bodily and verbal effort in bodily and verbal acts.9 Good actions
            
            such as giving and bad actions such as killing are performed with wholesome 
            and unwholesome mentalities.  
            This second characteristic is also found in the eighteen functional javana 
            consciousnesses and the four fruition consciousnesseses as well. 
            
            6 Vipākadhammadhammāti vipākasabhāvadhammā. Yathā jātijarāsabhāvā jātijarāpakatikā sattā jātidhammā jarādhammāti vuccanti evaṃ vipākajanakaṭṭhena vipākasabhāvā vipākapakatikā dhammāti attho. (Dhs-a. 84.). 
            
            7 Ml-ṭ. I. 38. 
            
            -   **saussāhaṭṭhena vipākadhammā** 
                -   having the capacity 10 of producing vipāka
                -   having javana effort 11 – meaning being endowed with the speed of javana
                    this second characteristic is also found in the eighteen functional javana consciousnesses and the four fruition consciousnesses
            -   **savipākaṭṭhena vipākadhammā** 
                -   note: savipāka can be explained as an abhidhānattha meaning of the term “vipākadhamma,” but explaining it as a intrinsic natue (sabhāvattha) helps one gain a clearer understanding of vipākadhamma states
                -   **having the nature of producing vipāka (vipākajanakaṭṭha)**
                    -   the nature of producing vipāka when other conditions come together.12 because of this nature of producing vipāka, the dhammasaṅgaṇī commentary describes vipākadhamma states as “vipākasabhāvā” and “vipākapakatikā.” this means they are states whose nature is to ripen – “vipākapakatikāti vipaccanapakatikā.”
                -   note: for a state to be a vipākadhamma, it is not necessary that it must actually produce vipāka. it is enough that it has the nature or capacity to produce vipāka. therefore, even wholesome higher-knowledge consciousnesses (kusala abhiññā cittuppāda) and ahosikamma cittuppāda are called vipākadhamma, even though they do not actually produce any vipāka. thus, the term “vipākajanakaṭṭha” in the commentary means the nature of being able to produce vipāka, not the actual act of producing vipāka.
                    -   **the capacity to give rise to vipāka “vipākupādanasamatthaṭṭha”**.13
                -   in brief, the intrinsic characteristics of vipāka-dhamma are: having effort aimed at producing vipāka and having the capacity to produce vipāka

            in some places: saussāha and sabyāpāra are together the capacity to produce results k– **saussāhanti phaluppādanasamatthatāvasena sabyāpāraṃ** 14

            Also, being endowed with javana speed is a characteristic of vipākadhamma.15 
            
            The saussāha nature of vipāka-dhamma can be briefly described as: Effort in bodily and verbal construction (kāyāṅga and vācāṅga abhisaṅkhāra), and effort in actions that will ripen in the future.16 
            
            What has been explained above refers to the saussāha and sabyāpāra nature of wholesome and unwholesome minds. 
            
            However, functional minds (kiriya citta) also have saussāha and sabyāpāra natures. These two natures found in kamma cittas and kiriya cittas should be clearly distinguished. 
            Now, let us discuss these two types of sabyāpāra (saussāha) natures.  
            
            (Note: Although all functional minds have saussāha and sabyāpāra natures, from this point onward, only functional javana minds excluding the two adverting minds (āvajjana) are considered as saussāha-sabyāpāra minds.)17 
            
            Nature of “sabyāpāra,” which is also “saussāha” 
            As explained above, “ussāha” or “byāpāra” refers to the manner found in action-producing 
            mind-moments. It is not the same as viriya (the mental factor of energy). 
            There are two forms of being sahussāha and sabyāpāra: 
            
            -   **savipākatāvisiṭṭha sabyāpārasahussāhatā** 18 
                -   having activity and effort that are distinguished by the ability to produce vipāka, or by the nature of producing vipāka.  Let us call this “savipāka sabyāpāra nature”
            -   **avipākatāvisiṭṭha sabyāpārasahussāhatā** 19
                -   having activity and effort that are distinguished by the inability to produce vipāka, or by the nature of not producing vipāka.  Let us call this avipāka sabyāpāra nature
            
            -   footnotes
                -   15 Vipaccanasabhāvatā ca anupacchinnāvijjātaṇhāmānasantāne sabyāpāratā. (Ml-ṭ. I. 38.). Ettha ca "sabyāpāratā"ti etena āvajjanadvayaṃ vipākañca nivatteti, "anupacchinnāvijjātaṇhāmāne"ti iminā avasiṭṭhaṃ kiriyaṃ nivatteti. (Anu-ṭ. I. 41.).
                -   16 Nirussāhasantabhāvo nāma kusalākusalānaṃ viya kāyaṅgavācaṅgābhisaṅkharaṇakiccesu ca āyatiṃ vipaccanakiccesu ca nirussāhabhāvena santabhāvo. Na kilesu pasantabhāvena. (Adp. 261.). The Anudīpanī passage quoted above explains the nirussāha (effortless) and santa (peaceful) nature of vipāka. In accordance with that, the meaning of “saussāha bhāva” should be understood.

II. 159), all functional minds are generally described as having the sabyāpāra nature. Therefore, it also seems that pañcadvārāvajjana is also considered to be sabyāpāra.
                -   18 Kiriyābyākatāya savipākato savipākatāvisiṭṭhasabyāpārasaussāhato vāti. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159.). 6
            
            There is a reason for savipāka sabyāpāra nature. This becomes clear from the definition of vipākadhamma given in the Mūlaṭīkā: 
            **Vipaccanasabhāvatā ca anupacchinnāvijjātaṇhāmānasantāne sabyāpāratā** 20 
            According to this, the cause of savipāka sabyāpāra nature is that the effortful consciousness arising in a mental continuum where ignorance (avijjā), craving (taṇhā), and conceit (māna) have not been destroyed. 
            
            The nature of sabyāpā that is supported by undestroyed avijjā, taṇhā, and māna is stronger and more powerful than the sabyāpāra nature that does not receive such support. 
            Therefore, even though the sabyāpāra nature may exist, it becomes capable of producing 
            vipāka only when it is supported by undestroyed defilements. 
            Sabyāpara nature that does not receive this support is not capable of producing vipāka. 
            The Anuṭīkā states: 
            Ussāhoti ca kiriyamayacittuppādassa pavattiākāro veditabbo, yo byāpāroti ca vuccati, na 
            vīriyussāho. svāyaṃ yathā asamugghātitānusāyānaṃ kiriyamayacittuppādesu sātisayo labbhati, 
            na tathā niranusāyānaṃ. tato eva te santasabhāvā vipākupādānabyāpārarahitāva honti.21 
            According to this passage, in beings who have not destroyed their latent tendencies (anusaya), 
            this kind of effort is found strongly in their kiriyamayacittuppāda. 
            In beings who are without anusaya, this kind of strong effort is not found to the same degree. 
            Therefore, kiriyamayacittuppāda of beings without anusaya are peaceful in nature and are 
            without the activity (effort) that produces vipāka. 
            But kiriyamayacittuppāda of beings who still have anusaya do have effort that can produce 
            vipāka. 
            This extraordinary activity that is not found in kriyā javana minds but is found only in 
            wholesome and unwholesome minds is called “high speed” (“adhika vega”). This speed is the 
            “manner of existence” (pavatti ākāra) found in wholesome and unwholesome mental clusters. 
            It is also called: the capacity to produce vipāka (sāmatthi), power (satti), and speed of kamma 
            momentum (kamma vega). It is also called the “seed of resultants” (“vipāka bīja”).22 
            Because kamma intention (cetanā) possesses this power, it is like a state that is “sagabbha” – 
            that is, pregnant with the fruit called vipāka.23 
            19 Kiriyābyākatā avipākato, avipākatāvisiṭṭhasabyāpārasaussāhato vāti evaṃ kusalākusalāya vuttavipallāsena. 
            (Vsm-ṭ. II. 160.). 
            20 Ml-ṭ. I. 38. 
            21 Anu-ṭ. III. 232-3. 
            22 Sāpi hi sahajātānaṃ saṃvidahanasaṅkhātena kammabhāvena pavattattā kammaṃ eva nāma hotīti. Yasmā 
            attano pavattākārasaṅkhātassa vipākabījassa nidahanaṃnāma, na tāvadeva bījakiccaṃ sampādeti. (Prd. 396.). 
            23 Savipākā ca sagabbhā viya oḷārikāti tabbipakkhato avipākā sukhumāti vuttā. (Ml-ṭ. II. 12.). 
            7 
            This same powerful cetanā is called “saṅkhāra” in the teaching of Dependent Origination. It 
            is javana cetanā with the nature of sabyāpāra supported by avijjā. 
            Therefore, the Tathāgata taught: “avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā.” 
            Accordingly, in the teaching of Dependent Origination, the “high speed” (savipāka-sabyāpāra
            bhāva) of cetanā should be understood as happening because of avijjā. 
            According to the Paṭṭhāna, this strong activity or ability to produce vipāka that exists in 
            wholesome and unwholesome cittuppādas actually resides in their cetanā. 
            That is the “manner” found in wholesome and unwholesome cetanā. Therefore, wholesome 
            and unwholesome cetanā are called “nānakkhaṇika kamma paccaya.” 
            Vipāka minds arise because of this kammic speed. This should be understood from statements 
            such as: “vipāko kammavegakkhittattā &#x2026;”24 More precisely, vipāka arises because of the speed 
            of past kamma. Vipāka states are not produced by cittābhisaṅkhāra. For this reason, vipāka 
            minds are without effort (nirussāha) and are peaceful (santa).25 
            Although kriyā javana minds arise through mental construction (cittābhisaṅkhāra), they are 
            peaceful because defilements such as avijjā have been destroyed in their mental continuum. 
            Moreover, vipāka states that arise through the speed of kamma are like reflections of kamma 
            (kamma paṭibimba sadisa). 
            Therefore, the Visuddhimagga says: 
            Kammavegakkhittā hi kammapaṭibimbabhūtā ca kāyakammādibyāpāravirahato nirussāhā 
            vipākā.26 
            Mental factors such as alobha found in vipāka minds do not have sabyāpāra nature in the way 
            that alobha and other mental factors in wholesome minds do.27 
            From this, two more characteristics of vipāka can be stated: 
            (1). Kammavegakkhittaṭṭha – being driven or propelled by the speed of kamma, meaning 
            being produced by the force of kamma. 
            (2). Kammapaṭibimbaṭṭha – being like a reflection of kamma.

            This point is explained by the Venerable Rerukāne Candawimala Mahā Thera in the Paṭṭhāna 
            Pakaraṇa Sannaya under the lesson on maggappaccaya as follows: 
            Vipāka minds arise without effort and by the force of the kamma. Therefore, the cetanā associated 
            with vipāka minds does not become a nānakkhaṇika kamma paccaya. 
            Now, let us examine the speed of past kamma.  
        
        3.  The Phrase “(purāṇassa) kammassa katattā upacitatta” and the speed of kamma
        
            Past kamma – that is, wholesome and unwholesome cetanā, or wholesome and unwholesome 
            cittuppāda in which cetanā is the chief factor – is called “old kamma” (purāṇa kamma). 
            Because old kamma has been done, vipāka arises. This idea is expressed in passages such as: 
            “Kammassa katattā upacitattā uppajjati cakkhuviññāṇaṃ.” 
            Here, “kammassa katattā” means “because kamma has been done.” 
            The phrase “kammassa upacitattā” conveys several meanings: 
            (1). First, it means that kamma has been built up strengthening it.28 
            That is, it has been made powerful in such a way that it can block the result of another 
            kamma and make room for its own vipāka to arise.29
            
            -   note: if it gave result, it overcame other kammas in giving result
            
            (2). Second, it means that kamma has been done in a way it builds the cycle of death and 
            rebirth (cuti and paṭisandhi), allowing the process to grow and continue.30 
            
            -   note: maggacetanā is maggassa katattā bhāvitattā, not upacitattā
                -   upacita accumulated: used for lokiya, not lokuttara
            
            Thus, “kammassa upacitattā” means that kamma has been performed in a way that is capable 
            of producing vipāka. 
            
            What does “capable of producing vipāka” mean? This should be understood in accordance with the Buddha’s teachings. 
            
            In many places, the Tathāgata taught that kamma follows the person who performs it, that both good and bad deeds take time to ripen, and that a being experiences suffering until bad kamma is exhausted. 
            
            For example: 
            **na hi pāpaṃ kataṃ kammaṃ sajju khīraṃva muccati**
            **ḍahantaṃ bālamanveti, bhasmacchannova pāvako**
            
            **pāpopi passati bhadraṃ, yāva pāpaṃ na paccati,
            yadā ca paccati pāpaṃ, atha pāpo pāpāni passati.** 
            
            28 Ml-ṭ. I. 303. 
            
            29 Upacitattāti yathā aññassa vipākaṃ paṭibāhitvā attano vipākābhimukhaṃ hoti tathā vaḍḍhitattā. (Ml-ṭ.. I. 124.). "Upacitattā"ti padaṃ saṅgayhati "asādhāraṇakammapaccayavasenā"ti vuttattā. "upacitattāti laddhāsevanattā"ti keci vadanti, taṃ paṭhamajavanassa na yujjati anāsevanattā. Tathā ca sati tassa vipākadānameva na siyāti tato aññathā atthaṃ dassento "yathā"tiādimāha. Tattha vipākābhimukhanti vipākadānābhimukhaṃ katokāsaṃ. Katokāsatā ca anādimhi saṃsāre anekesaṃ kammānaṃ katānaṃ atthitāya parassa paṭibāhanena hotīti "aññassa vipākaṃpaṭibāhitvā"tiādi vuttaṃ. Vaḍḍhitatā ca sakammassa baladānasamatthatāvasena attano kāraṇehi abhisaṅkhatatā. (Anu-ṭ. I. 131-2.). 
            
            30 Lokuttaravipākampi kusalasadisattā kusalānugatikameva katvā bhājitaṃ. Yasmā pana tebhūmakakusalaṃ cutipaṭisandhivasena vaṭṭaṃ ācināti vaḍḍheti, tasmā tattha katattā upacitattāti vuttaṃ. Lokuttaraṃ pana tena ācitampi apacināti, sayampi cutipaṭisandhivasena na ācināti, tenettha ‘Katattā upacitattā’ti avatvā katattā bhāvitattāti vuttaṃ. (Dhs-a 328)
            
            **Bhadropi passati pāpaṃ, yāva bhadraṃ na paccati,
            Yadā ca paccati bhadraṃ, atha bhadro bhadrāni passati**
            
            **Na tāva kālaṃ karoti – yāva na taṃ pāpaṃ byantī hoti**
            
            These verses illustrate this point. 
        
        4.  Views of the Kassapika and Other Schools, and the Theravāda View
        
            Kassapika (Suvarṣa Kāśyapīya) school: believed that past kamma accumulates and remains stored, and that it exists separate from consciousness (citta vippayutta). They called this “kammūpacaya”.
            
            -   they consider their founder V. Mahakassapa
            -   sarvastivādins: all past and future is stored
            
            Andhaka and Sammītiya schools: held similar views
            
            Theravādins: rejected it &#x2013; kamma in those teachings is the capacity of vipākadhamma to produce results, not a stored past kamma itself
            
            -   Kathāvatthu Anuṭīkā: \*Yaṃ pana ‘na tāva kālaṃ karoti, yāva na taṃ pāpaṃ byantī hotī’ti sutte vuttaṃ, taṃ yasmiñca santāne kammaṃ katūpacitaṃ, tattha tenāhitaṃ taṃ phaluppādanasamatthataṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ, na atītassa kammassa dharamānasabhāvattā.31 
                According to this, when the Tathāgata spoke of “until bad kamma is exhausted,” “good and evil that ripen,” or “evil that follows like fire covered by ashes,” he was referring to this: In a mental continuum where kamma has been done and built up (katūpacita), the kamma has placed there a capacity or power to produce its result – that is, kamma power (kamma satti). Therefore, one should not think that past kamma is stored somewhere as a separate, existing entitiy. Rather, what is “stored” is the capacity or power of kamma to produce vipāka.
            
            -   note: jhānavega: comm.: being remains rupa arises as long as jhānavega is not exhausted; it's the kammavega of the 5th jhānacitta
                -   jnkbvs: kammavega associates the mental and material stream
                    -   bhante: resembles the higg's field
                        -   see also higg's boson
            
            What is this “power to produce vipāka”? It is nothing other than: 
            (1). The kamma power in vipākadhamma, 
            (2). Activity (byāpāra), 
            (3). Effort (ussāha), 
            (4). The manner of existence (pavatti ākāra) of cetanā, and 
            (5). The “seed of vipāka” (vipāka bīja). 
            
            **Therefore, whenever the Dhamma says “kammassa katattā upacitatta” – meaning “because kamma was done and built up (so as to be capable of producing vipāka)” – it should be understood that kamma cetanā has placed in the mental continuum the power or capacity to 
            produce its result**
            
            Thus, the “being done” (katatta) and “being built up” (upacitatta) of kamma mean that kamma has deposited in the continuum its own ability or power to produce vipāka. 
            
            For this reason, the Paramatthadīpanī says: 
            \*Cetanā nāma pavattamānā dve kiccāni sādhenti sahajātānaṃ saṃvidhānañca āyatiṃ vipākapātubhāvatthāya tasmiṃ santāne attano pavattākārasaṅkhātabījanidhānañcāti. Tattha 
            
            31 Anu-ṭ. III. 97. 
            
            saṃvidhānakiccaṃ hoti. Bījanidhānakiccaṃ pana sabbacetanāsādhāraṇaṃ kusalākusalabhāvappattāya eva cetanāya hotī’ti vuttaṃ sahajātācetanātiādi.32 
            
            -   organizing coexisting mentalities
            -   storing the bīja in that mind stream in order to produce vipāka in the future
            
            This means, cetanā performs two functions: 
            (1). It organizes the associated mental states, and 
            (2). It deposits in the mental continuum a “seed” which is its manner of existence (pavatti ākāra), so that vipāka may arise in the future. 
            
            The first function belongs to all cetanā. The second function – depositing the seed – belongs only to cetanā that is wholesome or unwholesome. 
            Because vipāka arises from the kamma speed that has been deposited in the mental continuum by past kamma, vipāka states are without effort (nirussāha). They are not produced by cittābhisaṅkhāra (mental construction). For this reason, vipāka states do not themselves have activity aimed at producing further vipāka. Even in the case of the functional minds of Arahants, where effort is still present, if there is no activity aimed at producing vipāka, then it is obvious that vipāka minds, which are without effort, also do not have such activity. Therefore, vipāka minds that arise from the speed of past kamma do not possess the power of nānakkhaṇika kamma paccaya. It is important to discuss a few more points about this kamma speed that has been explained above.
            
            32 Cetanānāma pavattamānā dve kiccāni sādheti sahajātānaṃ saṃvidhānañca āyatiṃ vipākapātu bhāvatthāya 
            tasmiṃ santāne attano pavattā kārasaṅkhāta bījanidhānañcāti. Tattha saṃvidhānakiccaṃ sabbacetanā 
            sādhāraṇaṃ hoti. Bījanidhānakiccaṃ pana kusalākusalabhāvappattāyaeva cetanāya hotīti vuttaṃ 
            sahajātācetanātiādi. Tattha sahajātācetanāti antamaso pañcaviññāṇa sahajātāpi cetanā. Sāpi hi sahajātānaṃ 
            saṃvidahanasaṅkhātena 
            kammabhāvena 
            pavattattā 
            kammaṃevanāma 
            hotīti. 
            Yasmā 
            attano 
            pavattākārasaṅkhātassa vipākabījassa nidahanaṃnāma, na tāvadeva bījakiccaṃ sampādeti. Ambabījādīni viya 
            pariṇatabhāvaṃ patvā khaṇantareeva sampādetīti vuttaṃ nānākkhaṇikā cetanātiādi. (Prd. 395-6.).
