4.  pdf

    -   (1). Vipākā dhammā – non-self-realities which are the ripen stage of kamma, which have effortless and peaceful nature.
    -   (2). Vipākadhammadhammā (vipkākadhammā dhammā)1 – non-self realities that have a ripening nature and are endowed with effort to produce vipāka.
    -   (3). Adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā – non-self realities that neither have a peaceful and effortless vipāka nature nor have effort to produce vipāka.

5.  vipāka

    1.  Sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇaṇā of the term “vipāka”
    
        -   vipāka: cittacetasikas that arise as a result of past kamma
        -   vipākadhamma: ku / aku cittacetasikas that produce vipāka cittacetasikas
        
        paṭṭhāna-a: nirussāhasantabhāvā vipākā): nature without effort and peaceful 
        s- ussāha: effort, active striving: "manner" (pavatti ākāra) found in action producing minds (kiriyamaya cittuppāda), that is, in wholesome, unwholesome and functional javana3 consciousness. This is 
        
        also called byāpāra (involvement/engagement/activity). This is not the same as the mental factor of viriya (energy). This is not the same as viriya (energy as a mental factor). 
        That which does not have this kind of effort is called “nirussāha.” This refers to a nature that is without effort. This is also called “santabhāva” (peaceful nature/peacefulness/tranquility). These two characteristics of nirussāha and santabhāva together are called “vipākabhāva.” 
        
        Therefore, the intrinsic nature of vipāka (vipākaṭṭha) is being without effort 
        (nirussāhaṭṭha) and being peaceful (santaṭṭha). Explanation from the Mūlaṭīkā 
        The Mūlaṭīkā explains the effortlessness and peacefulness of vipāka as 
        follows:
        
        So hi vipākānaṃ payogena asādhetabbatāya payogena aññathā vā 
        sesapaccayesu siddhesu kammassa katattāyeva siddhito nirussāho santabhāvo 
        hoti, na kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo, tathāsantasabhāvato yeva bhavaṅgādayo 
        duviññeyyā. 
        Let us analyze this passage.  
        According to this passage, vipāka is without effort and peaceful for two 
        reasons: it should not be produced by effort, and it arises even without present 
        effort. 
        There are two types of payoga in this passage.  
        (1). Cittābhisaṅkhāra payoga – This means mental construction or mental 
        preparation, such as thinking: “I should give alms,” “I should kill my 
        enemies,” “I should eat,” “I should do this or that,” or even, “What 
        cannot be done by a person with desire? Therefore, I should fulfill the 
        perfections through my desire.” In this way, the mind is intentionally 
        focused and shaped toward action. This mental construction is called 
        abhisaṅkhāra, and that itself is called payoga. 
        (2). Sammā or micchā payoga – Some past wholesome kamma ripens and 
        gives results when success of means (payoga sampatti) is present – this 
        is called sammā payoga or correct application of effort. 
        Some past unwholesome kamma ripens and gives results when failure 
        of means is present (payoga vipatti) – this is called micchā payoga or 
        wrong application of effort. 
        2 
        Here, sammā or micchā payoga means the kind of supporting 
        condition that becomes a root cause for the arising of the result of 
        kamma (kamma-phaluppatti-mūlahetubhūto payogo). 
        Therefore, the nature of vipāka (vipākabhāva) – its effortlessness and 
        peacefulness — should be understood as follows: 
        (1). “Payogena asādhetabbatā” – Vipāka is not to be produced or created 
        by mental effort or construction. 
        Action-producing minds (kiriyāmaya cittas) arise through mental 
        construction, such as intentional striving and planning. But vipāka 
        minds are not things that should be intentionally created or brought 
        into being. 
        (2). “Payogena aññathā sesapaccayesu siddhesu kammassa katattāyeva 
        siddhibhāvo” – Even without correct or wrong application of effort, 
        when the other necessary supporting conditions are present, vipāka 
        arises simply because the kamma was done in the past. This, too, is 
        called its effortlessness and peacefulness. 
        Because the Buddha taught that some past wholesome and 
        unwholesome kamma ripens when success or means or failure of 
        means is present, someone might think that vipāka arises because of 
        that sammā or micchā payoga. However, even without such payoga, 
        when other supporting factors such as gati sampatti (success of 
        destination), gati vipatti (failure of destination), upadhi sampatti 
        (success of appearance), upadhi vipatti (failure of appearance) and 
        other necessary conditions such as nāma rūpa are present, vipāka 
        arises simply because the past kamma was performed.  
        Therefore, vipāka does not arise from a present effort. Its effortlessness and 
        peacefulness mean: It is not to be created by mental construction, and it arises 
        because of past kamma, even without the application of correct or wrong 
        effort. 
        Vipāka states should not be produced by mental construction 
        (cittābhisaṅkhāra). 
        Yet, vipāka can appear when the application of correct or wrong effort is 
        present, and it can also appear when they are not present. Therefore, whether 
        sammā payoga or micchā payoga exists or not, vipāka arises because the 
        kamma was done in the past. 
        This is explained in the Anuṭīkā as follows: 
        Kammassa katattāyeva payoge sati asatipi ti vuttamatthaṃ avadhāraṇena 
        dassento vipākānaṃ nirussāhattaṃ pākaṭaṃ karoti. 
        3 
        The minds of those who have attained jhāna4 and of Arahants are also 
        peaceful. This peacefulness is due to the defilements being suppressed or 
        destroyed in their mental continuum. 
        But the peacefulness of vipāka described here is not due to the suppression or 
        destruction of defilements. It is peaceful because it should not be produced by 
        mental construction, and it arises simply because past kamma was done. That 
        is why the Mūlaṭīkā says: “na kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo.”5 
        Furthermore, because vipāka is so peaceful in nature, even states such as 
        bhavaṅga are difficult to be known clearly: “tathāsantasabhāvato yeva 
        bhavaṅgādayo duviññeyyā.” 
        The Venerable Rerukāne Candawimala Mahā Thera, in his Paṭṭhāna 
        Prakaraṇa Sannaya, explains the nature of resultant consciousnesses as 
        follows: 
        Wholesome, unwholesome and functional minds and mental factors, which arise 
        in the form of performing actions such as giving (dāna) and killing (pāṇaghāta), 
        possess effort in carrying out their own functions, such as perceiving and 
        knowing. 
        Here, when it is said that they “have effort,” it should be understood that this 
        does not refer to the mental factor of energy (viriya). Rather, “effort” here 
        means the “strong” (high) active force of the functional speed that belongs to 
        each of these states. 
        Mind and mental factors that have weak or low functional speed are not capable 
        of performing special actions such as giving and other deliberate deeds. 
        Vipāka mind and mental factors, on the other hand, are states that arise without 
        effort, due to past wholesome and unwholesome kamma. Therefore, their 
        functional speed in activities such as perceiving and knowing is very weak. 
        Because they do not arise as acts of performing special actions such as giving, 
        they do not require the strong functional speed that wholesome, unwholesome 
        and functional mind and mental factors possess. 
        Since these vipāka mind and mental factors are without effort, they are also 
        peaceful. 
        The peacefulness of mind and mental factors is of two kinds: 
        4Na kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo yathā taṃ santānesu jhānasamāpattīsūti adhippāyo. (Anu-ṭ. 
        III. 233.). 
        5 Nirussāhasantabhāvenāti etena saussāhehi vipākadhammadhammehi kusalākusalehi 
        sārammaṇādibhāvena sadisavipākabhāvaṃ dasseti. So hi vipākānaṃ payogena 
        asādhetabbatāya payogena aññathā vā sesapaccayesu siddhesu kammassa kaṭattāyeva 
        siddhito nirussāho santabhāvo hoti, na kilesavūpasamasantabhāvo&#x2026; (Ml-ṭ. III. 172.). 
        4 
        (1). Peacefulness due to the removal of defilements. The minds of those who 
        have attained jhāna and of the Noble Ones are peaceful because 
        defilements have been removed. Especially, the mind of an Arahant is 
        peaceful for this reason. 
        (2). Peacefulness due to slowness of functional speed. Mind and mental 
        factors can also be peaceful because they function slowly. 
        The peacefulness of vipāka minds is not due to the removal of defilements, but 
        due to their slowness of functional speed. 
        From all the above, what becomes clear is this: 
        Wholesome and unwholesome kamma must be produced through 
        cittābhisaṅkhāra payoga (effort of mental construction), whereas vipāka 
        should not be produced through cittābhisaṅkhāra payoga. 
        However, a question now arises: What causes vipāka minds to arise? What is 
        the force that produces them? 
        The Dhamma states that they arise because kamma was done in the past. 
        Therefore, now we must explain the phrase: “kammassa katattā upacitatta” 
        — “because kamma was done and accumulated.” 
        For this purpose, first the term “vipākadhammadhammā” must be explained. 
        Sabhāvattha saṃvaṇṇaṇā of the term “vipākadhamma”  
        “Vipākadhamma” refers to realities (states) whose nature is to produce vipāka 
        in the future – “vipākajanakaṭṭhena vipākasabhāvā, vipākapakatikā.”6 They 
        are wholesome and unwholesome states. In another way of speaking, they are 
        states whose nature is to ripen – “vipākapakatikāti vipaccanapakatikā.”7 
        Among them, wholesome states produce desirable vipāka in the future, and 
        unwholesome states produce undesirable vipāka in the future. 
        The natural meaning of vipākadhamma states is “having effort and 
        involvement, and having the nature of producing vipāka” – “saussāha
        sabyāpāra-savipākaṭṭho.” 
        As explained above, “ussāha” refers to the manner found in action-producing 
        mind-moments. This itself is also called “byāpāra” (involvement/activity). 
        6Vipākadhammadhammāti vipākasabhāvadhammā. Yathā jātijarāsabhāvā jātijarāpakatikā 
        sattā jātidhammā jarādhammāti vuccanti evaṃ vipākajanakaṭṭhena vipākasabhāvā 
        vipākapakatikā dhammāti attho. (Dhs-a. 84.). 
        7Ml-ṭ. I. 38. 
        5 
        Being endowed with effort is meant by “saussāhaṭṭha,” and being endowed 
        with activity is meant by “sabyāpāraṭṭha.” 
        There is a group of states that possess this effort and activity. These states also 
        have the nature of producing vipāka (“savipākaṭṭha”). Therefore, mind and 
        mental factors that, with effort and activity, produce vipāka are meant by the 
        term vipākadhamma. 
        Sahussaṭṭha, sabyāpāraṭṭha and savipākaṭṭha 
        Now we shall study these three properties of vipākadhamma in detail.  
        (1). “Sabyāpāraṭṭhena vipākadhammā”  
        This characteristic can be explained in two ways: 
        (a). Being endowed with effort – that is, being suitable (yogya) of 
        producing vipāka. This means that because a state exists in a 
        certain way, vipāka can arise from it.8 This refers to the 
        “manner” found in vipākadhammas.  
        (b). Having bodily and verbal effort in bodily and verbal acts.9 
        Good actions such as giving and bad actions such as killing are 
        performed with wholesome and unwholesome mentalities.  
        This second characteristic is also found in the eighteen 
        functional javana consciousnesses and the four fruition 
        consciousnesseses as well. 
        (2). “Saussāhaṭṭhe vipākadhammā” 
        This characteristic can also be explained in two ways: 
        (a). Being endowed with power and capacity10 – meaning the 
        ability to produce vipāka. 
        (b). Having javana effort11 – meaning being endowed with the 
        speed of javana. 
        8Sabyāpāratoti saīhato. Tena yathā pavattamānāyassā vipākena bhavitabbaṃ, tathā pavattiṃ 
        vadanto vipākuppādanayogyatamāha. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159.). 
        9Kāyakammādibyāpārasabbhāvato vā sabyāpārato. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159.). 
        10 Saussāhatoti sasattito, tena vipākuppādanasamatthataṃ. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159.). 
        11 Javanussāhavasena saussāhato. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159.). 
        6 
        This second characteristic is also found in the eighteen 
        functional javana consciousnesses and the four fruition 
        consciousnesses. 
        (3). “Savipākaṭṭhena vipākadhammā. 
        This characteristic can also be explained in two ways: 
        [The nature of “savipāka” can be explained as an abhidhānattha 
        meaning of the term “vipākadhamma,” but explaining it as a intrinsic 
        natue (sabhāvattha) helps one gain a clearer understanding of 
        vipākadhamma states.] 
        (a). Having the nature of producing vipāka (vipākajanakaṭṭha). 
        This means the nature of producing vipāka when other 
        conditions come together.12 
        Because of this nature of producing vipāka, the 
        Dhammasaṅgaṇī Commentary describes vipākadhamma states 
        as “vipākasabhāvā” and “vipākapakatikā.” 
        This means they are states whose nature is to ripen – 
        “vipākapakatikāti vipaccanapakatikā.” 
        Here, an important point should be remembered: 
        For a state to be a vipākadhamma, it is not necessary that it 
        must actually produce vipāka. It is enough that it has the nature 
        or capacity to produce vipāka. 
        Therefore, 
        even 
        wholesome 
        higher-knowledge 
        consciousnesses (kusala abhiññā cittuppāda) and ahosikamma 
        cittuppāda are called vipākadhamma, even though they do not 
        actually produce any vipāka. 
        Thus, the term “vipākajanakaṭṭha” in the Commentary means 
        the nature of being able to produce vipāka, not the actual act of 
        producing vipāka. 
        (b). The 
        capacity 
        to 
        give 
        (“vipākupādanasamatthaṭṭha”).13 
        rise 
        to 
        vipāka 
        12 Savipākatoti vipākasabbhāvato, tena paccayantarasamavāyenassā vipākanibbattanaṃ. 
        (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159.). 
        13 Vipākuppādanasamatthatāvasena savipākatoti evamettha attho veditabbo. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 
        159.). 
        7 
        In brief, the natural characteristics of vipāka-dhamma are: having effort in 
        (aimed at) producing vipāka, and having the power or capacity to produce 
        vipāka. 
        In some places, both natures of saussāha (having effort) and sabyāpāra 
        (having activity) are together explained as the capacity to produce results – 
        “saussāhanti phaluppādanasamatthatāvasena sabyāpāraṃ.”14 
        Also, being endowed with javana speed is a characteristic of vipākadhamma.15 
        The saussāha nature of vipāka-dhamma can be briefly described as: 
        Effort in bodily and verbal construction (kāyāṅga and vācāṅga abhisaṅkhāra), 
        and effort in actions that will ripen in the future.16 
        What has been explained above refers to the saussāha and sabyāpāra nature 
        of wholesome and unwholesome minds. 
        However, functional minds (kiriya citta) also have saussāha and sabyāpāra 
        natures. These two natures found in kamma cittas and kiriya cittas should be 
        clearly distinguished. 
        Now, let us discuss these two types of sabyāpāra (saussāha) natures.  
        (Note: Although all functional minds have saussāha and sabyāpāra natures, 
        from this point onward, only functional javana minds excluding the two 
        adverting minds (āvajjana) are considered as saussāha-sabyāpāra minds.)17 
        14 
        Kaṇhasukkasappaṭibhāganti kaṇhañceva sukkañca. Tañca kho sappaṭibhāgaṃ 
        savipakkhaṃ katvā. Kaṇhaṃ paṭibāhitvā sukkaṃ, sukkaṃ paṭibāhitvā kaṇhanti evaṃ 
        sappaṭibhāgaṃ katvā kaṇhasukkaṃ desesi, kaṇhaṃ desentopi ca saussāhaṃ savipākaṃ 
        desesi, sukkaṃ desentopi saussāhaṃ savipākaṃ desesi. (D-a. III. 66.). 
        Saussāhanti 
        phaluppādanasamatthatāvasena 
        Vipākadhammanti attho. (D-ṭ. III. 63.). 
        ca 
        sabyāpāraṃ. Tenāha "savipāka"nti. 
        15 Vipaccanasabhāvatā ca anupacchinnāvijjātaṇhāmānasantāne sabyāpāratā. (Ml-ṭ. I. 38.). 
        Ettha 
        "sabyāpāratā"ti 
        etena 
        āvajjanadvayaṃ 
        vipākañca 
        "anupacchinnāvijjātaṇhāmāne"ti iminā avasiṭṭhaṃ kiriyaṃ nivatteti. (Anu-ṭ. I. 41.). 
        nivatteti, 
        16 Nirussāhasantabhāvo nāma kusalākusalānaṃ viya kāyaṅgavācaṅgābhisaṅkharaṇakiccesu 
        ca āyatiṃ vipaccanakiccesu ca nirussāhabhāvena santabhāvo. Na kilesu pasantabhāvena. 
        (Adp. 261.). 
        The Anudīpanī passage quoted above explains the nirussāha (effortless) and santa (peaceful) 
        nature of vipāka. In accordance with that, the meaning of “saussāha bhāva” should be 
        understood. 
        17 In expressions such as “kiriyamayacittasamuṭṭhānavāyodhātu,” it appears that 
        manodvārāvajjana is also regarded as a kiriyāmaya mind. Likewise, in the Visuddhimagga 
        8 
        Nature of “sbyāpāra,” which is also “sahussāha” 
        As explained above, “ussāha” or “byāpāra” refers to the manner found in 
        action-producing mind-moments. It is not the same as viriya (the mental factor 
        of energy). 
        There are two forms of being sahussāha and sabyāpāra: 
        (1). Savipākatāvisiṭṭhasabyāpārasahussāhatā18 
        This means having activity and effort that are distinguished by the 
        ability to produce vipāka, or by the nature of producing vipāka.  
        Let us call this “savipāka sabyāpāra nature.” 
        (2). Avipākatāvisiṭṭhasabyāpārasahussāhatā19 
        This means having activity and effort that are distinguished by the 
        inability to produce vipāka, or by the nature of not producing vipāka. 
        Let us call this “avipāka sabyāpāra nature.”  
        There is a reason for savipāka sabyāpāra nature. This becomes clear from the 
        definition of vipākadhamma given in the Mūlaṭīkā: 
        Vipaccanasabhāvatā ca anupacchinnāvijjātaṇhāmānasantāne sabyāpāratā.20 
        According to this, the cause of savipāka sabyāpāra nature is that the effortful 
        consciousness arising in a mental continuum where ignorance (avijjā), craving 
        (taṇhā), and conceit (māna) have not been destroyed. 
        The nature of sabyāpā that is supported by undestroyed avijjā, taṇhā, and 
        māna is stronger and more powerful than the sabyāpāra nature that does not 
        receive such support. 
        Therefore, even though the sabyāpāra nature may exist, it becomes capable of 
        producing vipāka only when it is supported by undestroyed defilements. 
        Sabyāpara nature that does not receive this support is not capable of producing 
        vipāka. 
        The Anuṭīkā states: 
        Ṭīkā (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159), all functional minds are generally described as having the sabyāpāra 
        nature. Therefore, it also seems that pañcadvārāvajjana is also considered to be sabyāpāra. 
        18 Kiriyābyākatāya savipākato savipākatāvisiṭṭhasabyāpārasaussāhato vāti. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 159.). 
        19 Kiriyābyākatā avipākato, avipākatāvisiṭṭhasabyāpārasaussāhato vāti evaṃ kusalākusalāya 
        vuttavipallāsena. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 160.). 
        20 Ml-ṭ. I. 38. 
        9 
        na 
        Ussāhoti ca kiriyamayacittuppādassa pavattiākāro veditabbo, yo byāpāroti ca 
        vuccati, 
        vīriyussāho. 
        svāyaṃ yathā asamugghātitānusāyānaṃ 
        kiriyamayacittuppādesu sātisayo labbhati, na tathā niranusāyānaṃ. tato eva te 
        santasabhāvā vipākupādānabyāpārarahitāva honti.21 
        According to this passage, in beings who have not destroyed their latent 
        tendencies (anusaya), this kind of effort is found strongly in their 
        kiriyamayacittuppāda. 
        In beings who are without anusaya, this kind of strong effort is not found to 
        the same degree. 
        Therefore, kiriyamayacittuppāda of beings without anusaya are peaceful in 
        nature and are without the activity (effort) that produces vipāka. 
        But kiriyamayacittuppāda of beings who still have anusaya do have effort that 
        can produce vipāka. 
        This extraordinary activity that is not found in kriyā javana minds but is found 
        only in wholesome and unwholesome minds is called “high speed” (“adhika 
        vega”). This speed is the “manner of existence” (pavatti ākāra) found in 
        wholesome and unwholesome mental clusters. It is also called: the capacity to 
        produce vipāka (sāmatthi), power (satti), and speed of kamma momentum 
        (kamma vega). It is also called the “seed of resultants” (“vipāka bīja”).22 
        Because kamma intention (cetanā) possesses this power, it is like a state that 
        is “sagabbha” – that is, pregnant with the fruit called vipāka.23 
        This same powerful cetanā is called “saṅkhāra” in the teaching of Dependent 
        Origination. It is javana cetanā with the nature of sabyāpāra supported by 
        avijjā. 
        Therefore, the Tathāgata taught: “avijjāpaccayā saṅkhārā.” 
        21 Anu-ṭ. III. 232-3.

        tāvadeva bījakiccaṃ sampādeti. (Prd. 396.). 
        23 Savipākā ca sagabbhā viya oḷārikāti tabbipakkhato avipākā sukhumāti vuttā. (Ml-ṭ. II. 12.).

        sattivisesaṃ sanipphādetabbataṃ passantī nipphādetabbāpekkhā hotīti taṃsambandheneva

        Sā hi vipākānaṃ hetubhāvato paccayabhāvo, taduppādanasamatthatāya sattiviseso, tehi

        10 
        Accordingly, in the teaching of Dependent Origination, the “high speed” 
        (savipāka-sabyāpāra-bhāva) of cetanā should be understood as happening 
        because of avijjā. 
        According to the Paṭṭhāna, this strong activity or ability to produce vipāka 
        that exists in wholesome and unwholesome cittuppādas actually resides in 
        their cetanā. 
        That is the “manner” found in wholesome and unwholesome cetanā. 
        Therefore, wholesome and unwholesome cetanā are called “nānakkhaṇika 
        kamma paccaya.” 
        Vipāka minds arise because of this kammic speed. This should be understood 
        from statements such as: “vipāko kammavegakkhittattā &#x2026;”24 More precisely, 
        vipāka arises because of the speed of past kamma. Vipāka states are not 
        produced by cittābhisaṅkhāra. For this reason, vipāka minds are without effort 
        (nirussāha) and are peaceful (santa).25 
        Although kriyā javana minds arise through mental construction 
        (cittābhisaṅkhāra), they are peaceful because defilements such as avijjā have 
        been destroyed in their mental continuum. 
        Moreover, vipāka states that arise through the speed of kamma are like 
        reflections of kamma (kamma paṭibimba sadisa). 
        Therefore, the Visuddhimagga says: 
        Kammavegakkhittā hi kammapaṭibimbabhūtā ca kāyakammādibyāpāravirahato 
        nirussāhā vipākā.26 
        24 A-ṭ. I. 241; Ml-ṭ. I. 67; Vsm-ṭ. II. 500.

        11 
        Mental factors such as alobha found in vipāka minds do not have sabyāpāra 
        nature in the way that alobha and other mental factors in wholesome minds 
        do.27 
        From this, two more characteristics of vipāka can be stated: 
        (1). Kammavegakkhittaṭṭha – being driven or propelled by the speed of 
        kamma, meaning being produced by the force of kamma. 
        (2). Kammapaṭibimbaṭṭha – being like a reflection of kamma. 
        This point is explained by the Venerable Rerukāne Candawimala Mahā Thera 
        in the Paṭṭhāna Pakaraṇa Sannaya under the lesson on maggappaccaya as 
        follows: 
        Vipāka minds arise without effort and by the force of the kamma. Therefore, the 
        cetanā associated with vipāka minds does not become a nānakkhaṇika kamma 
        paccaya. 
        Now, let us examine the speed of past kamma.  
        The Phrase “(purāṇassa) kammassa katattā upacitatta” and the speed of 
        kamma 
        Past kamma – that is, wholesome and unwholesome cetanā, or wholesome and 
        unwholesome cittuppāda in which cetanā is the chief factor – is called “old 
        kamma” (purāṇa kamma). 
        Because old kamma has been done, vipāka arises. This idea is expressed in 
        passages such as: “Kammassa katattā upacitatta uppajjati cakkhuviññāṇaṃ.” 
        Here, “kammassa katattā” means “because kamma has been done.” 
        The phrase “kammassa upacitatta” conveys several meanings: 
        (1). First, it means that kamma has been built up strengthening it.28 
        That is, it has been made powerful in such a way that it can block the 
        result of another kamma and make room for its own vipāka to arise.29 
        27 Kammavegakkhittesu vipākesu alobhādisahagatakammapaṭibimbabhūtā viya pavattamānā 
        alobhādayoti pañcaviññāṇesu viya nidānarahitatā sotapatitatāti daṭṭhabbā. (Ml-ṭ. II. 141.). 
        28 Ml-ṭ. I. 303. 
        29 Upacitattāti yathā aññassa vipākaṃ paṭibāhitvā attano vipākābhimukhaṃ hoti tathā 
        vaḍḍhitattā. (Ml-ṭ.. I. 124.). 
        "Upacitattā"ti 
        padaṃ saṅgayhati "asādhāraṇakammapaccayavasenā"ti vuttattā. 
        "upacitattāti laddhāsevanattā"ti keci vadanti, taṃ paṭhamajavanassa na yujjati anāsevanattā. 
        Tathā ca sati tassa vipākadānameva na siyāti tato aññathā atthaṃ dassento 
        "yathā"tiādimāha. Tattha vipākābhimukhanti vipākadānābhimukhaṃ katokāsaṃ. Katokāsatā 
        12 
        (2). Second, it means that kamma has been done in a way it builds the cycle 
        of death and rebirth (cuti and paṭisandhi), allowing the process to grow 
        and continue.30 
        Thus, “kammassa upacitatta” means that kamma has been performed in a way 
        that is capable of producing vipāka. 
        What does “capable of producing vipāka” mean? This should be understood 
        in accordance with the Buddha’s teachings. 
        In many places, the Tathāgata taught that kamma follows the person who 
        performs it, that both good and bad deeds take time to ripen, and that a being 
        experiences suffering until bad kamma is exhausted. 
        For example: 
        Na hi pāpaṃ kataṃ kammaṃ – sajju khīraṃva muccati 
        Ḍahantaṃ bālamanveti – bhasmacchannova pāvako. 
        Pāpopi passati bhadraṃ – yāva pāpaṃ na paccati, 
        Yadā ca paccati pāpaṃ – atha pāpo pāpāni passati. 
        Bhadropi passati pāpaṃ – yāva bhadraṃ na paccati, 
        Yadā ca paccati bhadraṃ – atha bhadro bhadrāni passati. 
        Na tāva kālaṃ karoti – yāva na taṃ pāpaṃ byantī hoti. 
        These verses illustrate this point. 
        Views of the Kassapika and Other Schools, and the Theravāda View 
        In ancient times, elders of the Kassapika (Suvarṣa Kāśyapīya) school believed 
        that past kamma accumulates and remains stored, and that it exists separate 
        from consciousness (citta vippayutta). They called this “kammūpacaya.” 
        Monks of the Andhaka and Sammītiya schools held similar views. They based 
        this on teachings such as those quoted above. 
        However, Theravādins do not understand the Buddha’s teachings to mean that 
        past kamma remains stored somewhere in the mental continuum as an existing 
        entity.

        30 Lokuttaravipākampi kusalasadisattā kusalānugatikameva katvā bhājitaṃ. Yasmā pana 
        tebhūmakakusalaṃ cutipaṭisandhivasena vaṭṭaṃ ācināti vaḍḍheti, tasmā tattha katattā 
        upacitattāti vuttaṃ. Lokuttaraṃ pana tena ācitampi apacināti, sayampi cutipaṭisandhivasena 
        na ācināti, tenettha ‘Katattā upacitattā’ti avatvā katattā bhāvitattāti vuttaṃ. (Dhs-a. 328.). 
        13 
        According to Theravāda, what is referred to as “kamma” in those teachings is 
        the capacity of vipākadhamma to produce results, not a stored past kamma 
        itself. 
        This is explained in the Kathāvatthu Anuṭīkā as follows: 
        Yaṃ pana ‘na tāva kālaṃ karoti, yāva na taṃ pāpaṃ byantī hotī’ti sutte vuttaṃ, 
        taṃ yasmiñca santāne kammaṃ katūpacitaṃ, tattha tenāhitaṃ taṃ 
        phaluppādanasamatthataṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ, na atītassa kammassa 
        dharamānasabhāvattā.31 
        According to this, when the Tathāgata spoke of “until bad kamma is 
        exhausted,” “good and evil that ripen,” or “evil that follows like fire covered 
        by ashes,” he was referring to this: 
        In a mental continuum where kamma has been done and built up (katūpacita), 
        the kamma has placed there a capacity or power to produce its result – that is, 
        kamma power (kamma satti). 
        Therefore, one should not think that past kamma is stored somewhere as a 
        separate, existing entitiy. Rather, what is “stored” is the capacity or power of 
        kamma to produce vipāka. 
        What is this “power to produce vipāka”? It is nothing other than: 
        (1). The kamma power in vipākadhamma, 
        (2). Activity (byāpāra), 
        (3). Effort (ussāha), 
        (4). The manner of existence (pavatti ākāra) of cetanā, and 
        (5). The “seed of vipāka” (vipāka bīja). 
        Therefore, whenever the Dhamma says “kammassa katattā upacitatta” – 
        meaning “because kamma was done and built up (so as to be capable of 
        producing vipāka)” – it should be understood that kamma cetanā has placed 
        in the mental continuum the power or capacity to produce its result. 
        Thus, the “being done” (katatta) and “being built up” (upacitatta) of kamma 
        mean that kamma has deposited in the continuum its own ability or power to 
        produce vipāka. 
        For this reason, the Paramatthadīpanī says: 
        Cetanā nāma pavattamānā dve kiccāni sādhenti sahajātānaṃ saṃvidhānañca 
        āyatiṃ 
        vipākapātubhāvatthāya 
        pavattākārasaṅkhātabījanidhānañcāti. 
        31 Anu-ṭ. III. 97. 
        tasmiṃ 
        Tattha 
        santāne 
        attano 
        saṃvidhānakiccaṃ 
        14 
        sabbacetanāsādhāraṇaṃ 
        hoti. 
        Bījanidhānakiccaṃ 
        pana 
        kusalākusalabhāvappattāya eva cetanāya hotī’ti vuttaṃ sahajātācetanātiādi.32 
        This means, cetanā performs two functions: 
        (1). It organizes the associated mental states, and 
        (2). It deposits in the mental continuum a “seed” which is its manner of 
        existence (pavatti ākāra), so that vipāka may arise in the future. 
        The first function belongs to all cetanā. The second function – depositing the 
        seed – belongs only to cetanā that is wholesome or unwholesome. 
        Because vipāka arises from the kamma speed that has been deposited in the 
        mental continuum by past kamma, vipāka states are without effort (nirussāha). 
        They are not produced by cittābhisaṅkhāra (mental construction). For this 
        reason, vipāka states do not themselves have activity aimed at producing 
        further vipāka. 
        Even in the case of the functional minds of Arahants, where effort is still 
        present, if there is no activity aimed at producing vipāka, then it is obvious 
        that vipāka minds, which are without effort, also do not have such activity.  
        Therefore, vipāka minds that arise from the speed of past kamma do not 
        possess the power of nānakkhaṇika kamma paccaya. 
        It is important to discuss a few more points about this kamma speed that has 
        been explained above.  
        “Samaṅgī” and “Samaṅgitā” 
        In many suttas, the Tathāgata frequently refers to people who engage in good 
        and bad deeds as “kāyasucarita samaṅgī,” “kāyaduccarita samaṅgī,” and so 
        on. When explaining such passages, the commentaries mention five types of 
        “samaṅgī” and five types of “samaṅgitā.” 
        “Samaṅgī” means a person who is endowed with something. His condition is 
        called “samaṅgitā.” This means that whatever thing or dhamma the person is 
        32 Cetanānāma pavattamānā dve kiccāni sādheti sahajātānaṃ saṃvidhānañca āyatiṃ 
        vipākapātu bhāvatthāya tasmiṃ santāne attano pavattā kārasaṅkhāta bījanidhānañcāti. 
        Tattha saṃvidhānakiccaṃ sabbacetanā sādhāraṇaṃ hoti. Bījanidhānakiccaṃ pana 
        kusalākusalabhāvappattāyaeva cetanāya hotīti vuttaṃ sahajātācetanātiādi. Tattha 
        sahajātācetanāti antamaso pañcaviññāṇa sahajātāpi cetanā. Sāpi hi sahajātānaṃ 
        saṃvidahanasaṅkhātena kammabhāvena pavattattā kammaṃevanāma hotīti. Yasmā attano 
        pavattākārasaṅkhātassa vipākabījassa nidahanaṃnāma, na tāvadeva bījakiccaṃ sampādeti. 
        Ambabījādīni viya pariṇatabhāvaṃ patvā khaṇantareeva sampādetīti vuttaṃ nānākkhaṇikā 
        cetanātiādi. (Prd. 395-6.). 
        15 
        endowed with – that very thing is his samaṅgitā.33 For example, a person who 
        constantly carries a stick is called a “daṇḍī.” His condition is called “daṇḍitā” 
        or “daṇḍittaṃ.” By that is meant the “stick” (daṇḍa) that he constantly 
        carries.34 In the same way, the samaṅgitā of a samaṅgī person is the very thing 
        with which he is endowed. 
        The five types of samaṅgī and samaṅgitā related to the law of kamma are as 
        follows. 
        (1). “Āyūhana samaṅgī” is a person who, while accumulating wholesome 
        and unwholesome kamma, is endowed with āyūhana. “Āyūhana” 
        means the wholesome or unwholesome volition that strives toward 
        future rebirth. The condition of an āyūhana samaṅgī is called 
        “āyūhana samaṅgitā.” That is the volition called āyūhana.” 
        (2). “Cetanā samaṅgī” is a person who, when performing wholesome or 
        unwholesome actions, is endowed with volition – that is, the mental 
        effort involved in the action. The condition of a cetanā samaṅgī is 
        called “cetanā samaṅgitā.” That is the wholesome or unwholesome 
        volition involved in performing actions. 
        (3). “Kamma-samaṅgī” is a person who is endowed with past kamma 
        which, until Arahantship, is capable of producing rebirth-result 
        (paṭisandhi vipāka). Here, kamma means past action that has not yet 
        produced rebirth-result but is still capable of doing so.35  
        The condition of a kamma samaṅgī is called “kamma samaṅgitā.” 
        That is the past kamma which has not yet produced rebirth-result but 
        is capable of doing so. According to Theravāda, this means the ability, 
        capacity, or power placed in the mental continuum by past kamma – 
        though it has not yet produced rebirth-result, it still has the capability 
        to do so. It is the kamma energy (nānakkhaṇika kammappaccaya satti). 
        Yasmiṃ santāne kammaṃ katupacitaṃ, asati āhārupacchede 
        vipākārahasabhāvassa avigacchanato so tena sahitoyevāti vattabboti 
        āha "samannāgatāti samaṅgībhūtā"ti.36 
        33 When followed by the bhāva taddhita suffix, the long vowel become shortened as in the 
        case of “sabbaññutā.”  
        34 Nīlattaṃ, nīlatā guṇo, yācakattaṃ, yācakatā kriyā, daṇḍittaṃ, daṇḍitā dabbaṃ. 
        (Padarūpasiddhi. 238.). 
        35 Katūpacitassa avipakkavipākassa kammassa vasena kammasamaṅgitā. (M-ṭ. IV. 302.). 
        36 Srd-ṭ. I. 403. 
        16 
        The Sāratthadīpanī explains this as follows: If kamma has been 
        performed and accumulated in a mental continuum, and if the 
        defilements (which are called its “āhāra”) that support it have not been 
        cut off, then the nature of being capable of giving result (nature of 
        ripening)37 does not disappear. Therefore, the person must be said to be 
        endowed with that kamma. Hence the words “samannāgata” and 
        “samaṅgībhūta” are used. 
        The capacity to ripen mentioned above is nothing other than the stored 
        power, strength, or accumulated force placed in the mental continuum. 
        (4). “Upaṭṭhāna-samaṅgī” should be understood as follows. Until 
        Arahantship, when beings pass away from one existence, a sign related 
        to the realm of their next birth appears in the mind. For those who are 
        to be reborn in hell, flames, iron cauldrons, and similar signs appear as 
        indications of that destination. For those who are to be reborn in a 
        mother’s womb, the womb appears as a sign. For those who are to be 
        reborn in a deva world, celestial mansions, wish-fulfilling trees 
        (kamma rukkha), and similar things appear as signs. A person who 
        does not fail to experience such death-proximate signs at the moment 
        of death is called “upaṭṭhāna samaṅgī.” 
        Not failing to experience such signs means that, at death, such signs 
        definitely arise in the mind. Therefore, the sub-commentaries say: 
        “upaṭṭhāna samaṅgitā according to the time when kamma and so on 
        appear” – 
        “Kammādīnaṃ 
        upaṭṭhānakālavasena 
        upaṭṭhānasamaṅgitā.”38 
        There are two aspects in the appearing of death-proximate signs.  
        (a). The sign that appears — that is, the object that manifest in the 
        mind at death: kamma, kamma-sign (kamma nimitta), or 
        destination-sign (gati nimitta). 
        (b).  The kamma that brings forth the sign to the mind, and the 
        kamma which had gained the opportunity for the rebirth by 
        bringing for that sign to the mind. 
        The appearing sign is the object that manifest to the mind at the 
        moment of death. It is of three kinds: kamma, kamma nimitta, and gati 
        
        37 Vipākadhammadhammāti vipākasabhāvadhammā. Yathā jātijarāsabhāvā jātijarāpakatikā 
        sattā jātidhammā jarādhammāti vuccanti evaṃ vipākajanakaṭṭhena vipākasabhāvā 
        vipākapakatikā dhammāti attho. (Dhs-a. 84.). 
        Vipākapakatikāti vipaccanapakatikā. (Ml-ṭ. I. 38.). 
        
        38 M-ṭ. IV. 302. 
        
        nimitta. That sign is presented to the mind by the power of past kamma. 
        When kamma brings such a sign to the mind, it is said that the kamma 
        “makes opportunity” (okāsaṃ karoti) for rebirth. If the dying being 
        grasps that sign without letting it go, that past kamma “obtains 
        opportunity” (okāsaṃ labhati).39 Such kamma is called “okāsakata 
        kamma” and “laddhokāsa kamma” in the Dhamma. 
        A person who is endowed with such kamma – which has gained 
        opportunity by presenting a sign to the mind at death – is called 
        “upaṭṭhāna samaṅgī.” His nature, “upaṭṭhāna samaṅgitā,” is that 
        kamma which has made or obtained opportunity. That is, okāsakata 
        kamma or laddhokāsa kamma. This refers to the capacity (sāmatthi) of 
        that past kamma to produce rebirth-result once it has gained 
        opportunity. 
        (some correction around here)
        
        Unlike the other types of samaṅgitā,40 this okāsa samaṅgitā can 
        change. A kamma that has gained opportunity to produce rebirth in a 
        bad realm may be obstructed by a stronger wholesome kamma suitable 
        for good rebirth, thus resulting in a good rebirth instead and vice versa. 
        The story of the father of Venerable Soṇa at the Acela monastery near 
        Soṇa mountain is an example of this. 
        
        When one kamma presents a sign suitable to or fitting (tajjā) itself, but 
        due to other conditions another kamma presents a different sign, this is 
        called transformation of the sign (nimitta parivattana).41 
        
        -   class notes:
            -   if a kamma "comes up" to produce result but is overcome by another kamma, it becomes ahosi (upaghātakakamma)
        
        (5). “Vipāka-samaṅgī” is a person who is endowed with resultant 
        consciousness and mental factors. His nature, “vipāka samaṅgitā,” is 
        those resultant mentalities themselves. 
        Accordingly, the strong force or energy within wholesome and unwholesome 
        volitions, which has been deposited in the mental and material continuum and 
        is capable of producing future rebirth, is called “kamma samaṅgitā.” When 
        that force gains opportunity for rebirth, it is called “upaṭṭhāna samaṅgitā.” 
        
        39 &#x2026; Ayaṃ kammasamaṅgitānāma. So pana yadā attano anurūpe paccaye labhati. Tadā 
        vipaccanatthāya okāsaṃ karoti. Kathaṃ karotīti. So āsannamaraṇassa sattassa attānaṃvā 
        paccupaṭṭhāpeti. Attano nimittaṃvā paccupaṭṭhāpeti. Gatinimittaṃvā paccupaṭṭhāpeti. Soca 
        satto taṃ amuñcamāno yadi gaṇhāti. Tadā okāsaṃ labhati. Ayaṃ upaṭṭhānasamaṅgitānāma. 
        (Prd. 43-4.) 
        
        40 Sesā niccalā avasesā catubbidhāpi samaṅgitā niccalā aparivattanato. (A-ṭ. I. 221.). 
        
        41 Calatīti parivattati. Ekena hi kammunā tajje nimitte upaṭṭhāpite paccayavisesavasena tato 
        aññena kammunā aññassa nimittassa upaṭṭhānaṃ parivattanaṃ. (M-ṭ. IV. 302.). 
        
        If there is stored force in the continuum but it is not capable of producing 
        rebirth-result, Theravāda does not call it kamma samaṅgitā. It is simply called 
        kamma satti – the capacity of kamma to produce results. 
        The deeper nature of this kamma satti and the changes it causes in the mental 
        continuum are discussed in detail under the nānākkhaṇika kammappaccaya in 
        the analysis of the twenty-four conditions. 
        Kamma Giving Results and Kamma “Ripening” 
        Because of kamma, results arise – “kammā vipākā vattanti.” A result is a 
        dhamma that has kamma as its origin or cause – “vipāko kammasambhavo.” 
        Vipāka is the fruit of kamma. Therefore, the formations (saṅkhāra) done in a 
        previous existence are one dhamma; the consciousness (viññāṇa) that arises 
        in a later existence is another dhamma. Accordingly, kamma is the cause, and 
        vipāka is the effect. 
        
        According to Theravāda, there are two kinds of “fruit” (phala).  
        (1). Phala: a fruit that arises because of a cause. 
        (2). Pakka phala: a fruit that arises as the “ripening” of a cause. 
        In relation to kamma, this should be explained as follows. There are two kinds 
        of fruits that arise because of kamma: material phenomena (rūpa) and mind
        and-mental-factors (citta cetasika dhammas). Among these, rūpa is a fruit of 
        kamma, but because it does not share the same characteristics as kamma, it is 
        called an “inferior fruit” (“nihīna phala”). However, the citta cetasika 
        dhammas that arise due to kamma are dhammas that bear certain features 
        similar to the kamma. Therefore, they are the “superior fruit” (“visiṭṭha 
        phala”) of kamma. Moreover, they are also the ripened fruit (“pakka phala”) – the fruit that arises through the ripening of kamma. For that reason, the citta 
        cetasika dhammas that arise due to kamma are also called “vipāka.” 
        Here the word-analysis is: “visiṭṭho pāko vipāko” – “vipāka is the special 
        pāka.” According to this, the word “pāka” carries the meaning of “fruit.” But 
        this is not an ordinary fruit of a cause; it is a fruit arisen as ripening – a pakka 
        phala. Hence it is said: “When the word pāka is used in the sense of fruit, then 
        here one should understand the meaning as: not inferior like rūpa, but special 
        like the ripened fruit (“pakka phala”) – therefore it is called vipāka.” 
        Sati pana pāka-saddassa phalapariyāyabhāve rūpaṃ viya na nihīno pakkaṃ 
        viya visiṭṭho pākoti vipākoti evaṃ vā ettha attho daṭṭhabbo.42 
        42 Anu-ṭ. I. 41. 
        19 
        A fruit arisen by ripening is not like other results. Ripening means that the 
        conditional power of the prior cause itself appears as a dhamma. Therefore, 
        although in terms of dhammas there are two – cause and effect – when the 
        cause appears in the mode of ripening, it is spoken of as “the cause itself has 
        ripened into the fruit.” This is a special feature to be understood regarding 
        cause and effect. 
        The ripening of kamma means kamma becoming vipāka. In the world, 
        “ripening” (vipaccana) is used for things like a young mango fruit passing 
        beyond rawness and becoming mature and ripe. In the same way, here the 
        meaning is that kamma passes beyond its “young stage” and becomes mature – that is what is meant by kamma giving result. Therefore, the 
        Paramatthadīpanī says:  
        Etthā kenaṭṭhena vipākaṃ nāmāti. Vipaccanaṭṭhena. Kiñca vipaccananti. Yathā 
        loke 
        ambaphalādīnaṃ 
        taruṇabhāvaṃ 
        atikkamma 
        pariṇatabhāvappattivipaccananti vuccati. Evamevaṃ idhapi vipaccanaṃ 
        veditabbanti.43 
        “Here, in what sense is it called vipāka? In the sense of ripening. And what is 
        ripening? Just as in the world the ripening of mango fruits and so on is called 
        the attaining of maturity after passing beyond the youthful state, so too here 
        ripening should be understood in that way.” 
        Accordingly, vipāka is the ripened fruit of kamma – or, stated in another way, 
        the “ripened stage” of kamma. In many suttas the Tathāgata explains kamma 
        giving results in this manner. 
        Let us understand how the Tathāgata describes the ripening of kamma. “Pāka 
        (pakka)” and “vipāka (vipakka)” are two words with the same meaning. Their 
        definitions are: “paccatīti pākaṃ” and “vipaccatīti vipākaṃ” – meaning “what 
        ripens is pāka” and “what ripens is vipāka.” It should also be taken as 
        “pākabhāvaṃ āpajjatīti pākaṃ” – “it reaches the state of ripening; therefore, 
        it is pāka,” and “vipākabhāvaṃ āpajjatitī vipākaṃ” – “it reaches the state of 
        ripening; therefore, it is vipāka.” The same can be expressed as “pākabhāvaṃ 
        apannoti pākaṃ” – “It is called pāka, since it has attained the state of ripening” 
        and “vipākabhāvaṃ āpajjatitī vipākaṃ” – “It is called vipāka, since it has 
        attained the state of ripening.” Thus, it should be understood that what ripens, 
        or what has ripened, is called vipāka. 
        The Tathāgata in various Suttas teaches, “kammaṃ vipaccati” – “kamma 
        ripens.” Here “paccati” is an intransitive (akammaka), non-volitional 
        (nirussāha) verb formed from the root √pac, and according to Saddanīti it 
        43 Prd. 43. 
        20 
        belongs to the divādi group. Therefore “kammaṃ paccati” is a plain active 
        construction, meaning simply: “Kamma ripens.” 
        The phrase “kammaṃ paccati” can also be written in another way according 
        to the definition “vipaccatīti vipāko,” namely: “kammaṃ vipākaṃ hoti” – 
        “Kamma becomes vipāka.” Because “vipaccati” is non-volitional, the sense of 
        √kar (“to do”) is not contained within it. “Kammaṃ vipākaṃ hoti” means 
        “kamma becomes a vipāka.” Accordingly, in the teaching of the Tathāgata, 
        “kamma ripens” means “kamma becomes a vipāka.” The same meaning 
        should be understood in phrases such as “pāpaṃ paccati” and “bhadraṃ 
        paccati.” 
        How correct this grammar-based explanation should be examined further. 
        Kamma is something whose nature is to ripen. Therefore, the commentary 
        explains the term “vipākadhammā” as: “Vipākadhammadhammāti 
        vipākasabhāvadhammā. Yathā jātijarāsabhāvā jātijarāpakatikā sattā 
        jātidhammā jarādhammāti vuccanti evaṃ vipākajanakaṭṭhena vipākasabhāvā 
        vipākapakatikā dhammāti attho”44 – “vipākadhammadhammā means realities 
        whose nature is vipāka; just as beings whose nature is birth and aging are 
        called ‘jātidhamma and ‘jarādhamma’, so too realities that are by nature 
        vipāka, having the character of producing vipāka, are meant here.” The 
        Mūlaṭīkā 
        explains 
        “vipākapakatikā” 
        as 
        “vipaccanapakatikā”45 
        (“vipākapakatikāti vipaccanapakatikā”) – “having the nature of ripening.” 
        Accordingly, “vipākadhammadhammā” are dhammas that have the nature of 
        ripening with the passing of time. 
        According to the teaching of cause and effect, the cause is one reality and its 
        effect is another reality. The cause is not in the effect, and the effect is not in 
        the cause. This also applies to kamma. This should be understood from 
        statements such as: “Phalena suññaṃ kammaṃ, phalaṃ kamme na vijjati – 
        kammañca kho upādāya, tato nibbattata phalaṃ” – “Kamma is empty of the 
        result; the result is not found in kamma; but dependent on kamma, the result is 
        produced.” Thus, it should be understood that no ultimate reality of the cause 
        exists in the effect, and that the effect was not present in the cause. 
        However, the fruit of kamma is somewhat different from other cause–effect 
        relations. It arises due to a “force” deposited in the mental continuum by 
        kamma. Through that force, kamma-born rūpa and vipāka citta cetasikas arise.  
        44 Dhs-a. 84. 
        45 Ml-ṭ. I. 38. 
        21 
        Among these, vipāka citta cetasikas arise with a nature similar to the kamma. 
        Kamma is “with an object” (sārammaṇa), and vipāka citta cetasikas are also 
        “with an object.” In this and other ways, vipāka is a reality that arises with 
        characteristics similar to kamma. Moreover, the nāma dhammas that appear 
        as vipāka are like a reflection of the nāma dhammas of kamma. Therefore, 
        only the citta cetasika dhammas that arise due to kamma receive the name 
        “vipāka” (the ripened stage of kamma). Kamma-born rūpa, which has different 
        (distant) characteristics, does not receive the name vipāka. 
        This same idea is stated in the Mūlaṭīkā as follows:  
        Yathā sālibījādīnaṃ phalāni taṃsadisāni nibbattāni vipakkāni nāma honti, 
        vipākaniruttiñca 
        labhanti, 
        kusalākusalānaṃ 
        phalāni 
        sukkakaṇhādibhāvena 
        ca 
        na 
        mūlaṅkurapattakhandhanāḷāni, 
        arūpadhammabhāvena 
        evaṃ 
        sārammaṇabhāvena 
        taṃsadisāni 
        vipakkabhāvamāpannānīti 
        vipākaniruttiṃ labhanti, na rūpadhammā kammanibbattāpi kammāsadisā.46 
        Just as the fruits of rice seeds and so on, when they arise similar to that seed, 
        are called “ripened” and receive the term “vipāka,” but not the root, sprout, 
        stem, and stalk; in the same way, the fruits of wholesome and unwholesome 
        states, insofar as they become similar by being immaterial dhammas, by being 
        with an object, and by being “white/black” (sukka/kaṇha) and so on, attain the 
        state of “ripened” and thus receive the term “vipāka”; but material dhammas, 
        though produced by kamma, are not similar to kamma. 
        The “ripening of kamma” is explained by Rerukāne Candawimala Thera as 
        follows.  
        The result-consciousnesses are those minds that do not accomplish action, have 
        weak impulse, and appear in the future. 
        When a rice seed is planted, a paddy plant arises from it. The seed gradually 
        decays and disappears. Yet, a certain special power, which is a cause for future 
        seeds, remains connected with the paddy plant, which is the seed’s continuum. 
        When the time comes for the paddy plant to produce seeds, it does not produce 
        some other kind of seed; it produces rice seeds again because of that special 
        power that remained connected to the plant from the originally planted seed. 
        The seeds produced by the paddy plant are the fruits of the seed that was planted. 
        In the same way, when a wholesome or unwholesome javana citta arises, after 
        it has ceased, a power capable of producing future fruit – vipāka cittas – 
        remains connected with that mental continuum. 
        The Buddha said:  
        Na hi pāpaṃ kataṃ kammaṃ - sajju khīraṃ va muccati, 
        Ḍahantaṃ bālamanveti - bhasmacchanno va pāvako  
        46 Ml-ṭ. I. 38. 
        22 
        A bad deed does not immediately flow out like milk; it follows the fool like fire 
        covered with ashes.  
        This was said with reference to that special power that remains in the continuum 
        after the javana citta has ceased. Just as the seed-power connected with the 
        paddy plant gradually matures and then produces rice seeds again, so too the 
        wholesome and unwholesome power that remains connected to the mind
        continuum comes to maturity, and as a mode of “ripening,” groups of citta 
        cetasika dhammas arise in that continuum corresponding to that power. “Pāka” 
        is a name for maturing and ripening. Because the special power left over from 
        past wholesome and unwholesome javanas appears as a maturing, the groups 
        of citta cetasika that arise in that way are called “pāka.” 
        Wholesome and unwholesome are by nature opposed to each other. In the word 
        “vipāka,” the prefix “vi” points to these mutually opposed wholesome and 
        unwholesome states. The dhammas that arise as their maturing are vipākas. 
        The special power connected with the mental continuum, which cannot be 
        directly pointed out as a dhamma and has no mark for direct showing, is one 
        stage of wholesome or unwholesome javana. The vipāka citta is another stage 
        of that. The seed-power connected with the plant ends when seeds arise; it does 
        not keep producing fruit again and again indefinitely. Likewise, the special 
        power left by javana ends when, as ripening, it manifests again as cittas. The 
        name “vipāka” is applied to citta and cetasikas, but not to anything else.  
        
        **vipāka &#x2013; abhidhānattha**
        
        -   **vipaccatīti vipākaṃ**: it is called vipāka because it comes to a ripened state
            -   vipaccati: comes to a ripened state &#x2013; past kamma comes to the point of arising in the present 47
                -   peṭakopadesa: iminā sattena evaṃ dhātukena evarūpaṃ kammaṃ kataṃ, taṃ atītamaddhānaṃ iminā hetunā tassa evarūpo **vipāko vipaccati** etarahi vipaccissati vā anāgatamaddhānaṃ
                -   vipaccana: ripening
                    -   unripe mango reaches maturity
                        kamma reaches maturity as vipāka
        
        prmtdpn:
        
        -   **aññamaññavisiṭṭhānaṃ kusalākusalānaṃ pākāti vipākā**: the ripening of wholesome and unwholesome states, which are distinct from each other (kamma is one, vipāka is another reality)
            -   vi-: distinct from one another (kusala ≠ akusala)
            -   **aññamaññavisiṭṭhānaṃ pākā vipākā**: the ripened states of those that are mutually distinct are vipākas.” This is an analysis based on the genitive tappurisa compound (chaṭṭhī tappurisa samāsa). The word “pāka” should be explained as stated earlier: “Paccatīti pāko” – “It is called pāka because it ripens.” In this analysis, in the definition “kusalākusalānaṃ pāko ti vipāko,” the words “kusalākusala” and “pāka” are in non-apposition (bhinnādhikaraṇa). If this definition is understood as “vipāka is the fruit of kamma,” then kamma must be understood as one dhamma and pāka (vipāka) as another dhamma. If it is understood as “vipāka is the ripened state of kamma,” then kamma should be taken as one stage, and vipāka as another stage of that same process. In that case, the difference of grammatical bases indicates difference of stages. The Vibhāvinī Ṭīkā gives another explanatory meaning by taking the prefix “vi” in the sense of “opposition.”
        
        -   notes:
            -   within the kammasamaṅgītā stage, there are other stages such as diṭṭhadhammavedaniya, aparāpariyavedanīya, upapajjavedanīya, etc
            -   kamma functions other than janaka can be done at any moment
            -   anusaya -> pariyuṭṭhāna: 2 stages of the same kilesa, but can be said that one is the cause for the other (stages of a reality considered as 2 separate things)
        
        -   **aññamaññavirodhānaṃ kusalākusalānaṃ pākāti vipākā**: The ripened dhammas of wholesome and unwholesome states, which are mutually opposed to each other, are called vipāka.” The mutual opposition of wholesome and unwholesome should be understood in terms of their direct oppositional characteristics, as explained in the kusalattika – such as pleasant result (iṭṭha vipāka), unpleasant result (aniṭṭha vipāka), abandoning (pahāyaka), and that which is to be abandoned (pahātabba), and similar directly opposing features.

        -   Their associated 38 cetasikas

        aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        Katame dhammā vipākā? Catūsu bhūmīsu vipāko – ime dhammā vipākā. 
        Classification into the sabba saṅgaha 
        Following is how the vipāka realities are categorized in the sabba saṅgaha. 
        Vipāka dhammas are categorized, 
        (1). In 4 khandhas.  
        (2). In 2 āyatanas.  
        (3). In 8 dhātus.  
        (4). Some in 1 sacca and some are freed from sacca.   
        Other meanings of the term vipāka 
        In the Nibbedhika Sutta of the Aṅguttara Nikāya, the Tathāgata mentioned 
        about the vipāka of kamma and vipāka of saññā (perception). There, the term 
        “vipāka” refers to the “phala”.48 
        Abhidhānattha of the term “vipākadhamma”  
        The literal meanings of the word “vipākadhamma” are as follows.

        Dabbattha Saṃvaṇṇanā of the term “vipākadhammadhammā” 
        The ultimate representatives of the term “vipākadhammadhammā” is as 
        follows. 
        (1). 12 akusala cittas 
        (2). 21 kusala cittas 
        (3). There associated 52 cetasikas 
        Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term vipākadhammadhammā according to 
        the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        Katame dhammā vipākadhammadhammā? Catūsu bhūmīsu kusalaṃ akusalaṃ – ime dhammā vipākadhammadhammā. 
        Classification into the sabba saṅgaha 
        Following is how the vipākadhamma realities are categorized in the sabba 
        saṅgaha. 
        Vipākadhammas are categorized,  
        (1). In 4 khandhas.  
        (2). In 2 āyatanas.  
        (3). In 2 dhātus.  
        (4). Some in 3 saccas and some are freed from saccas.   
        Nevavipākanavipākadhammadhammā 
        “Nevavipākanavipākadhamma” refers to non-self-realities that are neither 
        vipāka nor vipāka-producing. In other words, they are things that are neither 
        the result of past kamma nor have the nature of producing future vipāka. 
        Sabhāvattha of the term “nevavipākanavipākadhamma”  
        the intrinsic meaning of “nevavipāka-navipākadhamma”  
        The absence of the peaceful, inactive nature of vipāka (nirussāha santa 
        bhāva), and the absence of the active, effortful nature that produces vipāka 
        (sabyāpāra sahussāha bhāva), which is the nature of ripening in the future 
        (vipaccana bhāva). 
        Abhidhānattha of the term “nevavipākanavipākadhamma”  
        The definition of the third term is as follows.  
        “Na eva vipākā > nevavipākā; na vipākadhammā > navipākadhammā; 
        nevavipākā navipākadhammā > nevavipākanavipākadhammā” 
        26

        (1). 20 kiriya cittas (pdf corrected)
        (2). Their associated 35 cetasika  (pdf corrected)
        (3). 28 rūpas 
        (4). Nibbāna

6.  class notes

    -   st, some faculty can be produce by another kamma
    -   in case one kamma destroys the eye sight; that kamma is not obstructing, but destroying the other kamma that was producing the cakkhu
    -   4 upaghātakas
    -   the samaṅgī is with regard to one single kamma
        -   they are stages of the same kamma
        -   hindu: milk -> curd -> ghee etc; kamma itself ripens
        -   theravāda:
            -   phala
            -   pakkaphala
    -   kammaṁ vipaccati: kamma ripens
    -   every root has the meaning of "doing": gacchati = gamanaṁ karoti

<a id="orga68c90c"></a>

## 4. Upādiṇṇattika


<a id="orgdd2f0ac"></a>

### Pāḷi

[

-   **upādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā**
-   **anupādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā**
-   **anupādiṇṇaanupādāniyā dhammā**

] 


<a id="org4c990f1"></a>

### meaning

[

-   **upādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā**: non-self-realities that are taken by the kammas approached by clinging as their results and that become objects of clinging
    -   **upādiṇṇa** (sl: nn): results of lokiyakamma
        -   upa + ā + dā: grasped, taken
-   **anupādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā**: non-self-realities that are not taken by the kammas approached by upādāna as their results but that become objects of clinging
-   **upādāniya / upādānīya**: objects of upādāna: lokiyadhammas
-   **anupādiṇṇa-anupādāniyā dhammā**: non-self-realities that are not taken by the kammas approached by upādāna as their results or that does not become objects of clinging

1.  upādiṇṇa vs anupādiṇṇa

    dhsgn-a: upadiṇṇa's sabhāvattha = abhidhānattha
    
    -   ārammaṇakaraṇavasena taṇhādiṭṭhīhi upetena kammunā ādinnā phalabhāvena gahitāti upādiṇṇā: that which is taken as its own result by kamma that is approached by craving and wrong view by way of taking objects is called upādiṇṇa (upa + ā + √dā + ta > upādiṇṇa) 
        -   mūlaṭīkā: 2 interpretive methods for the above
            -   1: **upādiṇṇa**
                -   **approached**: lokiya ku / aku kamma are "approached" by the 4 upādānas in the sense of taking them as object
                -   **approacher**: 4 upādāna
                    -   upeta (upa + √i + ta)
                        -   upa: upaya &#x2013; 4 upādānas &#x2013; implies what is approached is lokiya because they are the ones that are "approached" by upādāna
                    -   upeta: what is approached &#x2013; kamma&#x2013;, which carries the notion of upetakiriyā (act of approaching) and upetā (the agent of that act of approaching) insofar as they are connected with the thing approached
                    -   rūpadhātuyā kho pana, gahapati, yo chando yo rāgo yā nandī yā taṇhā ye upayupādānā, cetaso adhiṭṭhānā abhinivesā anusayo
                        -   upaya: craving and wrong view
                    -   notes: upeti: act of approaching, upagamanaṁ; upeta/upetaṁ: what is approached; upetā (upetu): approacher; upādāna = upaya
                -   **the manner** it is approached is by way of taking it as an object
                    -   not by association
                        -   different manners of approaching: by association (samannāgamana), by taking as object (ārammaṇakaraṇa)
                -   **what has been taken** by it? dhammas taken by mundane kamma as though it takes them in the following manner: “these dhammas have arisen because of us; therefore, they are our results.” these dhammas are mundane resultant consciousness and mental factors, and kamma-produced material phenomena (kammaja rūpa), which arise due to mundane kamma.
                -   **how has it been taken?** lokiya kamma takes lokiya vipāka ctcsk and kammajarūpa as its own result. **The arising of something as the result of a iiiicause is regarded as though the cause takes (took) that result**. Therefore, in the Anuṭīkā it is stated: “Phalabhāvenāti hi ādānappakāravacanaṃ.” [The above fifth question is stated in the Anuṭīkā.] If what is taken by dhammas approached by craving and wrong view by way of taking them as objects is upādiṇṇa, and if the term upādiṇṇa is understood to mean only mundane resultant consciousness and mental factors and kamma-produced matter, then two problems may arise.
    
    **The first problem**: “upeta” means that which is approached by the 
    clinging of craving and wrong view by way of taking it as an object. All 
    mundane dhammas can be taken as objects in that way. There is no fixed 
    rule that the word “upeta” applies only to kamma. Therefore, there is no 
    reason to take the word “upeta” as referring only to kamma. If that is so, 
    then all conditioned phenomena that arise as results of mundane 
    conditional dhammas would fall under the category of upādiṇṇa, because 
    those conditioned phenomena too are taken by their conditions as their 
    own results. 
    
    -   note: why only lokiyakamma, why only ār.karaṇa, why (one more under the second problem)
    
    **The second problem**:
    
    -   upeta:
        -   upa: having been approached
            -   It does not specifically highlight the sense of “taking as an object” by excluding the meaning of association.
        -   ādinna: having been taken
            -   not the specific characteristic of being kamma-produced (kammaja)
            -   If it is taken in that way, would it
    
    not be possible to call all conditioned phenomena “upādiṇṇa”? 
    According to the Mūlaṭīkā, it cannot be so named. The reason is that the 
    teaching was given according to the disposition of those beings who were 
    to be instructed (**bodhaneyyajanajjhāsayavasena**). When the term 
    upādiṇṇa was used for the sake of enabling certain beings to understand, 
    they understood, by that very word, the dhammas mentioned above – 
    mundane resultant consciousness and mental factors, and kamma
    produced matter. 
    
    Because the meaning becomes grounded on the specific (visesa) while 
    the general meaning is illustrated, those who require the specific meaning 
    may apply that specific meaning appropriately. 
    
    In the present time (comm time), since there are beings who are unable to understand 
    the intended meaning merely upon hearing the term upādiṇṇa, the above 
    interpretive explanation has been taught for their understanding. 
    
    -   class notes:
        -   upādiṇṇa is the lokiya vipāka dhammas produced by lokiya kamma that is approached by upādāna
            -   kamma "takes" (ādinna, ādadāti) the result; figure of speech; taking a new life
            -   upādāna approaches (upeti) the lokiya kamma
        -   "cows" includes all types of cows;
            -   the specific is included in the general
        -   ādiṇṇa: takes in the sense of producing vipāka
        -   grammatically, the word itself could be applied to more than lokiyavipākas; every saṅkhāra could be so
            -   any lokiya reality can be approached by upādāna which can then produce vipākas and support even lokuttara dhamdhamma
            -   the Buddha
            -   3 upādiṇṇa: kammaja realities, living body parts, anything taken as mine
        -   upādiṇṇarūpa: strongly grasped by upādāna; but here, it's a different approach by the comm.

2.  2nd interpretation &#x2013; mūlaṭīkā

    -   **upādiṇṇa**
        -   **upa**: that which has been approached &#x2013; upeta
            -   carries together the meaning of the act of approaching and that which is approached
            -   e.g.: pācariyo (teacher of the teacher): pagato ācariyo pācariyo
                -   pa: gone away (pagata)
            -   upeta and gata do not have to be in the active tense
            -   although a prefix, it conveys the meaning of “approaching,” together with a derivational sense; that is, it expresses an action
                -   e.g.: atimālā (a garland that surpasses other garlands)
                    -   ati: surpassing (atikkamanaṃ) together with an accomplishment (sādhana) of the agent (kattu sādhana) in the domain of compounds (samāsa)
                    -   prefixes within compounds can also express grammatical accomplishments (sādhana)
            -   upagamana = clinging = upaya = upādānupaya
            -   upeta: dhamma approached by upaya (clinging) &#x2013; lokiya kamma, which does not occur in the absence of clinging
                -   upādānapaccayā bhavo
                -   purimajātuppannena upādānena upagatattā &#x2013; called upeta since it is approached by clinging in a previous existence by way of being a supporting condition (paccayabhāvena) for no one performs kamma while being unapproached by clinging and unwilling
                    -   paccayabhāvena: prior arising of clinging, including co-arisen clinging
                    -   co-arisen dhammas that function as conditions are spoken of as “previously arisen” dhammas. In the Paṭṭhāna, the passages “ekaṃ mahābhūtaṃ paṭicca tayo mahābhūtā” and “ekaṃ khandhaṃ paṭicca tayo khandhā” are examples of this. Though the gerund form “paṭicca” might express that the cause has arisen previously, above Paṭṭhāna phrases tell about causes that have arisen together with their effects.
                    -   Both clinging that has arisen previously and clinging that arises together also condition mundane kamma. Co-arising clinging conditions lobha-rooted kamma, whereas for mundane wholesome kamma and for dosa-rooted and moha-rooted kamma, previously arisen clinging functions as a condition such as decisive support (upanissaya paccaya), and so forth. Even in cases where there is no previously arisen clinging for mundane wholesome kamma and for dosa-rooted and moha-rooted kamma, it should be understood that clinging conditions those kammas simply because it has not been abandoned by the supramundane path.
                    -   notes:
                        -   upadana supports kamma: arising previously (upanissaya), arising co-nascent (sahajāta), being latent (
                            -   dosamulakamma,etc: upadana as upanissaya, not sahajāta
                            -   lobhamulakamma: can have upadana as upanissaya and co-nascent
                            -   kusalakamma: upadana support as a latent force
                        -   bhūmiladdha uppanna
                    -   When an unextinguished defilement (not destroyed by the path) obtains a cause, it can arise in the future and, as an ultimate dhamma, become a condition for its own result; therefore, even while it remains un-abandoned, it is suitable to be designated by usages such as “uppanna” (“arisen”) and “atthi” (“exists”).
                        -   uneradicated defilement considered as uppanna / atthi
                    -   In teachings such as “ariyaṃ aṭṭhaṅgikaṃ maggaṃ bhāvento… uppannuppanne pāpake akusale dhamme antarāyeva antaradhāpeti” and “santaṃ vā ajjhattaṃ kāmacchandaṃ atthi me ajjhattaṃ kāmacchando’ti pajānāti,” the terms uppanna and atthi refer to these unabandoned unwholesome states
                    -   Thus, through the kamma approached by clinging, rebirth is taken up. Beings take rebirth by kamma that can be object of cankers (sāsava kamma)
                    -   Therefore, rebirth itself becomes prominent as something that is taken up.
                        -   approaching: clinging;
                            taking: here, taking rebirth, not any result
                    -   This is also called “upapatti bhava.” Upapatti bhava constitutes mundane resultant consciousness, their mental factors and kamma-produced matter.
                    -   Upapatti bhava is what is well fitted (“samavaruddha”) to the term “jāti” in the phrase “bhavapaccayā jāti,” (it means what arises is the upapatti bhava) and thus by the term upādiṇṇa “upapatti bhava” is intended.
                        -   note: upa here covers the upadana and the kamma approached by it
                        -   ādiṇṇa: the upapattibhava
    
    -   notes:
        -
    
      According to the Dhātukathā, upapatti bhava is the resultant consciousness 
    pertaining to the threefold realms, their mental factors and kamma-born 
    matter. Therefore, in the commentary, by the phrase “upetena ādinnā,” 
    precisely these mundane resultant consciousnesses, their associated mental 
    factors and kammaja rūpa are intended. 
    On the other hand, the term “anupādiṇṇa” refers to dhammas that are not taken as 
    results by a kamma approached by clinging. That is, dhammas other than mundane 
    resultant consciousness, their associated mental factors and kamma-born matter. 
    Upādāniya and Anupādāniya 
    In the commentary and sub-commentaries to the Dhammasaṅgaṇī, the intrinsic 
    meaning and word meanings given for the term upādāniya are the same. 
    (1). “Ārammaṇabhāvaṃ upagantvā upādānasambandhena upādānānaṃ hitāti 
    upādāniyā.” 
    Having become an object, because it is supportive to clinging, in relation to 
    clinging, it is called upādāniya. [“upa + ā + √dā + anīya > upādānīya > 
    upādāniya”] or [upādāna + iya > upādāniya]. 
    (2). “Upādānānaṃ ārammaṇabhāvānativattanato upādānehi upādātabbāti 
    upādāniyā.” 
    Because it does not go beyond being an object of clinging (does not go 
    beyond the range of clinging), that which should be grasped by clinging is 
    called upādāniya. 
    (3). “Upādātuṃ arahantīti upādāniyā.” 
    That which is fit to be grasped is called upādāniya. 
    (4). “Upādāne niyuttāti upādāniyā.” 
    That which is involved in the act of grasping (that is, what grasped; 
    functioning as the object in the act of taking) is called upādāniya. [“upādāna 
    
    -   ika > upādānika > upādāniya”].
    
    5 
    By all these definitions, what is meant are all (ultimate) dhammas that are an object
    condition 
    (ārammaṇappaccaya) 
    for 
    clinging 
    “upādānassa 
    ārammaṇapaccayabhūtāname taṃ adhivacanaṃ.” That is, mundane dhammas. – 
    In this definition, by the term upādāniya, all ultimate dhammas that can become 
    objects of clinging are taken without distinction. But earlier, by the term upeta, only 
    kamma that is taken as an object by clinging was taken. 
    The dhammas termed upādāniya are twofold as upādiṇṇa and anupādiṇṇa. But 
    anupādāniya dhammas are always only anupādiṇṇa. There are no anupādāniya 
    dhammas that are upādiṇṇa. 
    On the other hand, by the term anupādāniya are meant dhammas that are not objects 
    for clingings; that is, supramundane dhammas. 
    Now we shall consider the three terms of the Upādiṇṇa-tika. 
    Upādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā 
    Now we shall study the sabhāvattha, abhidhānattha and dabbattha of the term 
    “upādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā.” 
    Sabhāvattha and abhidhānattha 
    The literal meaning here is: “upādiṇṇā ca te upādāniyā cāti upādiṇṇupādāniyā” – 
    “They are both upādiṇṇa and upādāniya; therefore, they are called 
    upādiṇṇupādāniya.” 
    This is a karmadhāraya compound. The intrinsic meanings (sabhāvattha) and the 
    etymological meanings (abhidhānattha) of the words in this compound have been 
    explained above. 
    What is meant here are material phenomena (rūpa dhamma) and immaterial 
    phenomena (arūpa dhamma) that have arisen due to kamma that can be objects of 
    taints (sāsava kamma).1 
    Dabbattha 
    The ultimate representatives of the term “upādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā” are as follows.  
    1
    Upādiṇṇā ca te upādāniyā cāti upādiṇṇupādāniyā, sāsavakammanibbattānaṃ 
    rūpārūpadhammānametaṃ adhivacanaṃ. (Dhs-a. 85.). 
    6 
    (1). 32 lokiya vipāka cittas  
    (2). There associated 35 cetasikas (except 3 virati) 
    (3). 20 kammaja rūpas 
    Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term upādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā 
    according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
    Katame dhammā upādiṇṇupādāniyā? Tīsu bhūmīsu vipāko, yañca rūpaṃ kammassa 
    katattā – ime dhammā upādiṇṇupādāniyā. 
    Classification into the sabba saṅgaha 
    Following is how the upādiṇṇupādāniya realities are categorized in the sabba 
    saṅgaha. 
    Upādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammas are categorized, 
    (1). In 5 khandhas 
    (2). In 11 āyatanas 
    (3). In 17 dhātus 
    (4). In 1 sacca 
    Since the “sadda rūpa” is an “anupādiṇna” reality, ultimate realities of this term do 
    not classify under “saddāyatana” and “sadda dhātu.” 
    Anupadiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā 
    Now we shall study the sabhāvattha, abhidhānattha and dabbattha of the term 
    “anupādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā.” 
    Sabhāvattha and abhidhānattha 
    The literal meaning here is: “anupādiṇṇā ca te upādāniyā cāti anupādiṇṇupādāniyā” – “They are both anupādiṇṇa and upādāniya; therefore, they are called 
    anupādiṇṇupādāniya.” 
    This is a karmadhāraya compound. The intrinsic meanings (sabhāvattha) and the 
    etymological meanings (abhidhānattha) of the words in this compound have been 
    explained above. 
    7 
    Dabbattha 
    The ultimate representatives of the term “anupādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā” are as 
    follows. 
    (1). 12 akusala cittas 
    (2). 17 lokiya kusala cittas 
    (3). 20 kiriya cittas 
    (4). Their associated 52 cetasikas 
    (5). 17 cittaja rūpas 
    (6). 15 utuja rūpas 
    (7). 14 āhāraja rūpas 
    Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term anupādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammā 
    according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
    Katame dhammā anupādiṇṇupādāniyā? Tīsu bhūmīsu kusalaṃ, akusalaṃ, tīsu 
    bhūmīsu kiriyābyākataṃ, yañca rūpaṃ na kammassa katattā – ime dhammā 
    anupādiṇṇupādāniyā. 
    Classification into the sabba saṅgaha 
    Following is how the anupādiṇṇupādāniya realities are categorized in the sabba 
    saṅgaha. 
    Anupādiṇṇupādāniyā dhammas are categorized, 
    (1). In 5 khandhas 
    (2). 1n 7 āyatanas 
    (3). In 8 dhātus 
    (4). In 2 saccas 
    Anupādiṇṇa-anupādāniyā dhammā 
    Now we shall study the sabhāvattha, abhidhānattha and dabbattha of the term 
    “anupādiṇṇa-anupādāniyā dhammā.” 
    Sabhāvattha and abhidhānattha 
    The literal meaning here is: “anupādiṇṇā ca te anupādāniyā cāti 
    anupādiṇṇānupādāniyā” – “They are both anupādiṇṇa and anupādāniya; therefore, 
    they are called anupādiṇṇānupādāniyā.” 
    This is a karmadhāraya compound. The intrinsic meanings (sabhāvattha) and the 
    etymological meanings (abhidhānattha) of the words in this compound have been 
    explained above. 
    8 
    Dabbattha 
    The ultimate representatives of the term “anupādiṇṇa-anupādāniyā dhammā” are as 
    follows. 
    (1). 8 lokuttara cittas 
    (2). Their associated 36 cetasikas (except 2 appamaññā) 
    (3). Nibbāna 
    Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term anupādiṇṇa-anupādāniyā 
    dhammā according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
    Katame dhammā anupādiṇṇaanupādāniyā? Cattāro maggā apariyāpannā, cattāri 
    ca sāmaññaphalāni, nibbānañca – ime dhammā anupādiṇṇaanupādāniyā. 
    Classification into the sabba saṅgaha 
    Following is how the anupādiṇṇa-aupādāniya realities are categorized in the sabba 
    saṅgaha. 
    Anupādiṇṇa-aupādāniyā dhammas are categorized, 
    (1). Some in 4 khandhas. Some are freed from khandhas.  
    (2). In 2 āyatanas. 
    (3). In 2 dhātus. 
    (4). Some in 2 saccas. Some are freed from saccas.  
    9
