<a id="orgfea2a4b"></a>

## 5. Saṅkiliṭṭhatika


<a id="org36b49d0"></a>

### Pāḷi

[

-   **saṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā dhammā**
-   **asaṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā dhammā**
-   **asaṃkiliṭṭhaasaṃkilesikā dhammā**

]  


<a id="org8daecfd"></a>

### meaning

[

-   **saṅkiliṭṭha**: polluted, defiled &#x2013; connected with kilesa &#x2013; includes all kilesas because they defile each other (never alone)
    -   like ghee mixed with poison: it is also poisonous
    -   citta, when associated with kilesa, is defiled
    -   if J has kilesa, santati is defiled
        citta is pure, but if associated with kilesa, it becomes defiled
        Bh is pure, but if J has kilesa, the entire santati is defiled
-   **asaṅkiliṭṭha**: not associated with kilesa
-   **saṅkilesika**: objects of kilesa, that are supportive to kilesa &#x2013; lokiyadhammas
-   **asaṅkilesika**: not object of kilesa &#x2013; lokuttaradhammas

]

1.  class notes

    -   one who took refuge in the triple gem has more value than an outsider that has jhāna
    -   2 sammādiṭṭhis:
        -   dasavatthuka sammādiṭṭhi: final attainment abhiññā (*:q: clarify*)
        -   catusacca sammādiṭṭhi: final attainment arahatship
            -   its entrance is the refuge in the Triple Gem
                -   (*:q: do paccekabuddhas have this refuge or just refuge on the Dhamma?  and a SSB, only Dhamma?*)
    -   lay sīlavanta is higher than a vattasampanna (protecting 4 pārājikas) is higher than sāmaṇera and sīlavanta lay person
    -   one with vipassanāñāṇa, status greatly increses
        -   lay sotp is higher than puthu monk
        -   bhante: lay person with upacāra may be higher than normal bhikkhu
        -   gati also affect someone's value
    -   minimum refuge to be buddhist:
        -   me: maybe just Dhamma (like the Buddha and Paccekabuddhas)
        -   can someone take refuge on the Buddha but not on the Dhamma? no, since one is ready to accept the teachings He would give; automatically, the Saṅgha also is included, since one accepts those indiviuals as having been liberated
        -   only Saṅgha: no, since they have followed and attained the Dhamma and followed the Buddha
        -   only Dhamma: one who finds a Dhamma book (no Saṅgha and no Buddha) would still accept the author of that such book
            -   SSB and PCKBD: seems to be only refuge on Dhamma
                -   2 pckbd practiced for 7 years so they have a clear vision of the mode of practice

2.  pdf

    -   **saṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā dhammā**: non-self realities that are oppressed, tormented and defiled by defilements and that become the object of defilements
    -   **asaṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā dhammā**: – non-self realities that are not oppressed, tormented or defiled by defilements but that become the object of defilements
    -   **asaṃkililṭṭha-asaṃkilesikā dhmmā**: non-self realities that are not oppressed, tormented or defiled by defilements and do not become the object of defilements
    
    1.  saṅkilesa
    
        dhammas that excessively oppress, torment, and defile a being and the continuum of mind-and-matter (nāmarūpa santāna)
        
        **saṅkilesetīti saṅkileso, vibādhati, upatāpeti, (vidūseti) cāti attho**: what is meant 
        here are the defilements (kilesa dhammas). 
        
        -   saddanīti: (saṃ)kilisatīti (saṃ)kileso
            -   saṁ in saṅkilesa: excessiveness, intensity (atisayattha)
        
        abhidhānattha: saṅkilissatīti saṅkileso 
        
        -   netti comm: saṅkilissati etenāti saṅkileso (that by means of which a being becomes oppressed or tormented is called saṅkilesa)
        
        dabbattha: 10 kilesas &#x2013; lobha (rāga), dosa, moha, māna, diṭṭhi, vicikicchā, thīna, uddhacca, ahirika, anottappa 
        
        1.  3 intrinsic characteristics of defilements (saṃkilesa)
        
            -   **vibādhanattha / bādhanattha** (oppression): Defilments oppress the being or the continuum of mind and matter. Here vibādana means causing suffering (pīḷana) and exhausting or tiring (kilamana).
            -   **upatāpanattha / tāpanattha** (tormenting): They torment the being or the continuum of mind and matter. Upatāpana means burning the continuum, causing anguish and agitation (pariḷāha), and destroying calmness in the mind and body.
            -   **vidūsanaṭṭha / dūsanaṭṭha** (defilement): They corrupt and make impure the being or the continuum of mind and matter.
        
        2.  3 consequences of being defiled
        
            -   **aggabhāva decreases**: value or worth of one's santāna decreases
                -   a person's value is determined by the magnitude of the karmic result produced when offerings are given to them
            -   **aniṭṭhaphala**: defiled dhammas produce undesirable results, even though they themselves are not defilements (*:q: clarify*)
            -   **gārayha**: dhammas (and beings) defiled by defilements are blameworthy in the eyes of the wise, even though they themselves are not defilements (*:q: clarify*)
    
    2.  saṅkiliṭṭha and asaṅkiliṭṭha
    
        intrinsic, etymological meanings: Dhammas that are oppressed and tormented by the above-mentioned defilements are called saṅkiliṭṭha &#x2013; (saṃ)kilissatīti saṅkiliṭṭho
        
        -   saṅkilissa: saṃ + √kilis + ta → saṅkiliṭṭha
        
        -   vibādhitaṭṭha / bādhitaṭṭha: oppressed and tormented by defilements
        -   upatāpitaṭṭha / tāpitaṭṭha: being tormented by defilements
        -   dūsitaṭṭha: if the meaning of defiling nature is emphasized, then being corrupted or defiled (dūsitaṭṭha) should also be taken as an intrinsic meaning of the word saṅkiliṭṭha
        
        -   both nāmasantāna and rūpakāya are oppressed
        
        -   saṅkiliṭṭha here: dhammas associated with defilements, as though they have become one with defilements (throught saṁyogalakkhaṇas)
            -   dhammas that share the same arising, cessation, base, and object with defilements
            -   kilesa sampayutta dhamma
            -   dabbattha: all akusala dhammas
        
        -   asaṅkiliṭṭha: dhammas that are not oppressed, not tormented, and not defiled by defilements
            -   not associated with defilements
            -   dabbattha: kusala dhammas and abyākata dhammas
            -   the intrinsic and etymological meanings of “asaṅkiliṭṭha” should be understood according to the explanation given for “saṅkiliṭṭha.”
    
    3.  saṅkilesika and asaṅkilesika
    
        -   **saṅkilesika**:  dhammas that can become objects of defilements
        -   intrinsic and etymological meanings
            -   2 explanations: 
                -   **attānaṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā pavattanena saṅkilesaṃ arahantīti saṅkilesikā**: since defilements exist taking them as object, they are suitable for saṃkilesa (defilements), hence they are called saṅkilesika.
                -   **saṅkilese vā niyuttā, tassa ārammaṇabhāvānatikamanato ti saṅkilesikā**: since they serve as objects of defilements, they do not transcend defilements. Therefore, they are called saṅkilesika.
                    This explanation follows the interpretation “things which are engaged in defilements by means of becoming their object” – “Saṅkilese niyuttā saṅkilesikā.” Because the phrase “saṅkilese niyuttā” (engaged in defilements) might suggest association similar to “cetasika” in “cetasī niyuttā cetasikā”, the Mūlaṭīkā provides the examples of vatthayugika and suṅkasālika. “Vatthayuge niyuttā vatthayugikā” – a person engaged in a pair of garments
        
        “Suṅkasālāyaṃ niyuttā suṅkasālikā” – tax collectors engaged in the tax hall 
        Although the word “engaged” appears in these examples, it does not indicate 
        co-arising or association. Likewise, “saṅkilese niyuttā” does not indicate co
        arising association but rather being taken as an object.  
        3 
        If a certain dhamma can be taken as an object by a mental dhamma, that 
        dhamma is said not to have transcended that mental dhamma. 
        Thus, an intrinsic meaning of “saṅkilesika” is “not transcending the defilements” 
        (“saṅkilesānatikamanaṭṭha”). That means not going beyond the range of objects of 
        defilements. 
        The ultimate referent (dabbattha) of the word “saṅkilesika” is all mundane dhammas 
        (lokiya dhammas) that can become objects of defilements. 
        On the other hand, “asaṅkilesika” refers to dhammas that cannot be taken as objects 
        by defilements. These are dhammas that transcend defilements, namely 
        supramundane dhammas (lokuttara dhammas). 
        The intrinsic and etymological meanings of “asaṅkilesika” should be understood 
        according to the explanation given for “saṅkilesika.” 
        Another meaning of “saṅkilesika” 
        However, in some contexts the word “saṅkilesika” refers to dhammas associated with 
        defilements. 
        For example, in the Paṭisambhidāmagga passage: “Hañci paccuppanne kilese 
        pajahati, tena hi … kaṇhasukkadhammā yuganaddhā samameva vattanti, saṅkilesikā 
        maggabhāvanā hoti.” Here “saṅkilesika” refers to dhammas associated with 
        defilements, that is, saṅkiliṭṭha dhammas. 
        Now let us examine each term of the saṃkiliṭṭha tika individually. 
        Saṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā Dhammā 
        The intrinsic meaning and the etymological meaning of the term 
        saṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesika are as follows. 
        Sabhāvattha  
        The intrinsic meaning of the term saṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesika should be understood 
        according to the explanations given for the words saṅkiliṭṭha and saṅkilesika. 
        That is, the intrinsic meaning consists of all the following characteristics: being 
        afflicted by defilements, being tormented by them, being corrupted or stained by 
        them, and not having transcended the defilements (vibādhita-upatāpita-vidūsita
        saṅkilesānatikkamanaṭṭha). 
        Abhidhānattha  
        “Saṅkiliṭṭhā ca te saṅkilesikā cāti saṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā” – “They are saṅkiliṭṭha, and 
        they are also saṅkilesika. Therefore, they are called saṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā.” 
        Dabbattha 
        The ultimate referent of the expression saṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā dhammā is as follows. 
        4 
        (1). 12 akusala cittas 
        (2). Their associated 27 cetasikas 
        Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term saṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā 
        dhammā according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        Katame dhammā saṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā? Dvādasākusalacittuppādā – ime 
        dhammā saṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā. 
        Classification into the sabba saṅgaha 
        Following is how the saṃkiliṭṭha-saṃkilesika realities are categorized in the sabba 
        saṅgaha. 
        Saṃkiliṭṭha-saṃkilesika dhammas are categorized, 
        (1). In 4 khandhas. 
        (2). In 2 āyatanas. 
        (3). In 2 dhātus. 
        (4). In 2 saccas.  
        Asaṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā Dhammā 
        The intrinsic meaning and the etymological meaning of the term 
        asaṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesika are as follows. 
        Sabhāvattha 
        The intrinsic meaning of the term asaṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesika should be understood 
        according to the explanations given for the words asaṅkiliṭṭha and saṅkilesika. 
        That is, the intrinsic meaning consists of all the following characteristics: not being 
        afflicted by defilements, not being tormented by them, not being corrupted or stained 
        by them, yet not having transcended the defilements (avibādhita-anupatāpita
        avidūsita-saṅkilesānatikkamanaṭṭha). 
        Abhidhānattha  
        “Asaṅkiliṭṭhā ca te saṅkilesikā cāti asaṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā” – “They are 
        asaṅkiliṭṭha, and they are also saṅkilesika. Therefore they are called 
        asaṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā.” 
        Dabbattha 
        The ultimate referent of the expression saṅkiliṭṭhasaṅkilesikā dhammā is as follows. 
        (1). 17 lokiya kusala cittas 
        (2). 32 lokiya vipāka cittas 
        (3). 20 kiriya cittas 
        (4). Their associated 38 cetasikas 
        (5). 28 rūpa 
        5 
        Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term asaṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā 
        dhammā according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        Katame dhammā asaṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā? Tīsu bhūmīsu kusalaṃ, tīsu bhūmīsu 
        vipāko, tīsu bhūmīsu kiriyābyākataṃ, sabbañca rūpaṃ – ime dhammā 
        asaṃkiliṭṭhasaṃkilesikā. 
        Classification into the sabba saṅgaha 
        Following is how the asaṃkiliṭṭha-saṃkilesika realities are categorized in the sabba 
        saṅgaha. 
        Asaṃkiliṭṭha-saṃkilesika dhammas are categorized, 
        (1). In 5 khandhas. 
        (2). In 12 āyatanas. 
        (3). In 12 dhātus. 
        (4). In 1 saccas.  
        Asaṅkiliṭṭhāsaṅkilesikā Dhammā 
        The intrinsic meaning and the etymological meaning of the term asaṅkiliṭṭha
        asaṅkilesika are as follows. 
        Sabhavattha  
        The intrinsic meaning of the term asaṅkiliṭṭha-asaṅkilesika should be understood 
        according to the explanations given for the words asaṅkiliṭṭha and asaṅkilesika. 
        That is, the intrinsic meaning consists of all the following characteristics: not being 
        afflicted by defilements, not being tormented by them, not being corrupted or stained 
        by them, and having transcended the defilements (avibādhita-anupatāpita-avidūsita
        saṅkilesātikkamanaṭṭha). 
        Abhidhānattha  
        “Asaṅkiliṭṭhā ca te asaṅkilesikā cāti asaṅkiliṭṭhāsaṅkilesikā” – “They are 
        asaṅkiliṭṭha, and they are also asaṅkilesika. Therefore, they are called asaṅkiliṭṭha
        asaṅkilesikā.” 
        Dabbattha 
        The ultimate referent of the expression asaṅkiliṭṭha-asaṅkilesikā dhammā is as 
        follows. 
        (1). 8 lokuttara cittas 
        (2). Their associated 36 cetasikas (except 2 appamaññā) 
        (3). Nibbāna 
        6 
        Dabbattha saṃvaṇṇanā of the term asaṃkiliṭṭha-asaṃkilesikā 
        dhammā according to the aṭṭhakathā kaṇḍa of the Dhammasaṅganī 
        ca 
        Katame dhammā asaṃkiliṭṭhaasaṃkilesikā? Cattāro maggā apariyāpannā, 
        cattāri 
        sāmaññaphalāni, 
        asaṃkiliṭṭhaasaṃkilesikā. 
        nibbānañca 
        Classification into the sabba saṅgaha – 
        ime 
        dhammā 
        Following is how the asaṃkiliṭṭha-asaṃkilesika realities are categorized in the sabba 
        saṅgaha. 
        Asaṃkiliṭṭha-asaṃkilesika dhammas are categorized, 
        (1). Some in 4 khandhas. Some are freed from khandhas. 
        (2). In 2 āyatanas. 
        (3). In 2 dhātus. 
        (4). Some in 2 saccas. Some are freed from saccas.

<a id="org95496d9"></a>

## 6. Vitakkatika


<a id="org24442b8"></a>

### Pāḷi

-   **savitakkasavicārā dhammā**: realities that associate vitakka and vicāra
-   **avitakkavicāramattā dhammā**: realities that associate only vicāra without vitakka
-   **avitakkaavicārā dhammā**: realities that do not associate either


<a id="orgeb85194"></a>

### meaning

[

-   **savitakkasavicārā dhammā**: non-self realities that associate vitakka and vicāra
-   **avitakkavicāramattā dhammā**: non-self realities that do not associate vitakka but do vicāra
    -   why not just vicāramattā? because vicāra can only be the mariyādā when vitakka is absent
    -   matta:
        -   merely, only vicāra (with regard to vitakka vicāra)
            -   not with regard to other realities
            -   vicāra is the lower limit (mariyādā), grossest factors
        -   subtle, in the sense of being the subtlest vicāra possible
    -   MN-upakkilesasutta: savitakkasavicāra samādhi, avitakkavicāramatta samādhi, avitakkaavicāra samādhi, sappītika, nippītika, satthasahagata samādhi, upekkhāsahagata samādhi
    -   5 samādhis under the bodhi tree (lokiya); maggas based on the 4-fold jhāna classification
-   **avitakkaavicārā dhammā**: non-self realities that do not associate either

]


<a id="org34537de"></a>

    -   tikamuttaka vicāra is savitakka avicārā dhammā
    -   vitakka
        -   of the 1st pada goes to the 2nd pada
    -   vicāra
        -   of the 1st pada goes to muttaka
        -   of the 2nd pada goes to 3rd pada
    -   here total number of ct is ok
    -   vitakka is **avitakkavicāramatta**
    -   vicāra in avitakkavicāramatta ct is **avitakkaavicāra** (3rd pada)

    adj. keeping the rules; sticking to the boundaries; staying within limits [mariyādā + a] ~
    fem. boundary; border; limit ✓

### extra notes

-   nippadesa teachings: abhidhamma includes all dhammas such as 5-fold jhānas
-   **vit vs. vic**

-   self doctrine accepts self-support; buddhism does not accept a reality that supports itself
-   parāmāsaduka:
    -   it's diṭṭhi
    -   sampayutta and vippayuta
    -   diṭṭhi is neither parāmāsasampayutta nor vippayutta
-   indirect self-support: one dhamma cannot support itself, but it support another dhamma that supports it (research)


<a id="org567f0ba"></a>

## 7. Pītittika


<a id="orgd99ef6a"></a>

### Pāḷi

-   **pītisahagatā dhammā**
-   **sukhasahagatā dhammā**
-   **upekkhāsahagatā dhammā**


<a id="org554d660"></a>

### meaning

[

-   **pītisahagatā dhammā**: realities associated with pīti (does not include pīti itself)
-   **sukhasahagatā dhammā**: = sukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā
-   **upekkhāsahagatā dhammā**: = adukkhamasukhāya vedanāya sampayuttā dhammā

]  

1.  pīti

    abhidhānattha
    
    -   **piṇayatīti**
        -   that which is exceedingly pleasing
        -   that which develops (inflating or uplifting) both mind and body 
            -   the mind and body that are inflated by pīti are like a blossomed lotus
        -   prmdpn: pinayati kāyacittaṃ tappeti, tuṭṭhaṃ suhitaṃ janeti, supupphitapadumaṃ viya vā vaḍḍhetīti pīti
    -   **pinanti (vā) etāya taṃ samaṅgino puṇṇacandoviya virocamānakāyacittā hontīti pīti**
        -   that by owing to which a person who possesses it becomes radiant in body and mind like a full moon, due to that very thing
    
    sabhāvattha
    
    -   **sampiyāyanaṭṭhena pīti**: characteristic: sampiyāyaṇalakkhaṇā pīti &#x2013; being intensely pleasing, extremely happy, intense joy
    -   **pīṇanaṭṭhena pīti**: function: kāyacittapīṇanarasā pīti &#x2013; uplifting the body and mind
        -   cdvml (abhidharmaye mūlika karuṇu)
            -   a kind of swelling or inflation
            -   like heated air arising from the bottom of a pot of rice, causing the water to boil over. Thus, the inflation caused by pīti also occurs in the body. The mind and body inflated by pīti are like a blossomed lotus, as stated in the Paramatthadīpanī, “Pinayati kāyacittaṃ tappeti, tuṭṭhaṃ suhitaṃ janeti, supupphitapadumaṃ viya vā vaḍḍhetīti pīti.” Furthermore, it is mentioned in the same text (Abhidharmaye Mūlika Karuṇu) that pīti generates a kind of forceful air throughout the body, causing expansion. Due to this, the body of one who possesses pīti becomes inflated. Like waves arising in the ocean, laughter arises due to this uplifting energy produced by pīti-associated consciousness. It is also stated that pīti can cause the body to rise up and even move or dance.
    -   **pharaṇaṭṭhena pīti**: function: pharaṇarasā pīti &#x2013; pervading the entire body with refined (excellent) sensations
    -   **haṭṭhapahaṭṭhākāraṭṭhena pīti**: characteristic: delighting in the object
        -   dhsgn-a: pubbabhāgārammaṇe haṭṭhapahaṭṭhākārā pīti – pīti is the joyful and elated mode toward the preliminary object
            -   pīti delights in the object and becomes joyful. this is also evident from the fact that pīti is a factor of jhāna. jhāna factors are those dhammas that exert an intensified effort in grasping the object. thus, pīti is a miscellaneous mental factor (pakinnaka cetasika) which exists with intense delight and uplifts both body and mind. this is the ultimate representation (dabbattha) of the term “pīti.”

2.  sukha

    discussed under vedanāttika

3.  upekkhā

    abhidheyyattha
    
    -   **sukhadukkhākārappavattiṃ upekkhatīti upekkhā, majjhattākārasaṇṭhitattā tenākārena pavattatīti attho**
        -   that which observes the processes of pleasure and pain with neutrality
        -   it proceeds in that neutral manner since this feeling is well established in a neutral mode
        -   upekkhati: to remain indifferent
            -   implies that even when experiencing an object, there is neither inclination toward its pleasant aspect nor toward its unpleasant aspect, nor toward the experience of either. that is, there is neither attraction towards nor repulsion from those aspects. [“upa + √ikkha + a > upekkha + ā > upekkhā”]
    -   **upetā yuttā sukhadukkhānaṃ aviruddhā ikkhā anubhavanaṃ upekkhā**: the observation (tasting) or experience that is compatible and non-opposed to both pleasure and pain
        -   upetā / upapatti: appropriate, fitting, thus explained as “yuttā.”
            -   appropriate: being compatible with both pleasant and painful feelings without opposing them
    
    -   ikkhā: seeing, but here tasting
    
    sabhāvattha: anubhavanaṃ (experiencing)
    
    non-opposition to pleasure and pain: it occurs subsequent to both pleasant and painful feelings
    
    -   it exists between them (byavadhāyikabhūta > vi + ava + √dhā)
    -   it is not opposed to them
    -   pleasure and pain are opposed to each other, do not occur immediately one after the other in succession
    
    -   upapattito ikkhatīti upekkhā\*: that which sees (experiences) impartially, without falling to one side
        -   mainly used for the upekkhā of the 3rd jhāna (*:q: tatramajjhattatā?*)
        -   some: also apply it to upekkhā vedanā
            -   but not all because when with unwholesome does not involve true impartiality
    
    **sabhāvattha**
    
    -   **nāti-upabrūhana-milāpanaṭṭhena upekkhā**: function: neither excessively uplift nor cause the withering of the associated dhammas
    -   all intrinsic meanings of adukkhamasukha vedanā should be understood as the intrinsic meanings of upekkhā vedanā
    
    **dabbattha**
    vedanā (sabbacitta sādhāraṇa cetasika): what tastes the object in a neutral manner

4.  sahagata

    When a dhamma or a group of dhammas arises together with another dhamma in the 
    mode of having the same arising (ekuppāda), having the same cessation (ekanirodha), 
    having the same object (ekārammaṇa) and having the same base (ekavatthuka), that 
    dhamma or group of dhammas is said to be “sahagata” with that other dhamma. 
    For example, a group of dhammas that arises together with pīti is called “pīti 
    sahagata.” This means dhammas that are associated (sampayutta) and conjoined 
    (saṃsaṭṭha) with pīti. 
    Now, let us discuss the terms of the pīti triad individually. 

5.  2 purposes for preaching the triad

    -   1: to distinguish nippīti sukha from sappīti sukha 
        -   If dhammas associated with pīti (pīti sahagata dhamma) are also dhammas associated
    
    with sukha (sukha sahagata), then by the term “pīti sahagata” the sukha sahagata 
    dhammas would already be implied. Therefore, it would not be necessary to state 
    “sukha sahagata.” Likewise, if “sukha sahagata” is stated, there would be no need to 
    state “pīti sahagata.” 
    In that case, the triad would become a dyad. To complete the triad, the term 
    “dukkhasahagata” would have to be introduced. Then this triad would become 
    identical to the triad of feeling. 
    However, when “pīti sahagata” is stated and then “sukha sahagata” is stated again, 
    it shows that: “There exists a sukha that is dissociated from pīti.” Thus, that sukha is 
    distinguished from sukha associated with pīti. Sukha dissociated from pīti (nippīti 
    sukha) refers to the sukha of the fourth jhāna (or the third jhāna in the fourfold 
    system), and the sukha associated with kaya viññāṇa (body consciousness). 
    Understand this idea as follows. Not only pītisahagata dhammas are sukhasahagata 
    (pītisahagatāyeva na sukhasahagatā). Not all sukhasahagata dhammas are 
    pītisahagata (sukhasahagatāpi na pītisahagatā eva). Therefore, pītisahagata 
    dhammas and sukhasahagata dhammas are distinct (bhinna) dhammas. 
    Thus, although sukhasahagata dhammas are mostly associated with pīti, there are 
    cases where a group of dhammas is associated with sukha but not associated with pīti. 
    That sukha is called nippītisukha. 
    This distinction is shown by this triad. 
    
    -   2: to show the difference (nānatta) between pīti and sukha
        -   Among these two, in the term “pītisahagata”, a portion (ekadesa) of sukha is
    
    included, but pīti itself is not included. On the other hand, in the term 
    “sukhasahagata”, all pīti (sabbapīti) is included, but sukha itself is not included. 
    Furthermore sukha-sahagata dhammas that are dissociated from pīti are not included 
    under “pīti-sahagata,” but they are included under “sukha-sahagata.” 
    By this, the difference between pīti and sukha is made clear. 
    Thus, the term pīti-sahagata includes a partial inclusion (sāvasesa) of sukha. The term 
    sukha-sahagata includes a complete inclusion (niravasesa) of pīti. 
    Among these two dhammas, pīti-sahagata dhamma is sukha and sukha-sahagata 
    dhamma is pīti. Although pīti and sukha are associated (saṃsaṭṭha) with each other, 
    they are included under two different terms rather than a single term. By this, their 
    distinctness is understood. 
    
    **Additional View**
    
    -   some: to distinguish the 4th jhāna sukha as extremely refined (atipaṇīta sukha) by separating it from coarse factors (olārika aṅga), thereby showing its refined nature
    
    However, since all dhammas associated with sukha-vedanā are included under the 
    term “sukha-sahagata,” this interpretation requires further examination. 
    The reason is, inn the Niddesa of the Dhammasaṅgaṇī, it is stated, “sukhabhūmiyaṃ 
    kāmāvacare”, and in the Aṭṭhakathā section, it is stated, “kāmāvacarakusalato cattāro 
    somanassa-sahagata-cittuppādā”, etc.

<a id="org8beb538"></a>

## 8. Dassanattika


<a id="org752085c"></a>

### Pāḷi

-   **Dassanenapahātabbā dhammā**
-   **Bhāvanāyapahātabbā dhammā**
-   **Nevadassanena na bhāvanāya pahātabbā dhammā**


<a id="org0046034"></a>

### meaning

[

-   **dassanenapahātabbā dhammā**: non-self realities that are abandoned by the path of stream-entry which is called dassana
    -   **dassana**: sotāpattimagga &#x2013; 8 path factors or entire cittuppāda
    -   **pahātabba**: should, must, ought to (be) abandon(ed) &#x2013; akusalas
        -   kilesas / akusalas
            -   appahīna akusala

-   **bhāvanāyapahātabbā dhammā**: non-self realities that are abandoned by the higher three supramundane paths which are called bhāvanā
    -   **bhāvanā**: 3 higher maggas &#x2013; path factors or cittuppādas
-   **nevadassanena-nabhāvanāya-pahātabbā dhammā**: non-self realities that are not abandoned by the path of stream entry or higher three supramundane paths

1.  dassanenapahātabbā dhammā

    **abhidhānattha and sabhāvattha** 
    
    -   **Nibbānaṃ paṭhamaṃ passatīti dassanaṃ**: that which sees Nibbāna for the first time is called dassana &#x2013; sotāpatti magga

    dassanena pahātabba: only the general term “dassana” is heard (without specification), and because the commentary distinguishes between seeing (dassana) and the performing the tast that ought to be performed (kattabbakicca-karaṇa)
    
    -   dassana includes the sāmañña and the visesa dassana
        -   sāmañña is taking as object, which may or may not include the visesana
        -   visesana is penetration, which includes taking as object
    
    paṭivedha: seeing Nibbāna and simultaneously performing the functions of pariññādi
    
    -   both actions cannot be separated because without the functions, there is no penetration
    
    -   paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti: having seen with wisdom, his taints are destroyed
    
    Two functions that occur at the same time, that is, functions of identical temporal occurrence, may be expressed as though they occur at different times. This is called the method of “figurative separation” (bhedopacāra naya).
    
    -   two things happening at the same time but expressed separately
    
    In the commentary statement, “The path of stream-entry is called ‘dassana’ because it first sees Nibbāna,” the meaning intended is seeing Nibbāna in the sense of taking it as an object. However, what is meant here is not a mere general seeing. It is a seeing that performs the task called the abandoning of defilements. Therefore, this is a “special vision.”
    
    2 Nibbāna dassanas
    
    -   **sāmañña dassana** (general vision) – mere taking as object
    -   **visesa dassana** (special vision) – taking Nibbāna as object while also performing functions such as abandoning defilements and full understanding; the one meant in the comm.
    
    another explanation:
    
    -   explanation of why the stream-entry path is called dassana can also be given. The “Dhammacakkhu” refers to the three lower paths: the path of stream-entry, the path of once-returning, and the path of non-returning. When the path arises three times, the second and third occurrences are to be understood as repeated arising and development. Therefore, those two are called bhāvanā. The Dhammacakkhu that has not yet become developed through repeated arising is called dassana. The Dhammacakkhu, by performing the four path-functions such as full understanding, is able to see the Four Noble Truths, and thus it is superior to the gotrabhū consciousness. Therefore, in the expression “dassanena pahātabbā,” the gotrabhū consciousness does not receive the designation dassana. The dhammas that are to be abandoned by the path of stream-entry mentioned above are called “dassanena pahātabbā” – “dassanena pahātabbā ti dassanena-pahātabbā.”
        This is a tatiyā tappurisa samāsa of which the case ending has not been elided.

2.  bhāvanāyapahātabbā dhammā

    1.  abhidhānattha and sabhāvattha
    
        **bhāvetīti bhāvanā**: that which causes growth or develops (or cultivates) 7
        
        **bhāvanāvasena uppajjati, adiṭṭhapubbaṃ kiñci na passati, tasmā bhāvanāti vuccati**: 8 the three higher paths arise by way of development (bhāvanā) in regard to the dhamma (noble truths) that has already been seen by the first path, namely the path of stream-entry; and since they do not see anything new that has not been previously seen, they are called bhāvanā. the dhammas that are to be abandoned by means of those three higher paths mentioned above are called “bhāvanāyapahātabbā” – “bhāvanāya pahātabbāti bhāvanāyapahātabbā.” this is a tatiyā tappurisa samāsa of which the case ending has not been elided 
    
    2.  dabbattha
    
        bhāvanāyapahātabbā dhammā
        (1). 4 diṭṭhigata vippayutta cittas connected with gross kāmarāga (that does not lead to 4 apāya), 2 dosamūla cittas connected with gross paṭigha (that does not lead to 4 apāya), their associated 25 cetasikas (except diṭṭhi and vicikicchā), which are eradicated by the sakadāgāmī magga
        (2). 4 diṭṭhigata vippayutta cittas connected with subtle kāmarāga, 2 dosamūla cittas connected with subtle paṭigha, their associated 25 cetasikas (except diṭṭhi and vicikicchā), which are eradicated by the anāgāmī magga
        (3). 4 diṭṭhigata vippayutta cittas connected with rūpa raga and arūpa rāga, uddhacca sampayutta citta, their associated 21 cetasikas (except diṭṭhi, dosa, issā, macchariyha, kukkucca and vicikicchā), which are eradicated by the arahatta magga

3.  nevadassanena-nabhāvanāya-pahātabbā dhammā

    1.  abhidhānattha and sabhāvattha
    
        nevadasanenanabhāvanāyapahātabba: negation of the dhammas that are to be abandoned, as expressed in the above two terms. It should not be understood as negating the abandoning 
        dhammas themselves – namely, dassana and bhāvanā – by means of the two negative particles “na.” If it were interpreted in that way, it would imply that there exist dhammas, other than dassana and
        
        bhāvanā, which perform abandonment by way of eradication (samuccheda pahāna).
        
        In that case, the meaning of the term “nevadasanenanabhāvanāyapahātabba” would be dhammas that are to be abandoned by some other abandoning dhammas, aprart from dassana or bhāvanā.
        
        It should be understood that apart from the path of stream-entry denoted by the term dassana and the three higher paths denoted by the term bhāvanā, there are no other dhammas that perform abandonment by way of eradication.
        
        It should also be understood that defilements abandoned by suppression (vikkhambhana pahāna) arise again; therefore, even the concentrated minds associated with access concentration (upacāra samādhi), the exalted consciousness (jhāna cittas), and even insight consciousness (vipassana citta) are not dhammas that abandon by way of eradication.
        
        Furthermore, what is included under this third term are dhammas that are not to be abandoned.
        
        Dhammas that are to be abandoned are not included therein.
        
        Therefore, what is meant by this term is dhammas other than those that are to be abandoned by dassana and those that are to be abandoned by bhāvanā.9 The analysis of the term is as follows:
        
        “Na eva dassanena pahātabbā > nevadasanenapahātabbā; na bhāvanāya pahātabbā > 
        nabhāvanāyapahātabbā; nevadasanenapahātabbā nabhāvanāyapahātabbā > 
        nevadasanenanabhāvanāyapahātabbā.” 
        
        The meaning of this is dhammas that are not to be abandoned either by dassana or by bhāvanā – that is, all dhammas other than unwholesome dhammas. 
        
        In this third term of the triad, the word pahātabba (“to be abandoned”) is connected not only with “na 
        bhāvanāya” but also with the preceding term “nevadasanena.” 
        
        Although expressions such as “dassanena pahātabbā” are explained as single terms in the Mātikā in terms of their essential meaning, in reality these are not compounds but sentences.
        
        This is stated in the Atthasālinī Bhāsāṭīkā.10

4.  pahātabba akusala

    1.  pahāna
    
        **pahāna** (abandonment): causing a dhamma not to arise (bringing it to non-arising)
        
        -   also called vimutti 11, parinibbāna 12
        
        -   2 types
            -   **temporary**
                -   **tadaṅga pahāna / vimutti / parinibbāna**: making and akusala not to arise by means of its opposing factor
                -   **vikkhambhana pahāna / vimutti / parinibbāna**: making an akusala not to arise by means of upacāra and appanā samādhi (so long as that concentration does not decline)
            -   **permanent**: making an unwholesome dhamma permanently not arise
                -   **samuccheda pahāna / vimutti / parinibbāna**
        
        By abandoning desire and lust (chanda rāga) toward a given dhamma and thereby making that dhamma never arise again is called “nissaraṇappahāna,” “nissaraṇa vimutti,” and “(nissaraṇa) parinibbāna.” 
        
        When unwholesome dhammas are made temporarily not to arise, what is abandoned is the manifest occurrence/obsession stage (pariyuṭṭhānappavatti) of the unwholesome.
        
        When unwholesome dhammas are made permanently not to arise, the underlying tendency/latent tendency (anusayappavatti) is abandoned, and through that, the manifest occurrence/stage of obsession is also abandoned automatically. 
        
        Among these two types of abandonment, what is relevant to this triad is the making of dhammas permanently non-arising. 
        
        This can be done in two ways: 
        (1). Directly abandoning a dhamma by means of opposing dhammas, that is, making it not arise. 
        
        -   To directly abandon a dhamma by opposing dhammas means that wholesome dhammas abandon unwholesome dhammas. Thus, un-abandoned unwholesome dhammas are removed by the wholesome dhammas opposed to them. Rāgo pahīyati, doso pahīyati, moho pahīyati, rāgappahānāya dhammaṃ deseti, dosappahānāya dhammaṃ deseti, mohappahānāya dhammaṃ deseti. Lust is abandoned, hatred is abandoned, delusion is abandoned; the Dhamma is taught for the abandoning of lust, for the abandoning of hatred, for the abandoning of delusion. Statements such as these convey this meaning.
        
        (2). Abandoning a dhamma by removing desire and lust toward it, thereby making it not arise. 
        
        -   Removing desire and lust toward a dhamma is itself the abandoning of that dhamma. For example, removing desire and lust toward form (rūpa) is the abandoning of form; removing desire and lust toward feeling (vedanā) is the abandoning of feeling. What occurs thereby is that the forms that would have arisen due to desire and lust toward form do not arise.
            -   Yo, bhikkhave, rūpasmiṃ chandarāgo taṃ pajahatha. Evaṃ taṃ rūpaṃ pahīnaṃ bhavissati ucchinnamūlaṃ tālāvatthukataṃ anabhāvaṃkataṃ āyatiṃ anuppādadhammaṃ. Yo vedanāya chandarāgo taṃ pajahatha. Evaṃ sā vedanā pahīnā bhavissati ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā. Yo saññāya chandarāgo taṃ pajahatha. Evaṃ sā saññā pahīnā bhavissati ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā. Yo saṅkhāresu chandarāgo taṃ pajahatha. Evaṃ te saṅkhārā pahīnā bhavissanti ucchinnamūlā tālāvatthukatā anabhāvaṃkatā āyatiṃ anuppādadhammā. Yo viññāṇasmiṃ chandarāgo taṃ pajahatha. Evaṃ taṃ viññāṇaṃ pahīnaṃ bhavissati ucchinnamūlaṃ tālāvatthukataṃ anabhāvaṃkataṃ āyatiṃ anuppādadhammaṃ.

Yaṃ pana phalakkhaṇe paṭippassaddhattaṃ kilesānaṃ, ayaṃ paṭippassaddhivimutti nāma. Sabbasaṅkhatanissaṭattā pana sabbasaṅkhāravimuttaṃ nibbānaṃ, ayaṃ nissaraṇavimutti nāma. (Ud-a. 30.).

    3.  dhammas that can be abandoned
    
        What is abandoned by the supramundane path are un-abandoned defilements (appahīna kilesa), that is, 
        defilements not yet brought to non-arising by the path. These are of two types: 
        (1). Strong un-abandoned defilements (thāmagata appahīna kilesa) – defilements that, due to 
        repeated occurrence, have gained strength and can arise when conditions are present. These are 
        also called anusaya (latent tendencies). There are seven such latent tendencies. 
        (2). Weak un-abandoned defilements (athāmagata appahīna kilesa) – defilements not strengthened, 
        not yet abandoned by the path. These include unwholesome dhammas such as moral 
        shamelessness (ahirika), moral fearlessness (anottappa), restlessness (uddhacca), envy (issā), 
        stinginess (macchariya), remorse (kukkucca), etc. Although these are un-abandoned 
        defilements, they are not designated as anusaya. 
        Un-abandoned unwholesome dhammas are those that lie dormant in the continuum and follow the 
        continuum. They persist in the continuum even when wholesome and indeterminate consciousness 
        arise. However, they can arise when suitable conditions are present. Therefore, they are also conventionally called “uppanna kilesa” (arisen defilements). When conditions arise, un-abandoned defilements arise together with javana cittas. 
        
        These un-abandoned defilements are removed by path-knowledge (maggañāṇa). That defilement is not past (atīta), future (anāgata), or present (paccuppanna); it is regarded as **bhūmiladdhuppanna**
        
        -   bhūmi: khandhas (ground for kilesas to exist)
            -   to be so, they must be "not completely comprehended" (apariññāta khandha)
                -   if pariññāta, not a base for kilesa
        -   laddha:
            -   obtain as object (not the case)
            -   remain uneradicated depending on the bhūmi
        -   uppanna: things that could arise, supposed to arise, possible to arise (not present)
        -   not past, future, or present, but in a different stage / state
        -   they are not paññatti; not paramattha in another sense; prmdpn: paramatthajātika
        
        -   how they are eradicated: a tree that has the potency to bear fruit;  when the tree is destroyed, those fruits that would have appeared, are "removed"; done so by completely comprehending khandhas
        -   pariññāta khandha: future live's pariññāta khandhas are also "eradicated", brought into non-existence
            -   abīja: not a seed for kilesa (cause for kilesa)
        -   apariññāta khandha: kilesabīja
        
        However, since it is a defilement that can arise when conditions are present, it is also designated as “future (anāgata).” This matter is clarified in the Abhisamayakathā.
    
    4.  abhisamaya kathā
    
        Abhisamayakathā (Paññāvagga of the Paṭisambhidāmagga)
        What is shown there is the abandonment of defilements carried out by the supramundane path. 
        
        Yvāyaṃ kilese pajahati, atīte kilese pajahati…pe… anāgate kilese pajahati, paccuppanne kilese pajahati, atīte kilese pajahatīti. Hañci atīte kilese pajahati, tena hi khīṇaṃ khepeti, niruddhaṃ nirodheti, vigataṃ vigameti, atthaṅgataṃ atthaṅgameti, atītaṃ yaṃ na atthi taṃ pajahatīti?
        Na atīte kilese pajahatīti. Anāgate kilese pajahatīti. Hañci anāgate kilese pajahati, tena hi ajātaṃ pajahati, anibbattaṃ pajahati, anuppannaṃ pajahati, apātubhūtaṃ pajahati, anāgataṃ yaṃ na atthi taṃ pajahatīti? Na anāgate kilese pajahatīti. Paccuppanne kilese pajahatīti. Hañci paccuppanne kilese pajahati, tena hi ratto rāgaṃ pajahati, duṭṭho dosaṃ pajahati, mūḷho mohaṃ pajahati, vinibaddho mānaṃ pajahati, parāmaṭṭho diṭṭhiṃ pajahati, vikkhepagato uddhaccaṃ pajahati, aniṭṭhaṅgato vicikicchaṃ pajahati, thāmagato anusayaṃ pajahati, kaṇhasukkadhammā yuganaddhā samameva vattanti, saṃkilesikā maggabhāvanā hoti? Na hi atīte kilese pajahati, na anāgate kilese pajahati, na paccuppanne kilese pajahatīti. 
        Hañci na atīte kilese pajahati, na anāgate…pe… na paccuppanne kilese pajahati, tena hi natthi maggabhāvanā, natthi phalasacchikiriyā, natthi kilesappahānaṃ, natthi dhammābhisamayoti? Atthi maggabhāvanā, atthi phalasacchikiriyā, atthi kilesappahānaṃ, atthi dhammābhisamayo. Yathā kathaṃ viya? Seyyathāpi taruṇo rukkho ajātaphalo. Tamenaṃ puriso mūlaṃ chindeyya. Ye tassa rukkhassa ajātaphalā, te ajātāyeva na jāyanti, anibbattāyeva na nibbattanti, anuppannāyeva na uppajjanti, apātubhūtāyeva na pātubhavanti. Evamevaṃ uppādo hetu, uppādo paccayo kilesānaṃ nibbattiyāti. Uppāde ādīnavaṃ disvā anuppāde cittaṃ pakkhandati. Anuppāde cittassa pakkhandattā ye uppādapaccayā kilesā nibbatteyyuṃ, te ajātāyeva na jāyanti, anibbattāyeva na nibbattanti, anuppannāyeva na uppajjanti, apātubhūtāyeva na pātubhavanti. Evaṃ hetunirodhā dukkhanirodho. Pavattaṃ hetu, nimittaṃ hetu, āyūhanā hetu. Āyūhanā paccayo kilesānaṃ nibbattiyāti. Āyūhane
        ādīnavaṃ disvā anāyūhane cittaṃ pakkhandati. Anāyūhane cittassa pakkhandattā, ye āyūhanapaccayā kilesā nibbatteyyuṃ, te ajātāyeva na jāyanti, anibbattāyeva na nibbattanti, anuppannāyeva na uppajjanti, apātubhūtāyeva na pātubhavanti. Evaṃ hetunirodhā dukkhanirodho. Evaṃ atthi maggabhāvanā, atthi phalasacchikiriyā, atthi kilesappahānaṃ, atthi dhammābhisamayoti.14 
        
        What defilement is abandoned by the supramundane path is explained here
        
        Defilement is of three kinds:
        
        -   past, future, and present
        
        -   if magga abandoned a past defilement, it would abandon something that has already ceased
        -   if magga abandoned a past defilement that has already ceased, then since there is nothing to be abandoned (pahātabba), the path would become a fruitless effort
        -   conclusion: magga does not abandon past defilements
        
        Hañci atīte kilese pajahati, tena hi khīṇaṃ khepeti, niruddhaṃ nirodheti, vigataṃ vigameti, atthaṅgataṃ atthaṅgameti, atītaṃ yaṃ na atthi taṃ pajahatīti? Na atīte kilese pajahatīti 
        
        If it (supramundane path) abandons past defilement, then it would be discarding what is already exhausted, casing to cease what is already ceased, removing what is already gone, bringing to an end what has already ended. Does the supramundane path be abandoning what, being past, does not exist? 
        Therefore, the supramundane path does not abandon past defilement. 
        
        -   if magga abandoned future defilements, it would abandon something that has not yet arisen
        -   since the defilement that will arise in the future does not exist at this moment, it cannot be abandoned.
        -   Since there is nothing to be abandoned (pahātabba), the path would become a fruitless effort
        -   conclusion: Therefore, the path does not abandon future defilement.15
        
        Hañci anāgate kilese pajahati, tena hi ajātaṃ pajahati, anibbattaṃ pajahati, anuppannaṃ pajahati, apātubhūtaṃ pajahati, anāgataṃ yaṃ na atthi taṃ pajahatīti? Na anāgate kilese pajahatīti. 
        
        If it (supramundane path) abandons future defilement, then it would be abandoning what is unborn, what has not come into being, what has not arisen, what has not appeared. Does the supramundane path be abandoning what, being future, does not exist? Therefore, the supramundane path does not abandon future defilement. 
        
        If the path abandons present defilement, then two problems arise. 
        
        -   (1). Since no real abandonment would occur, the path would become a fruitless effort.
        
        If the path abandons a presently existing dhamma, then abandonment would have to occur while the thing to be abandoned still exists. If abandonment has already taken place, how can the dhamma to be abandoned still exist? On the other hand, if the dhamma to be abandoned still exists, how can abandonment have occurred? The ideas “the thing to be abandoned exists” and “abandonment has occurred” mutually negate one another; they are contradictory (vippaṭisiddha). Why? Because while the dhamma to be abandoned exists, abandonment does not occur. Therefore, the effort appropriate to that abandonment (tadupaga vāyāma) would be fruitless. This is like the fruitlessness of light if light and darkness were to coexist in the same place.16 
        
        -   (2). Since a defilement would be present when the path arises, the cultivation of the path would be associated with defilements; it would be defiled (saṅkilesika).(at times, sankilesika means defiled, sankilittha)
            If the path abandons present defilement, then one attached by lust would be abandoning lust. One corrupted by hatred would be abandoning hatred. One deluded by moha would be abandoning moha. One whose mind is tightened, that is, one who is vinibaddha, would be

II. 498.).
        
        abandoning conceit. One who is parāmaṭṭha, one who has grasped tightly, would be 
        abandoning wrong view. One who has gone into distraction would be abandoning restlessness. 
        One who has not reached certainty would be abandoning doubt. One in whom defilements have 
        arisen in the continuum and become firm, that is, one possessing an anusaya that has become strong, would be abandoning anyusaya [Here, anusaya refers to the defilements that have arisen in the mind]. (can be used to refer to latent and the arisen dflm)
        
        Unwholesome (kaṇha) and wholesome (sukka) dhammas would coexist yoked together equally. The cultivation of the supramundane path would become tainted by defilements (saṅkilesika). Therefore, the path does not abandon present defilement. 
        
        Hañci paccuppanne kilese pajahati, tena hi ratto rāgaṃ pajahati, duṭṭho dosaṃ pajahati, mūḷho mohaṃ pajahati, vinibaddho mānaṃ pajahati, parāmaṭṭho diṭṭhiṃ pajahati, vikkhepagato uddhaccaṃ pajahati, aniṭṭhaṅgato vicikicchaṃ pajahati, thāmagato anusayaṃ pajahati, kaṇhasukkadhammā yuganaddhā samameva vattanti, saṃkilesikā maggabhāvanā hoti? Na hi atīte kilese pajahati, na anāgate kilese pajahati, na paccuppanne kilese pajahatīti 
        
        If it (the supramundane path) abandons present defilement, then would it be that one attached would be abandoning lust, one corrupt would be abandoning hatred, one deluded would be abandoning delusion, one tightened would be abandoning conceit, one grasped strongly would be abandoning wrong view, one gone into distraction would be abandoning restlessness, one not arrived at certainty would be abandoning doubt, one in whom defilements has become strong would be abandoning the defilements; black and white dhammas would proceed yoked together evenly, and the cultivation of the path would be tainted by defilements? Therefore, the supramundane path does not abandon past defilement, it does not abandon future defilement, and it does not abandon present defilement. 
        
        Moreover, if one were still to maintain that the path abandons present defilement, then it would have to be said that it abandons a “citta vippayutta kilesa,” a defilement dissociated from consciousness.
        
        Then, as in the view of the Sarvāstivādins, who teach citta vippayutta saṅkhāra, present defilement would become something dissociated from consciousness.
        
        An immaterial dhamma is present only when they share the same base (vatthu) and so on with the co arising mental realities, and belong to the three moments of genesis, existing and vanishing.
        
        How then could they be dissociated from consciousness (citta vippayutta)? They could not. Therefore, there is no such thing as the consciousness-dissociated state of defilements. There is no presently existing defilement dissociated from consciousness. This is an objection not explicitly raised in the text. Therefore, it should be understood that the path does not abandon past, future, or present defilement. Defilements are three-time entities. Further, if the path does not abandon past, future, or present defilement, then the question arises: is there no cultivation of the supramundane path (magga bhāvanā), since there is no task for the supramundane path to accomplish? Is there no realization (or attainement) of the supramundane fruit (phala sacchikiriyā)? Is there no abandonment of defilements (kilesappahāna)? Is (dhammābhisamaya)? there complete comprehension (penetration) of Noble Truths The answer to that is: there is cultivation of the supramundane path. There is realization of the supramundane fruit. There is abandonment of defilements. There is penetration of the Four Noble Truths. How is that? There is a young tree bearing un-arisen fruit [A tree in which fruit could arise but has not 
        arisen yet]. A man cuts that tree at its root. Whatever fruits of that tree had not yet born, they do not 
        come into birth being in the state of unborn. Whatever fruits had not yet arisen do not arise being in the 
        state of not arisen. Whatever fruits had not yet appeared do not appear being in the state of not appeared. 
        In the same way, the cause of the arising of defilements is uppāda – that is, the aggregates associated 
        with arising. Seeing the danger in uppāda, the yogi’s mind leaps into (pakkhandati) anuppāda (non
        arising), which is Nibbāna. When the mind enters non-arising, those defilements that would arise 
        because of uppāda do not come into birth being in the state of unborn. They do not arise being in the 
        state of not arisen. They do not appear being in the state of not appeared. 
        10 
        Likewise, pavatti (existence in succession), nimitta (conditioned realities), and āyūhana (volition which 
        exert effort for future rebirth) are causes for the arising of defilements. Seeing the danger in pavatti, 
        nimitta, and āyūhana, the yogi’s mind leaps respectively into appavatti (state opposite of the existence 
        in succession), animitta (unconditioned reality), and anāyūhana (state of not exerting for future rebirth), 
        which is Nibbāna. When the mind enters appavatti, animitta, and anāyūhana, those defilements that 
        would arise because of pavatti, nimitta and āyūhana do not come into birth being in the state of unborn. 
        They do not arise being in the state of not arisen. They do not appear being in the state of not appeared. 
        Here, the above uppāda, pavatti, nimitta, and āyūhana are to be understood in relation to the present 
        existence.17 
        Just as, when the root of a young tree bearing un-arisen fruit [a tree in which fruit could arise but has 
        not arisen yet] is not cut, the fruits that were supposed to arise would arise. But when the root is cut, 
        they do not arise and are said to be destroyed. Even so, when the supramundane path is cultivated, the 
        defilements that were supposed to arise, if cultivation of the path were not accomplished, are said to be 
        “abandoned” because, due to the cultivation of the path, they do not arise. This is the correspondence 
        of the above simile.18 
        Thus, through the cessation of the cause there is the cessation of suffering – “Evaṃ hetunirodhā 
        dukkhanirodho.” That is, by the cessation of the defilements that are the causes of suffering, there is 
        the cessation of the suffering that would otherwise arise in the future. Therefore, there is cultivation of 
        the supramundane path. There is realization of the supramundane fruit. There is abandonment of 
        defilements. There is penetration of Noble Truths. 
        Seyyathāpi taruṇo rukkho ajātaphalo. Tamenaṃ puriso mūlaṃ chindeyya. Ye tassa rukkhassa 
        ajātaphalā, te ajātāyeva na jāyanti, anibbattāyeva na nibbattanti, anuppannāyeva na uppajjanti, 
        apātubhūtāyeva na pātubhavanti. Evamevaṃ uppādo hetu, uppādo paccayo kilesānaṃ nibbattiyāti. 
        Uppāde ādīnavaṃ disvā anuppāde cittaṃ pakkhandati. Anuppāde cittassa pakkhandattā ye 
        uppādapaccayā kilesā nibbatteyyuṃ, te ajātāyeva na jāyanti, anibbattāyeva na nibbattanti, 
        anuppannāyeva na uppajjanti, apātubhūtāyeva na pātubhavanti. Evaṃ hetunirodhā dukkhanirodho. 
        Pavattaṃ hetu, nimittaṃ hetu, āyūhanā hetu. Āyūhanā paccayo kilesānaṃ nibbattiyāti. Āyūhane 
        ādīnavaṃ disvā anāyūhane cittaṃ pakkhandati. Anāyūhane cittassa pakkhandattā, ye āyūhanapaccayā 
        kilesā nibbatteyyuṃ, te ajātāyeva na jāyanti, anibbattāyeva na nibbattanti, anuppannāyeva na 
        uppajjanti, apātubhūtāyeva na pātubhavanti. Evaṃ hetunirodhā dukkhanirodho. Evaṃ atthi 
        maggabhāvanā, atthi phalasacchikiriyā, atthi kilesappahānaṃ, atthi dhammābhisamayoti.19 
        By the above terms uppāda, pavatti, nimitta, and āyūhana is meant the present continuum of aggregates. 
        Whatever path-knowledge arises in a given continuum of aggregates, that continuum of aggregates 
        becomes a non-seed (abīja) with respect to the defilements that are to be abandoned by that particular path-knowledge. Because that continuum of aggregates has become a non-seed, unarisen defilements do not arise based upon it. 
        The continuum of aggregates that has thus been made a non-seed is called “the aggregates that have been fully comprehended” (pariññāta kandha). 
        Seeing the danger in the above uppāda and so forth means seeing the danger in the continuum of aggregates as impermanent and so on. 
        
        Further, the terms anuppāda, appavatti, animitta, and anāyūhana denote the Nibbāna. 
        By “cittaṃ pakkhandati” is meant that the consciousness associated with the supramundane path plunges into Nibbāna. 
        
        17 Uppādapavattanimittāyūhanā paccuppannabhavavasena gahitā. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 499.). 
        18 Evamevāti yathā ajātaphale taruṇarukkhe mūle chinne asati chedane āyatiṃ uppajjanārahāni chedanapaccayā anuppajjamānāni vinaṭṭhāni nāma honti, evameva maggabhāvanāya asati uppajjanārahā kilesā maggabhāvanāya anuppajjamānā "pahīnā"ti vuccantīti upamāsaṃsandanaṃ veditabbaṃ. (Vsm-ṭ. II. 499.). 
        19 Psm. 394-5. 
        
        By the passage “hetunirodhā dukkhanirodho” is meant: by the cessation of the non-arising of the continuum of aggregates, which is the seed of the defilements, there is the cessation of the non-arising of the defilements that are the causes of future suffering, namely future aggregates.
        
        What is meant by this is that, through seeing danger in terms of impermanence and so on, and through 
        plunging into Nibbāna, the aggregates that are the seed of defilements are made seedless, and thereby 
        un-arisen defilements are made not to arise.
        
        Thus, by the cessation of the non-arising of the defilements that are the causes of suffering, there is the 
        cessation of the non-arising of suffering.
        
        Because in this way the the abandonment of defilements is justifiable (reasonable), it is taught in the 
        Paṭisambhidāmagga: “There is cultivation of the supramundane path.
        
        There is realization of the 
        supramundane fruit.
        
        There is abandonment of defilements. There is penetration of the Four Noble 
        Truths.” 
        What is made clear by the above teaching? What is made clear by it is the abandonment of bhūmiladdha 
        kilesa. If one asks, “Are bhūmiladdha kilesas past, future, or present?” the answer should be they are 
        bhūmiladdhuppanna.20 
        20 "Etena kiṃ dīpitaṃ hoti? Bhūmiladdhānaṃ kilesānaṃ pahānaṃ dīpitaṃ hoti. Bhūmiladdhā pana kiṃ atītā 
        anāgatā, udāhu paccuppannāti? Bhūmiladdhuppannāyeva nāmā"ti vatvā kathitakilesappahānassa vitthārakathā 
        sutamayañāṇakathāya maggasaccaniddesavaṇṇanāyaṃ vuttanayeneva veditabbā, idha pana maggañāṇena 
        pahātabbā kilesāyeva adhippetāti. (Psm-a. II. 305.). 
        12

            -   from apariññāta to apariññāta khandha:
                -   what changes is the knowledge, not the khandhas
                -   but the ñāṇa is part of the khandha, so the khandhas do "change"

5.  4 uppannas

    -   **vattamānuppanna**: a dhamma that has reached the stages of arising (uppāda), existing (ṭhiti), and dissolution (bhaṅga) is called vattamānuppanna. by this, present dhammas are meant
    -   **bhutvāpagatuppanna**: Wholesome and unwholesome dhammas that have tasted the flavor of the object and then ceased are called bhutvāpagatuppanna
        -   Although all cittas involve tasting, wholesome and unwholesome dhammas have a more intense tasting of the object than indeterminate cittas, because they exist based on cittābhisaṅkhāra in a continuum accompanied by distortion (vipallāsa). When mentalities such as lust, etc., and their mentalities take the object having thought (vikappagāha), unwholesome mental clusters and wholesome mental clusters specially taste the object.
        -   On the other hand, resultant dhammas do not experience the taste of the object in that way because they are produced by the force of kamma. Rootless functional cittas do not experience the taste of the object in that way because they are extremely weak. Rooted functional cittas arise in Arahants endowed with the six-factored equanimity (chaḷaṅgupekkhā) in whom the taints have been destroyed, and so they are nāma dhammas of extremely peaceful occurrence. Therefore, they too do not taste the flavor of the object in the way wholesome and unwholesome dhammas do.1 In addition, the remaining conditioned dhammas of the past, which had reached the three moments of arising (uppāda), existing (ṭhiti), and dissolution (bhaṅga) and have ceased, are also called bhutvāpagatuppanna. By this, past dhammas are meant.
    -   **okāsakatuppanna**: past kamma, performed previously, which, having obstructed another result, has made room (opportunity) for its own result and 1Satipi sabbesampi cittuppādānaṃ saṃvedayitasabhāvā ārammaṇānubhavane savipallāse pana santāne cittābhisaṅkhāravasena pavattito abyākatehi visiṭṭho kusalākusalānaṃ sātisayo visayānubhavanākāro. Yathā vikappaggāhavasena rāgādīhi tabbipakkhehi ca akusalaṃ kusalañca nippariyāyato ārammaṇarasaṃ anubhavati, na tathā vipāko kammavegakkhittattā, nāpi kiriyā ahetukānaṃ atidubbalatāya, sahetukānañca khīṇakilesassa chaḷaṅgupekkhāvato uppajjamānānaṃ atisantavuttittā, tasmā rajjanādivasena ārammaṇarasānubhavanaṃ sātisayanti akusalaṃ kusalañca … bhūtāpagatanti vuttaṃ. (A-ṭ. I. 241.). stands ready,2 is called okāsakatuppanna – “okāso kato etenāti okāsakato.” By this, past kamma is meant. Likewise, because room has been made by that kamma, the result that is certainly destined to arise, though it has not yet arisen, is also called okāsakatuppanna – “okāso kato etassāti okāsakato.” By this, future result is meant.3
    -   **bhūmiladdhuppanna**: Unwholesome dhammas that, in the respective aggregates of clinging (upādānakkhandha) such as human and divine existence, in the respective continuum, have not been made into something whose nature is not to arise in the future – that is, have not been destroyed – and therefore should not be spoken of as “non-existent” (natthi)
        -   uppanna: applied to these undestroyed unwholesome dhammas because, since they have not been destroyed, they should not be spoken of as non-existent.4 By this are meant unwholesome dhammas whose nature is different from other types of uppannas such as vattamānuppanna and so forth.5 Accordingly, it should be understood that bhūmiladdha akusala is neither a present unwholesome dhamma that has reached the three moments, nor a past unwholesome dhamma that had reached the three moments and ceased, nor a future unwholesome dhamma that has not yet arisen

6.  bhūmiladdhuppanna / bhūmiladdhakilesa

    **bhūmiladdhuppanna / bhūmiladdhakilesa**: what is abandoned by the lokuttara magga
    
    -   **bhūmi**: apariññāta khandha (5 aggregates belonging to the three planes which are not yet fully comprehended), which are the objects of insight meditation
    -   **bhūmiladdha**: group of defilements (kilesajāta) fit to arise in those not yet-fully-comprehended mundane aggregates
        -   because that group of defilements has obtained that ground, that group of defilements is called bhūmiladdha
    
    -   notes
        -   strong okasakatakamma cannot be prevented , but some weaker ones can be postponed or avoided entirely acc. to some cases
        -   bhūmi of vipassanā meditation: different from here
            bhūmi for kilesas is relative: sotāpanna's khandhas are not bhūmi for diṭṭhi for instance
        -   objects do not cause citta to arise; paññatti do not cause cittas to arise
    
    -   **bhūmiladdha**: uneradicated subtle defilements that lie latent (anuseti) in the continuum
        -   not in the sense of **taking as object** (ārammaṇa vasena)
            -   if ārammaṇavasena, kilesas arise even with regard to the aggregates of Arahants
                -   if so, it would not be possible to remove them
        -   in the sense of **obtaining a base** (vatthu): obtained aggregates as a base, place for their own arising
    
    **vatthu bhāva for kilesas**:
    
    -   personal nature: only one's own ajjhatta khandhas are the vatthu for one's own kilesas
    -   vatthu bhāva for kilesa: being apariññāta
    
    -   from the arising of apariññāta khandhas, vaṭṭa mūlas lie latent in them
        -   called bhūmiladdha in the sense of being in an uneradicated state
        -   lying latent: unabandoned state
        -   anusaya kilesas are bhūmiladdha since they have obtained khandhas that serve as the base for their arising
            -   (*:q: 2 reasons for being called bhūmiladdha: obtaining khandhas as vatthu and being uneradicated?*): 2 char. of bhumiladdha
    
    -   not all the bhūmis (khandhas) are the basis for all defilements
        -   acc. to **puggala**
            -   only one's own apariññāta ajjhatta khandhas are vatthu for one's own bhūmiladdhakilesas
        -   acc to **time**
            -   for bhūmiladdhakilesas based on past aggregates, only such past aggregates are their vatthu, not future or present aggregates
            -   similarly for future and present kilesas
                -   bhumiladdha not in any time (closest to future) because it has to arise to do so
                -   anāgatavisesa / anāgatasāmañña: AN-ṭ: called anagata but not real; anything that could arise; if really anagata, it could be past and present as well; so not really anagata
                    -   includes khandhas, kilesas, jāti jarā maraṇaṁ that can happen in the future
                        -   what is eradicated is jātivisesa, etc.
                    -   anusayas are present in the khandhas latently, but jāti etc not
                        -   so 2 layers of anāgatavisesa
                            -   anusaya: only akusalas; should not be called past future present; some say anāgata, but better anāgatavisesa
                                -   they affect present khandhas, not the others below
                            -   could arise: khandhas, kusala akusala kammas, etc
        -   acc. to **plane**
            -   for bhūmiladdhakilesas based on kāmabhūmi aggregates, only such khandhas are their vatthu
            -   similarly for rūpa- and arūpa bhūmi khandhas
    
    -   question
        -   if the respective aggregates are the basis only for the respective defilements pertaining to those aggregates, do bhūmiladdha defilements lie latent in aggregates by separately and distinctly taking the aggregates as their ground, just as defilements in general take aggregates separately and distinctly as their object? Latent defilements do not abide (lie) in aggregates by separately and distinctly taking the aggregates as their ground
    
    In whatever specifically distinct bases (vatthu) – such as past, pertaining to 
    the sensual-sphere, and so on – there is an un-abandoned state of certain defilements, 
    that is like the defilements are existing in them as if they have specifically and 
    distinctly separated (chosen) them. Therefore, it was said above that for defilements 
    lying latent in an un-abandoned state in past aggregates, only past aggregates are their 
    basis, and so forth. 
    
    Accordingly, because defilements become bhūmiladdha through obtaining a basis 
    (aggregates), the distinction among defilements is shown according to the distinction 
    among those bases (aggregates). 
    
    The famous phrase, “Aññataro lolajātiko taṃ rasapathaviṃ aṅguliyā sāyi” – “A 
    certain greedy-natured being tasted that savoury earth with his finger” is stated in the 
    Aggañña Sutta in this way because there is such a differentiated classification. 
    According to that, craving for taste and so on, which had lain latent in the sensual
    sphere aggregates, having passed beyond fine-material and immaterial aggregates, 
    again took sensual-sphere aggregates as their basis. 
    
    veditabbaṃ. Yattha yattha hi vipassanāya apariññātā khandhā uppajjanti, tattha tattha 
    uppādato pabhuti tesu vaṭṭamūlaṃ kilesajātaṃ anuseti. Taṃ appahīnaṭṭhena bhūmiladdhanti 
    veditabbaṃ. (Psm-a. I. 158-9.). 
    
    Likewise, it should be understood that defilements lying latent in fine-material and 
    immaterial aggregates, having passed beyond sensual-sphere aggregates, again 
    persist taking fine-material and immaterial aggregates as their basis. 
    
    The un-abandoned state of defilements, that is, their lying latent, is inferred from 
    pariyuṭṭhāna, called “overpowering” (abhibhavana). In some places overpowering is 
    also described as “vitikkama.”7 This is because, in the absence of latent tendencies, 
    there is no manifestation (pariyuṭṭhāna) or transgression (vitikkama). This should be 
    understood according to the distinction of the stages of defilements. That will be 
    explained later. 
    
    in khandhas of ariyas, certain groups of defilements that are the respective roots of the round (vaṭṭa mūla) are abandoned by the respective paths. Those aggregates of that noble person, since they are no longer the basis for those respective roots of the round that have been abandoned, do not receive the name bhūmi.
    
    puthujjana: because of vaṭṭamūla, kamma is kusala or akusala

    -   the latent tendencies lie in all 5 khandhas
    
    This should be understood by the analogy of the way the nutrients of earth and so on 
    spreads throughout a tree. Just as a great tree stands upon the earth and, depending 
    upon the nutrients of earth (paṭhavi rasa) and the nutrient of water (āpo rasa), and 
    due to them, develops roots, trunk, branches, leaves, flowers, and fruits, covers the 
    sky, and continues its lineage uninterrupted through a succession of seeds until the 
    end of the aeon, one should not say that the taste of earth and so on is only in the roots 
    and not in the trunk and so on, or only in the fruits and not in the roots and so on. 
    Why is that? Because, without distinction, the taste of earth and so on persists 
    throughout all the roots and the rest, pervading them.
    
    Suppose some man, dispassionate with the flowers, fruits, and so on of that tree, 
    plants poisonous thorns called maṇḍūka kaṇṭaka in the four sides. Thereafter, that 
    tree, having been touched by that poison, comes to a state in which, because the 
    nutrients of earth and the nutrients of water have been exhausted, it can no longer 
    ripen and is incapable of producing a continuum (another tree).
    
    Likewise, a clansman dispassionate with the process of the aggregates begins in his own continuum the fourfold meditation (practice), just as that man applied poison in the four sides. Thereafter, that continuum of aggregates, having been touched by the poison of the four supramundane paths, because the root-defilements of the round have been completely 
    exhausted and ended, becomes a continuum having only the nature of functional activity, with all kinds of bodily kamma and so on reduced to mere kiriyā-nature, and becomes incapable of producing a further continuum in another existence by bringing about a new rebirth. With the cessation of the mere “final consciousness” (carimaka viññāṇa), the continuum of aggregates becomes without clinging and is completely extinguished, like a fire without fuel.

    in this way, the diversity of bhūmi and bhūmiladdha should be understood.

9.  3 stages of kilesa

    1.  vītikkamāvatthā (transgression)
    
        -   unwholesome states arise associated with impulsion consciousness (javana citta) when bodily and verbal misconduct (kāya and vacī duccarita) and wrong livelihood (micchā ājīva) are carried out. This is the gross stage of defilements (kilesa) and is also referred to as “gross defilement” (oḷārika kilesa). Its abandonment occurs through suppression by substitution (tadaṅga pahāna).2 In some contexts, the stage of transgressionis also referred to as the “stage of overpowering” (abhibhavana avatthā) of defilements
    
    2.  pariyuṭṭhānāvatthā (obsession/manifestation)
    
        -   This is the stage in which unwholesome states arise associated with unwholesome impulsion consciousness. This is the intermediate stage of defilements, also called “medium defilement” (majjhima kilesa).3 Its abandonment occurs through suppression (vikkhambhana pahāna). From the standpoint of literal meaning, pariyuṭṭhāna refers to the arising of defilements by surrounding and overpowering the continuum of wholesome states of the mind.4 Both the stages of transgression and obsession refer to unwholesome states arising associated with impulsion consciousness. However, they are distinct stages. For example, bodily and verbal misconduct and wrong livelihood do not have a stage of obsession; they have only the stages of transgression and latency. As stated,
        -   **Duccharita-durājīvānaṃ pana ṭhapetvā vītikkamāvatthaṃ, anusaya āvatthañca, vītikkamāvatthāya visuṃ pariyuṭṭhānāvatthā na upalabbhati**.5
        -   If misconduct and wrong livelihood had a stage of obsession, then their obsessive stage would need to be removed by rūpāvacara and arūpāvacara consciousness. However, these higher wholesome states oppose only the stage of obsession of defilements, not the stage of transgression. Therefore, rūpāvacara and arūpāvacara wholesome consciousness do not purify the stage of transgression of misconduct. Consequently, abstinence (virati) does not arise in sublime consciousnesses. This is the interpretation given in the Abhidhammāvatāra Purāṇa Ṭīkā.6 By undertaking moral discipline (sīla), the abandonment of transgression (vītikkama pahāna) occurs. While one is established in moral discipline, defilements that remain in the continuum in a latent form (anusaya) may arise when conditions are present, but they do not gain the opportunity to manifest as transgression through violation of sīla.7 Therefore, even though defilements are restrained at the level of transgression, it is not possible to prevent them from reaching the stage of obsession. What is achieved is only the prevention of their progression from obsession to transgression. This interpretation is found in the Sāratthadīpanī. In some contexts, the stage of obsession itself is referred to as the “stage of overpowering” (abhibhavanāvatthā) of defilements. These two stages (transgression and obsession) may also collectively be referred to as the stage of obsession (pariyuṭṭhānavatthā).
    
    3.  anusayāvatthā (latent)
    
        unwholesome states persist in the mental continuum without having been eradicated and without reaching the stage of obsession. The mere non-eradicated state of unwholesome states is termed “latent tendency” (anusaya). This is the subtle stage of defilements, also called “subtle defilement” (sukhuma kilesa).8 **Appahīnaṭṭhena santāne kāmarāgapaṭighānusayā**. 9
        
        -   Its abandonment occurs through eradication (samuccheda pahāna) Latent defilements, when conditions arise, progress to the stage of obsession and then to transgression. For example, a snake lies dormant in its hole. When a man strikes it, the snake rises, spreads its hood, and hisses. Sometimes it goes further and bites the man. Similarly, when wholesome or indetermined consciousness is present, defilements remain in the latent stage. When an object impinges on a sense door and unwise attention (ayonisomanasikāra) arises, the latent defilement gains a condition to arise. It then associates with impulsion consciousness and reaches the stage of obsession, producing intentions such as stealing. In some cases, it progresses further to the stage of transgression, such as actually stealing. The wise, even when defilements reach the stage of obsession, generate wise attention (yonisomanasikāra) and suppress them, preventing their progression to transgression.

                -   "are you not one who aspires to attain omniscience by fulfilling all perfections? What is this lapse (stumbling and falling) in your virtue? This is like one who wishes to cross the great ocean but drowns in water barely enough to wet a cow’s hoof"
            -   he resolved: “Even if that king or anyone else were to cut that ascetic woman into pieces with a sharp weapon, I will not break my perfection of virtue

            reach the stage of obsession. 
            If the stages of latency and obsession are considered separately, it must be stated that 
            the stage of obsession arises dependent upon the latent stage. Without latent 
            defilements, there is no stage of obsession11 – that is, defilements do not arise 
            associated with impulsion consciousness. Therefore, by abandoning latent 
            tendencies, the stage of obsession is also abandoned. 
            As stated in the Nettippakaraṇa, referring to the Ajita Māṇava Pucchā in the Sutta 
            Nipāta: “Paññāyete pidhiyyare.”  
            Paññāya anusayā pahīyanti, anusayesu pahīnesu pariyuṭṭhānā pahīyanti. Kissa, 
            anusayassa 
            pahīnattā? 
            Taṃ 
            yathā 
            khandhavantassa 
            rukkhassa 
            anavasesamūluddharaṇe kate pupphaphalapallavaṅkurasantati samucchinnā 
            bhavati. Evaṃ anusayesu pahīnesu pariyuṭṭhānasantati samucchinnā bhavati 
            pidahitā paṭicchannā. Kena? Paññāya.12 
            By wisdom, the latent tendencies are abandoned; when latent tendencies are 
            abandoned, the obsessive defilements are abandoned. Just as when a tree with a 
            trunk is completely uprooted, its flowers, fruits, shoots, and sprouts are destroyed,

            vaḍḍhituṃ na adāsinti. Tenevāha "yadi naṃ brāhmaṇi"ntiādi. 
            Tassattho – taṃ paribbājikaṃ brāhmaṇiṃ so rājā vā añño vā koci tiṇhāyapi nisitāya sattiyā

            11 Anusayappahānaṃ idha pidhānaṃ adhippetanti āha – "paññāya anusayā pahīyantī"ti. 
            Yasmā anusayanimittaṃ pariyuṭṭhānaṃ anusayābhāve na hotīti āha "anusayesū"tiādi. Idāni 
            tamevatthaṃ upamāya vibhāvento "taṃ yathā khandhavantassā"tiādimāha. (Nett-a. 67.). 
            12 Nett. 14-5.  
            4 
            so too when latent tendencies are eradicated, the continuity of obsessive defilements 
            is destroyed and cut off by wisdom. 
            Thus, when latent (unabandoned) defilements are eradicated by wisdom, unarisen 
            obsessive defilements do not arise. This itself is the abandonment of obsession 
            (pariyuṭṭhāna pahāna). 
            Here, defilements are analyzed into stages, with one stage serving as the condition 
            for another. Just as the presence of obsession allows inference of latent defilements, 
            so too the non-arising of obsessive defilements indicates the eradication of latent 
            tendencies. 
            However, not every uneradicated defilement should be termed anusaya. Only those 
            that have reached a state of strength (thāmagata) are called latent tendencies. 
            Accordingly, the uneradicated state of defilements should be divided into: 
            (1). The strengthened state (thāmagata), and 
            (2). The non-strengthened state (athāmagata) 
            Finally, it should be understood that anusaya refers specifically to the seven types of 
            defilements such as sensual desire (kāmarāga) and aversion (paṭigha). Mental factors 
            such as shamelessness (ahirika), moral recklessness (anottappa), and restlessness 
            (uddhacca) are not classified as latent tendencies.
